### Day 34 — 29 June 2026 (Monday)

**Progress:**
Today's work on HomeSewa focused entirely on the Super Admin feature set — three new restricted-access admin screens, a secure login enforcement fix, and customer block enforcement. The HR team had specified three capabilities exclusive to the super admin role: User Management (enable/disable professionals, block/unblock customers), Professional Verification (approve or reject pending professional registrations), and Admin Notifications (send targeted push notifications to different audience segments). All three were designed, built, wired into navigation, and secured against access by regular professional admins.

**New professionals start in Pending state — `PostApiCareer.ts`:**
The career form submission was creating new professional records with `status: 'Active'` in both the `workforce` table and the `admins` table. This meant a newly registered professional could log in immediately — before a super admin had reviewed and approved them. Changed both status values to `'Pending'` in `PostApiCareer.ts`. New professionals now sit in Pending state until a super admin explicitly approves them via the Professional Verification screen.

**User Management screen — `UserManagement.tsx`:**
Built a new Super Admin-only screen with two tabs:

- **Professionals tab** — Fetches all records from the `workforce` table. Each card shows the professional's name, phone, positions, preferred city, and a colour-coded status pill (green for Active, amber for Pending, red for Inactive). A Disable / Enable toggle button fires a confirmation `Alert` before updating `workforce.status`. A live search bar filters by name or phone.
- **Customers tab** — Fetches unique customer phones from the `booking` table, cross-referenced against the `blocked_customers` table to mark which are blocked. Each card shows name, phone, and a Block / Unblock toggle button.

The screen includes a super admin guard: on mount it reads `adminTable` from AsyncStorage — if the value is not `'admins'`, an "Access Denied" alert fires and the user is immediately routed back.

**Professional Verification screen — `ProfessionalVerification.tsx`:**
Built a new Super Admin-only screen that fetches all `workforce` records where `status = 'Pending'`, ordered by `application_date` descending (newest first). Each card shows the professional's headshot (or an initial placeholder), full name, phone, positions, preferred city, working areas (as chip tags), years of experience, and application date. Two action buttons are displayed per card:

- **Approve** — Updates `workforce.status` and `admins.status` to `'Active'` for the professional's phone. Shows a success alert, then removes the card from the list immediately without a full refetch.
- **Reject** — Updates `workforce.status` to `'Rejected'` and removes the card from the list. A pending count badge in the screen header shows the number of applications awaiting review.

**Admin Notifications screen — `AdminNotifications.tsx`:**
Built a Super Admin-only notification sender with three tabs, each targeting a different audience:

- **All Users** — Title + Message fields. Calls `notifyAll()` to broadcast to every subscribed device.
- **Professionals** — Service Type + City + Message fields. Calls `notifyProfessionalsInCity(service, city)` to send to professionals matching that service in that city.
- **Customers** — Title + Message fields. Calls the `notifyCustomers()` function added to `api/notifications.ts`, which targets OneSignal devices tagged with `role = 'user'`.

Each tab shows an information banner explaining exactly who will receive the message. A confirm `Alert` previews the full notification content before sending. The send button shows an `ActivityIndicator` during the async call and is disabled to prevent duplicate sends.

**Navigation registration — `admin/_layout.tsx`:**
Added `<Stack.Screen>` entries for all three new screens (`UserManagement`, `ProfessionalVerification`, `AdminNotifications`) in the admin Stack navigator so Expo Router can resolve navigation to them.

**Super Admin drawer section — `CustomDrawer.tsx`:**
Added a dedicated "Super Admin" section in the sidebar drawer, visible only when `adminTable === 'admins'`. The section appears below a divider with a small section label and contains three menu items: User Management, Verification, and Notifications — each with a distinct Ionicons icon. Professional admins (`adminTable === 'workforce'`) never see this section.

**Login block for disabled professionals — `AdminLogin.tsx`:**
Identified a critical security gap: when a professional's `workforce.status` was set to `'Inactive'` via User Management, their `admins` table row remained `status: 'Active'`. The login screen checked `admins` first — so the professional could still pass the `isAdmin` check and log in with super admin access.

Two fixes were applied:
1. Added `admin.role !== 'professional'` to the `isAdmin` condition — true super admins have a non-professional role, so professionals in the `admins` table are excluded from the admin login path regardless of their status.
2. The `admins` select query was updated to include the `role` field.

Additionally, a specific error message was added: if the phone+PIN combination matches a record but the account is inactive, the user sees "Your account has been disabled by the admin. Please contact support." instead of the generic "Invalid phone or PIN."

**Sync `admins` table on toggle — `UserManagement.tsx`:**
The `toggleProfessional` function previously only updated `workforce.status`. Even with the login fix above, leaving the `admins` row out of sync was inconsistent. Updated `toggleProfessional` to also call `supabase.from('admins').update({ status: newStatus }).eq('phone', phone)` immediately after the workforce update. Both tables now stay in sync, so the block takes effect through both enforcement layers simultaneously.

**Customer block enforcement — `BookingOtp.tsx`:**
Blocking a customer in User Management wrote their phone to the `blocked_customers` table but nothing ever checked that table — blocked customers could continue submitting bookings. Added a Supabase query to `BookingOtp.tsx` immediately before `createBooking()`: if the customer's phone is found in `blocked_customers`, the booking is stopped and they see "Your account has been restricted. Please contact HomeSewa support." No booking record is created. The `supabase` client was also imported into `BookingOtp.tsx`, where it had been missing.

**Learning:**
The disabled-professional login bypass was a textbook example of an incomplete security fix — updating one table while leaving a parallel table in an inconsistent state. The correct mental model for any access control check is to think about every path through the system: in this case, both the `workforce` table (checked as `isWorker`) and the `admins` table (checked as `isAdmin`) could independently grant login access. Disabling only one path left the other open. The `role !== 'professional'` guard ensures the `admins` table login path is permanently restricted to true super admins by design, not just by status — meaning even a future data error that accidentally sets a professional's `admins.status` back to Active cannot grant them super admin access.

The customer block fix reinforced the principle that a database write is meaningless unless there is a corresponding read that enforces it. Adding a row to `blocked_customers` without checking it anywhere in the booking flow was data being written for no effect. Every enforcement table must have a corresponding check at the correct enforcement point — in this case, the OTP confirmation step, which is the last gate before a booking is committed.

**Skills Gained:**
React Native — multi-tab admin screens with live search, FlatList with status-coded cards, confirmation Alert before destructive actions, super admin guard with AsyncStorage check · Supabase — cross-table updates for status sync, `maybeSingle()` for optional row checks, `role` field gating in login queries · Security — dual-path login bypass analysis, role-based access control with `role !== 'professional'`, enforcement table read/write pairing · Push notifications — OneSignal tab-based audience targeting (all users / by service+city / customers only), `notifyCustomers` with role tag filter · Expo Router — `Stack.Screen` registration for new admin screens, drawer section visibility gating by `adminTable`

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Disabled professionals could still log in as super admins. Root cause: `admins` table row remained `status: 'Active'` after `workforce.status` was set to `'Inactive'`, and `isAdmin` check did not exclude professionals by role. Fix: added `role !== 'professional'` to `isAdmin` condition AND synced `admins.status` when toggling a professional in User Management. (2) Blocked customers could still submit bookings. Root cause: `blocked_customers` table was written to but never queried anywhere in the booking flow. Fix: added a `blocked_customers` lookup in `BookingOtp.tsx` immediately before `createBooking()` — blocks the submission if a match is found.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's work was primarily about closing gaps between intent and enforcement — three cases where the data was being written correctly but the application was not actually using it to enforce the intended behaviour. The professional disable, the customer block, and the login role check all fell into this category. I am developing a habit of asking "where does this data get read back and enforced?" whenever I write a new record or status to the database, rather than assuming that writing it is sufficient. Security and access control features are only real if every path through the system respects them.

### Day 35 — 30 June 2026 (Tuesday)

**Progress:**
Today's work on HomeSewa covered six areas across the super admin experience: drawer navigation cleanup and layout refinement, Update Profile field visibility for super admin, User Management simplification, a Khalti payment flow revert, and a full rebuild of the Admin Notifications screen with multi-select dropdowns for service type and city.

**Super Admin drawer cleanup — `CustomDrawer.tsx`:**
The sidebar drawer was showing items that are irrelevant to a super admin — Services, About Us, Contact, and FAQs are customer-facing pages that a super admin has no reason to visit. Wrapped all four items in `{adminTable !== 'admins' && (...)}` so they are hidden when the logged-in user is a super admin. The Help Box menu item was also repositioned: it now appears directly below Booking History for super admin, rather than after the divider where the customer-facing items were. The divider itself (`<View style={styles.divider} />`) was made conditional — it only renders when `adminTable !== 'admins'`, eliminating the gap that appeared between Booking History and Help Box when the items above the divider were all hidden. A second conditional was applied to the divider above Change PIN — it now only renders for non-logged-in users, so the Change PIN item appears flush with the section above it for both super admin and professionals. The resulting super admin drawer order is: Home → Booking History → Help Box → (Super Admin section: User Management, Verification, Notifications) → Change PIN.

**Drawer item height increase — `CustomDrawer.tsx`:**
Compact mode items (used for super admin section items) had `paddingVertical: 3` and `iconBoxCompact` at 32×32px — noticeably smaller than the regular items. With the customer-facing items removed, the super admin drawer now has fewer total items and the compact sizing made each item look undersized relative to the available height. Increased `itemCompact.paddingVertical` from 3 to 9 (matching regular items), `iconBoxCompact` from 32×32 to 38×38, `labelCompact.fontSize` from 13.5 to 14.5, and compact icon size from 18 to 20. The `sectionLabel.paddingVertical` was increased from 4 to 6. The menu container already uses `justifyContent: 'space-evenly'`, so the larger items now distribute naturally to fill the drawer height without excessive gaps.

**Update Profile — super admin field visibility — `UpdateProfile.tsx`:**
The Update Profile screen was rendering Email, Gender, Your Expertise, Years of Experience, Preferred City, and Working Area fields for all users including super admin. The `admins` table has none of these columns — only `full_name` is editable for a super admin. Two changes were made: the Email and Gender fields were wrapped in `{adminTable !== 'admins' && (...)}` to hide them for super admin, and the already-present professional fields block (Expertise, Experience, City, Area) was confirmed as correctly wrapped under the same condition. The save handler uses an explicit `if (adminTable === 'admins') { update full_name only } else { update all workforce fields }` branching to ensure the `admins` table never receives workforce-only columns such as `gender` or `positions`. The phone query on load was also normalised with `.replace(/\D/g, '').slice(-10)` to ensure a match regardless of how the phone was stored in AsyncStorage.

**User Management — delete functionality removed — `UserManagement.tsx`:**
The user management cards previously showed both a Disable/Enable toggle and a trash/delete icon button. The HR team confirmed that permanently deleting professional or customer records is not a desired action — disable is sufficient. The `deleteProfessional` and `deleteCustomer` functions were removed entirely, along with the trash icon `TouchableOpacity` from the `renderItem` body and the `deleteBtn` style from the StyleSheet. Each card now shows only the Disable/Enable (or Block/Unblock for customers) toggle button.

**Khalti payment flow reverted — `WorkCompletionOTP.tsx`:**
The work completion OTP screen was navigating to `PaymentMethod.tsx` after a successful OTP verification, which then presented a Khalti vs Cash payment choice. The HR team decided to remove this Khalti payment step for now — the Khalti merchant account is not yet activated and the feature was premature. Reverted the `handleVerify` success path from `router.replace({ pathname: '/admin/PaymentMethod', ... })` back to `setSuccess(true)`, which shows the already-existing green success screen ("Work Completed!" with the customer's name and a "Back to Bookings" button) directly after OTP verification. `PaymentMethod.tsx` and `KhaltiPayment.tsx` remain in the codebase but are no longer reachable from the booking flow.

**Admin Notifications — multi-select dropdowns — `AdminNotifications.tsx` and `api/notifications.ts`:**
The Professionals notification tab previously used two plain `TextInput` fields for Service Type and City — requiring the admin to type values manually and risk mismatches against database values. Replaced both with a custom `MultiSelectDropdown` component built inline in the screen. The component renders a styled button showing the selected count, opens a bottom-sheet `Modal` with a `FlatList` of checkbox rows when tapped, and provides "Clear All" and "Confirm (N)" buttons in the modal footer. Confirmed selections appear as removable chip tags below the dropdown button.

The service options list contains all 40 HomeSewa services sourced from `src/data/ServiceData.ts`: Deep Cleaning, Garden Care, Masonry Repair, Plumbing Repair, Electrical Repair, Carpentry, Washing Machine Repair, EV Charger Installation, AC Services, Painting, Packing & Moving, AirBnB Maintenance, Bridal Makeup, RO Water Purifying, Refrigerator Repair, CCTV Services, Modular Kitchen, Home Renovation, Pest Control, Drywall Repair, Salon at Home, Tiling, Chef at Home, Home Automation, Parqueting, Indoor Planting, Spa at Home, Physiotherapy, Handyman, Massage Therapy, Water Tank Cleaning, Aluminum Fabrication, False Ceiling, Computer Repair, Geyser Repair, Chimney Repair, Tree Cutting & Pruning, Septic Tank Cleaning, Pet Grooming, Lift / Elevator Repair. The city options are limited to the three cities HomeSewa currently operates in: Kathmandu, Bhaktapur, and Lalitpur.

The `notifyProfessionalsInCity` function in `api/notifications.ts` was updated from `(service: string, city: string)` to `(service: string | string[], city: string | string[])`. Arrays are normalised at the start: `const services = (Array.isArray(service) ? service : [service]).map(s => s.trim())`. The Supabase query uses `.overlaps('positions', services)` — the PostgreSQL `&&` operator — to match professionals who offer any of the selected services, rather than requiring all of them. For a single city, `.ilike('preferred_city', city)` is used; for multiple cities, an `.or()` filter is built as a comma-separated `preferred_city.ilike.City` string for case-insensitive matching across all selected cities. The notification heading and fallback message body both use the full joined label (e.g., `"Plumbing Repair, Carpentry in Kathmandu, Bhaktapur"`).

**Learning:**
The `MultiSelectDropdown` implementation reinforced the value of composable UI components. By keeping the modal state, chip rendering, and confirmation logic self-contained inside the `MultiSelectDropdown` function, the parent screen only needs to hold `selectedServices` and `selectedCities` as state arrays — it has no awareness of how the selection UI works. This separation means the dropdown can be reused anywhere else in the admin interface without modification.

The Supabase `.overlaps()` operator was new. Understanding the distinction between `.contains()` (the positions array must contain all specified values) and `.overlaps()` (the positions array must share at least one value with the specified array) is critical for matching multi-service professionals correctly. `.contains()` would only return professionals who offer every selected service simultaneously — wrong for this use case. `.overlaps()` correctly returns any professional who offers at least one of the selected services.

The `justifyContent: 'space-evenly'` lesson from the drawer work is also worth noting: when a container has fewer items than its maximum capacity, `space-evenly` distributes the remaining space as gaps between items. Increasing the item height reduces the remaining space and therefore reduces the gap — the layout self-adjusts without requiring any hardcoded margin values. This makes the drawer layout robust to the varying number of items each user role sees.

**Skills Gained:**
React Native UI — bottom-sheet `Modal` with `FlatList` checkboxes, removable chip tags, multi-select state with temporary `temp` array and confirm/cancel pattern · Supabase — `.overlaps()` for array intersection queries, `.or()` with `ilike` for case-insensitive multi-value city filtering · React Native layout — `justifyContent: 'space-evenly'` with item height tuning, compact vs regular item size parity, conditional drawer section and divider rendering · UX — role-aware field visibility in profile forms, explicit `if/else` save branches per user role to prevent cross-table column writes

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Divider appearing between Booking History and Help Box when customer-facing items were hidden. Root cause: the divider was rendered unconditionally above the About/Contact/FAQ block; with those items hidden, the divider became the first thing below Booking History. Fix: made the divider conditional on `adminTable !== 'admins'` — it only renders for non-super-admin users. (2) `notifyProfessionalsInCity` receiving arrays but expecting strings after the UI change. Root cause: the old function signature only accepted `string`, so passing `selectedServices` (a `string[]`) would silently pass an array to a string parameter. Fix: updated the function signature to `string | string[]`, normalised both params to arrays at the top of the function, and updated the Supabase query accordingly. (3) Khalti merchant account "Invalid token" error blocking the payment flow. Decision: reverted `WorkCompletionOTP` to the original success-screen flow — Khalti integration will be re-enabled once the merchant account is activated by Khalti.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's session was primarily about refinement and correctness rather than new capabilities — closing gaps between what the UI presented and what the super admin role actually needs, removing an incomplete feature rather than leaving it broken, and improving the notification system with a proper multi-select interface that eliminates manual text entry errors. The drawer cleanup in particular had a compounding effect: each individual change (hiding irrelevant items, repositioning Help Box, removing dividers, increasing item sizes) was small, but together they produced a sidebar that feels genuinely tailored to the super admin workflow rather than a filtered version of the customer drawer. Small, coherent changes with a clear user role in mind consistently produce better outcomes than large, generic additions.

## Week 7 — 01 July to 07 July 2026 (Wednesday – Tuesday)

---

### Day 36 — 01 July 2026 (Wednesday)

**Progress:**
Today's session covered four areas: completing the project-wide keyboard overlap fix, assessing the Khalti payment integration status, investigating the Android notification icon issue, and resolving a multi-layered Google Play Store production build pipeline failure.

**Project-wide keyboard overlap fix — all remaining screens:**
Following up on the keyboard avoidance work started in the previous session, I completed the fix across every remaining screen in the HomeSewa project that contained `TextInput` fields. The screens fixed today were: `BookingOtp.tsx`, `PartnershipOTP.tsx`, `helpboxOTP.tsx`, `AdminCreate.tsx`, `UpdateProfile.tsx`, and `HelpBoxDetail.tsx`.

The pattern applied consistently across all screens was to replace the outermost `<View style={{ flex: 1 }}>` wrapper with `<KeyboardAvoidingView behavior="padding" style={{ flex: 1 }}>`. For screens that used `SubmitOverlay` (specifically `PartnershipOTP.tsx`), the overlay was kept inside the `KeyboardAvoidingView` — matching the pattern established earlier for `CareerOTP.tsx`. For scroll-based screens (`UpdateProfile.tsx` and `HelpBoxDetail.tsx`), the `KeyboardAvoidingView` was inserted as a direct wrapper around the `ScrollView` rather than the outermost container, so the fixed header components remain outside the keyboard-avoidance region. All six screens also had `KeyboardAvoidingView` and `Platform` added to their imports. The full fix now covers nine screens total across the project: `AdminNotifications`, `WorkCompletionOTP`, `CareerOTP`, `BookingOtp`, `PartnershipOTP`, `helpboxOTP`, `AdminCreate`, `UpdateProfile`, and `HelpBoxDetail`.

**Notification icon root cause analysis:**
Investigated the ongoing Android notification icon issue where notifications displayed a dark green circle with a white logo instead of the intended white-background green logo. Traced the issue to the build lifecycle: the `large_icon: 'homesewa'` field had been correctly added to every OneSignal notification payload in `api/notifications.ts`, and `largeIcons: ["./assets/images/homesewa.png"]` was correctly configured in the `onesignal-expo-plugin` block of `app.json`. However, drawable resources are compiled into the APK at build time — the currently installed app was built before the `largeIcons` configuration was added, so the `homesewa` drawable does not exist in the installed APK. When OneSignal cannot resolve the drawable name, it silently falls back to the app's adaptive icon, which Android renders as the foreground layer only (green logo without the white background). The fix requires a new EAS production build so the plugin copies `homesewa.png` into `android/app/src/main/res/drawable/` during the build process.

**Khalti payment integration assessment:**
Reviewed the existing Khalti integration codebase with the HR team. Confirmed that `api/khalti.ts` (with `initiateKhaltiPayment` and `lookupKhaltiPayment`), `KhaltiPayment.tsx` (QR screen with 5-second polling), and `PaymentMethod.tsx` (Khalti vs Cash selection) are all fully implemented. The live merchant API keys (`live_secret_key_0eb0379b74e64aff975580d33dbeccda`) were already present in `.env`. The current flow is production-ready pending Khalti merchant account activation. A security concern was noted: the `EXPO_PUBLIC_` prefix on the secret key means it is embedded in the APK bundle and extractable. Moving the `initiateKhaltiPayment` and `lookupKhaltiPayment` calls to a Supabase Edge Function is recommended before public release to prevent key exposure. Integration work was deferred — the feature will be re-enabled once the Khalti merchant account is formally activated.

**Google Play Store production build pipeline — version code and package name resolution:**
Attempted to upload the recently completed EAS production build AAB to Google Play Console, which was rejected with two separate errors: (1) "Version code 4 has already been used" and (2) package name mismatch between the uploaded AAB (`com.pracas.homesewa`) and the Play Store listing (`com.pracasinfosys.sriyog.com`).

For the version code issue: the root cause was `"autoIncrement": true` combined with `"appVersionSource": "remote"` in `eas.json` — EAS manages version codes on its own remote counter, ignoring any `versionCode` set in `app.json`. The EAS remote counter was at 4, far below the Play Store's minimum required value (previous production release was version 50). Used `eas build:version:set --platform android --profile production` to manually set the EAS remote counter to 51. Removed the `versionCode: 51` that had been added to `app.json` (it is ignored by EAS and created a false impression of version control).

For the package name issue: discovered that when an `android/` directory is present in the project, EAS ignores `android.package` in `app.json` entirely and reads `applicationId` from `android/app/build.gradle` directly. The `build.gradle` had `applicationId 'com.pracas.homesewa'` — changed to `applicationId 'com.pracasinfosys.sriyog.com'` to match the package name registered with Google Play Console. Updated `app.json` `package` and `bundleIdentifier` fields to match for consistency.

Resolved the keystore credential issue by providing the existing `@sriyog__homesewa.jks` keystore file with its password and alias — EAS accepted the credentials and registered the keystore. A new production EAS build was successfully queued (version code 55, package `com.pracasinfosys.sriyog.com`).

**Learning:**
Gained a thorough understanding of how EAS Build handles versioning in projects with a native `android/` directory. The key insight is that two separate version-control systems are in play simultaneously: `app.json` is Expo's metadata layer, while `android/app/build.gradle` is the actual Android build system. When both exist, EAS reads `applicationId` and `namespace` from `build.gradle` exclusively, while version codes are managed by EAS's own remote counter when `appVersionSource: "remote"` is set. Understanding this distinction prevents the confusion of making changes to `app.json` and assuming they will affect the build output.

Also reinforced the Android signing architecture: the `applicationId` in `build.gradle` is the permanent identifier for a Play Store listing — it cannot be changed after the first release. Any change to `applicationId` after publication creates a completely new app as far as the Play Store is concerned, breaking the update chain for all existing users. This is why the `build.gradle` value had to be reverted to `com.pracasinfosys.sriyog.com` rather than accepting the rebrand to `com.pracas.homesewa`.

**Skills Gained:**
React Native UI — `KeyboardAvoidingView` with `behavior="padding"` applied across nine screens, scroll-based vs fixed-layout wrapping strategies · EAS Build — `appVersionSource: "remote"` and `autoIncrement` interaction, `eas build:version:set` for manual remote counter override, keystore credential registration via `eas credentials` · Android build system — `applicationId` vs `namespace` in `build.gradle`, EAS native directory detection overriding `app.json` · Google Play Console — version code conflict diagnosis, package name mismatch resolution · Security — `EXPO_PUBLIC_` key exposure risk, Supabase Edge Function as backend proxy for payment API secret keys

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) EAS auto-incremented version code (4 → 5) instead of using the `versionCode: 51` set in `app.json`. Root cause: `appVersionSource: "remote"` in `eas.json` causes EAS to manage version codes remotely and ignore `app.json`. Fix: ran `eas build:version:set` to set the remote counter to 51, then removed the `versionCode` from `app.json`. (2) Package name in the built AAB was `com.pracas.homesewa` despite `app.json` showing `com.pracasinfosys.sriyog.com`. Root cause: EAS reads `applicationId` from `android/app/build.gradle` when a native directory is present, ignoring `app.json`. Fix: updated `applicationId` in `build.gradle` to `com.pracasinfosys.sriyog.com` to match the Play Store registration. (3) Keystore not recognized by EAS remote credential storage. Fix: provided the local `@sriyog__homesewa.jks` file with its password and alias; EAS accepted and stored it for future builds.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's Play Store pipeline work was a reminder that production release engineering involves several parallel systems — EAS's remote versioning, Android's native build configuration, Google Play's package identity enforcement, and keystore credential management — each with its own source of truth that can silently diverge from the others. The `app.json` changes that appeared to fix the version code and package name had no actual effect on the build because EAS was reading from different sources entirely. Learning to identify which system is actually authoritative for each configuration value is a critical skill for any developer working on a live production application.

### Day 37 — 02 July 2026 (Thursday)

**Progress:**
Today's session focused entirely on resolving a persistent EAS Build failure that had blocked the HomeSewa app from reaching the Google Play Store, as well as completing two Play Store submission tasks: resolving an advertising ID policy issue and writing the first-release changelog in two languages.

**EAS Build — root cause of persistent `processReleaseGoogleServices` failure:**
The build had been failing repeatedly with `No matching client found for package name 'com.pracasinfosys.sriyog.com'` despite multiple attempts to fix the `google-services.json` content. The error comes from Firebase's Gradle plugin, which reads `android/app/google-services.json` and checks that the `package_name` in that file matches the `applicationId` in `build.gradle`.

After exhaustive log analysis, the root cause chain was identified:

1. **Both `google-services.json` files were listed in `.gitignore`** — the lines `google-services.json` and `android/app/google-services.json` had been added to `.gitignore` as a security measure early in the project. This meant EAS's upload archive never included these files, so the build server never received any updates to them.

2. **EAS credential injection was overwriting the file** — `app.json` had `"googleServicesFile": "./google-services.json"` in the `android` section. When EAS first processed this field, it uploaded the file as a stored credential on its servers. On every subsequent build, EAS injected the old stored version during the "Prepare credentials" step — overwriting the file even after we had un-gitignored it and committed an updated version to the repository.

3. **The old stored credential contained `com.pracas.homesewa`** — the first `google-services.json` had been created when the package name was still `com.pracas.homesewa`. EAS had been injecting this stale version on every build, which is why the Gradle plugin consistently reported that `com.pracasinfosys.sriyog.com` was not found.

**Fixes applied (in order):**

1. Removed `google-services.json` and `android/app/google-services.json` from `.gitignore` so the files are included in the EAS upload archive.

2. Removed `"googleServicesFile": "./google-services.json"` from the `android` section of `app.json` to prevent EAS from injecting its stored credential.

3. Updated both `google-services.json` files (root and `android/app/`) to contain only the `com.pracasinfosys.sriyog.com` client entry — the `com.pracas.homesewa` entry was removed entirely.

4. Added a `fixGoogleServicesJson` Gradle task to `android/app/build.gradle` as a definitive failsafe. The task uses `doFirst` to force-write the correct JSON content directly to `${projectDir}/google-services.json` at Gradle execution time, and `tasks.whenTaskAdded` hooks it as a dependency of `processReleaseGoogleServices`. This approach cannot be bypassed by EAS credential injection because it runs at the Android build layer, after all file injection from EAS has already occurred.

All four changes were committed and pushed to GitHub, and a new EAS production build was triggered.

**Play Store — Incomplete advertising ID declaration:**
The Google Play Console showed a policy warning: "Incomplete advertising ID declaration." This is required for apps targeting Android 13+ that use the Advertising ID via any Firebase SDK. The fix was to click "Update declaration" in Play Console → confirm the app uses Advertising ID → check the Analytics use case → Save. The warning is resolved without requiring any code changes.

**Play Store — First-release changelog (en-US and ne-NP):**
Wrote the release notes for the first public release of HomeSewa in two languages required by Google Play:

*English (en-US):*
> HomeSewa connects you with trusted home service professionals across Nepal. Book services like cleaning, plumbing, electrical repair, salon at home, and 36 more — right from your phone. Track your bookings, get OTP-verified completion, and pay your way. Serving Kathmandu, Bhaktapur, and Lalitpur.


**Learning:**
The most important lesson from today is understanding how EAS's credential injection system works and how it interacts with `.gitignore`. When a file is gitignored, EAS has no way to receive updated content from the repository — the only path into the build is either EAS's own stored credential system or a post-install script. This creates a hidden dependency: once EAS stores a credential, it will silently inject its stored version on every subsequent build regardless of what you commit to git. Removing the `googleServicesFile` reference from `app.json` severs this injection path and returns full control to the repository.

The `fixGoogleServicesJson` Gradle task is a useful pattern beyond just this project: any build-time file that could be overwritten by external tooling can be protected with a `doFirst` task that asserts the correct content right before the task that depends on that file executes.

**Skills Gained:**
EAS Build — credential injection lifecycle, `googleServicesFile` in `app.json` as the trigger for EAS credential storage, `.gitignore` interaction with EAS upload archive · Gradle — `doFirst` block for file overwrite, `tasks.whenTaskAdded` hook for injecting task dependencies at configuration time · Firebase — `processReleaseGoogleServices` task requirements, `package_name` to `applicationId` matching · Google Play Console — advertising ID policy declaration, release notes localisation for en-US and ne-NP

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) `processReleaseGoogleServices` kept failing across five or more builds despite updating `google-services.json`. Root cause: `.gitignore` excluded the file from EAS archive AND `app.json`'s `googleServicesFile` caused EAS to inject its old stored version with the wrong package name. Fix: removed file from `.gitignore`, removed `googleServicesFile` from `app.json`, and added a `fixGoogleServicesJson` Gradle task as a definitive build-layer override. (2) EAS credential injection was not visible in the build logs — the "Prepare credentials" step ran silently before Gradle, making it appear that the correct file had been committed when it had actually been overwritten. Fix: checked the `processReleaseGoogleServices` Gradle task output directly, which showed which `package_name` values were actually found in the JSON at build time — these did not match what was in the committed file, confirming injection was occurring. (3) Play Store advertising ID warning with no clear action path. Fix: used "Update declaration" in Play Console policy settings, confirmed Firebase Analytics as the use case — no code changes required.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's work was a lesson in diagnosing a problem that had multiple interacting root causes, each of which masked the others. Fixing only the `.gitignore` would not have resolved the EAS credential injection; fixing only the credential injection would not have helped if the file was still gitignored. And without the Gradle task as a final failsafe, any future developer who re-adds `googleServicesFile` to `app.json` could reintroduce the problem silently. The layered fix — gitignore, app.json, and Gradle task together — is more robust than any single change would be. On the Play Store side, writing the release notes in both English and Nepali was a reminder that localization is not just about the app UI but also about the product's presence in the store, which is often the first thing a potential user sees. A Nepali description in the user's native language signals that the product is genuinely built for them.

---

### Day 38 — 05 July 2026 (Sunday)

**Progress:**
Today's session was the largest single-day undertaking so far, covering a full schema-drift audit across the professional/admin data layer, implementation of a new HR-mandated role and booking-approval policy, a project-wide UI overlap sweep, and a structural database change separating professionals from admin accounts entirely.

**Full schema-drift audit — seven files silently broken:**
Started by re-verifying every Supabase query against the live database rather than trusting the column names the code was written against. Confirmed the `workforce` table had been migrated at some point to a different structure than several screens assumed: real columns are `first_name`/`middle_name`/`last_name`, `profile_status`, `services`, `working_areas`, `headshot_url`, `government_issued_id_url`, `created_date`, `years_experience`, `emergency_contact`, `referred_by`, `issues` — not the `full_name`/`status`/`positions`/`preferred_city`/`pin` names several features had been built against. This meant `PostApiCareer.ts` (professional signup), three functions in `api/notifications.ts` (`pushAreaProfessionals`, `notifyProfessionalsAccepted`, `notifyProfessionalsInCity`), `UpdateProfile.tsx`, `AdminChangePassword.tsx`, `PaymentMethod.tsx`, and `BookingDetails_1.tsx` were all either failing outright or silently returning no data. Rewrote all seven to use the real column names, added a `splitFullName` helper to convert a single "Full Name" field into `first_name`/`middle_name`/`last_name` on write, and used `.or('phone.eq.X,phone.eq.977X')` throughout to match both the legacy 977-prefixed phone format and the current 10-digit format when looking up a professional by phone.

**HR-directed Access Roles and booking-approval workflow:**
Received a role/permission matrix from HR: Super Admin, Admin, Professional, Customer, and Public, each with a defined set of allowed actions, plus a new rule that a booking must be confirmed by a booking manager before professionals are notified about it. Implemented:
- A new **Admin** role, distinct from Super Admin, added to the drawer/session logic via a new `adminRole` value in `AsyncStorage` (previously only `adminTable` — `'admins'` vs `'workforce'` — was tracked, which could not distinguish Super Admin from Admin).
- An **Admins tab** in `UserManagement.tsx`, visible only to Super Admin, with an "Add Admin" form that either creates a new Admin account or, if the phone number already belongs to an existing account, promotes it in place.
- A **booking-approval queue**: new bookings are now created with `status: 'Pending Confirmation'` instead of immediately notifying nearby professionals. `BookingHistory.tsx` hides these from professionals entirely and adds an "Awaiting Confirmation" tab visible only to Admin/Super Admin. `BookingDetails_2.tsx` shows a "Confirm & Notify Professionals" action for that status — only pressing it flips the booking to `'New / Open'` and fires the professional push notification, which had previously fired unconditionally at booking creation in `BookingOtp.tsx`.

**Project-wide UI overlap sweep:**
Following a bug report that the back button sometimes landed on the FAQ page instead of Home, traced the cause to the Drawer navigator's `initialRouteName="(tabs)"` not being backed by an explicit `<Drawer.Screen name="(tabs)">` entry — `FAQs`, the first explicitly declared screen, was becoming the fallback target whenever navigation reset to "the first screen." Fixed by declaring `(tabs)` first, hidden from the drawer's own item list since the custom drawer content renders its own menu. Separately audited every modal and form screen in the project for two classes of bug: bottom sheets whose action buttons sat flush against the Android gesture nav bar (missing `useSafeAreaInsets` padding — fixed in `ProfessionalVerification.tsx` and two spots in `UserManagement.tsx`), and text inputs that could be covered by the keyboard (found and fixed one gap, the newly-built Add Admin modal, which had no `KeyboardAvoidingView` at all).

**Structural database change — dedicated `professional` table:**
HR requested that the `admin` table contain only Super Admin and Admin accounts, with all professionals moved to their own table. Created a new `professional` table mirroring `admin`'s columns (minus `role`, since the table itself now encodes that), migrated all 159 professional-role rows out of `admin` and into `professional`, deleted them from `admin`, and updated every file that had queried `admin` for professional data — `AdminLogin.tsx` (tries `admin` first, falls back to `professional`), `PostApiCareer.ts`, `ProfessionalVerification.tsx`, `UserManagement.tsx` (including reworking the Add Admin promotion logic to move a row across tables rather than flip a role field), `AdminChangePassword.tsx`, and `CustomDrawer.tsx`. Verified the full login path live against the database for all three account tiers before considering the migration complete.

**Learning:**
The recurring theme today was that a live database's actual schema is the only reliable source of truth — column names assumed from earlier code, however confidently written, can silently drift out of sync with reality after a migration, and Supabase/PostgREST will not always surface this as a loud error; several of these bugs failed silently and just returned empty data. Verifying every assumption directly against the database before writing a fix, rather than trusting what a query "should" return, caught issues that would otherwise have taken far longer to diagnose from bug reports alone. Splitting `professional` out of `admin` also reinforced a database design principle: when two categories of account need fundamentally different fields or lifecycles, a separate table with its own identity is often cleaner than a shared table distinguished only by a `role` flag, even though it means every consumer of that data has to be updated to know which table to query.

**Skills Gained:**
Supabase/PostgREST — live schema verification workflow, `.or()` multi-format phone matching, RLS policy authoring for a new table (`for all to anon using (true) with check (true)`), REST API bulk insert/delete for data migration · React Native — `useSafeAreaInsets` for modal/keyboard-safe bottom action bars, Drawer navigator `initialRouteName` vs explicit `Drawer.Screen` ordering · Application architecture — role-based access control design, moving a booking notification trigger from creation-time to an explicit approval step, cross-table record promotion (insert + delete as a single logical "move")

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The back button intermittently landing on the FAQ screen had no obvious trigger and no error in the logs. Root cause: `initialRouteName="(tabs)"` was set on the Drawer navigator, but `(tabs)` was never explicitly declared as a `Drawer.Screen` — it was only discovered implicitly via file-based routing, while `FAQs` was the first screen explicitly declared. Fix: declared `(tabs)` first, with `drawerItemStyle: { display: 'none' }` since the custom drawer content already renders its own menu items. (2) Promoting an existing Professional to Admin used to simply flip a `role` field, but after splitting the tables this could no longer work, since the two account types now live in physically separate tables. Fix: reworked the promotion logic to insert the professional's existing name/phone/PIN into `admin` and then delete the original row from `professional`, keeping their login credentials unchanged through the move. (3) A brand-new Supabase table created via the SQL Editor had Row Level Security enabled by default with no policy, so the app's anon key was silently rejected on insert. Fix: added an explicit `for all to anon` policy matching the access pattern already used on the `admin` table.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today reinforced that "the code compiles and the logic looks right" is not the same as "it works" — several of the bugs fixed today had been silently broken for a while, since Supabase queries against nonexistent columns simply return no data rather than throwing where a developer would immediately notice. Going forward, I want to make live-schema verification a standard first step before extending any feature that touches the database, rather than an audit I only perform after something visibly breaks. The role-split and access-control work today also gave me a much clearer sense of how a permissions system needs to be designed deliberately — deciding what's shared, what's exclusive, and what happens to existing data when a boundary changes — rather than added on top of an existing single-table structure as an afterthought.

### Day 39 — 06 July 2026 (Monday)

**Progress:**
Today's session covered three areas: building a new Lead Opening Fee payment feature for professionals, removing every remaining trace of the legacy Airtable backend from the codebase, and a targeted bug-fixing pass across the payment, authentication, and notification layers that resolved eight concrete defects.

**Lead Opening Fee feature — masked leads, Khalti checkout, and a new `lead_unlocks` table:**
HR requested a monetisation feature: a professional must pay a small fee before a new booking's customer contact details are revealed to them. Implemented the full flow across three layers. First, a shared `maskCustomerName` utility (`src/utils/maskName.ts`) truncates each word of a name to its first three letters (e.g. "Pratik Thapa" → "Pra... Tha..."), used both in `BookingHistory.tsx`/`BookingDetails_1.tsx` for the UI and in `api/notifications.ts` for outbound push text. Second, `BookingDetails_1.tsx` now checks a new Supabase table, `lead_unlocks` (`booking_id`, `professional_phone`, `pidx`, `amount`, unique on the first two columns), on every focus via `useFocusEffect` — if the current professional hasn't paid for that specific booking, the screen shows the masked name, hides the phone number, and replaces the Accept/Reject buttons with a "Pay NPR 49 to View Contact" button; Super Admins bypass the gate entirely. Third, a new screen, `LeadPayment.tsx`, presents the fee (NPR 49 against a struck-through "Regular Price NPR 100"), calls the existing `initiateKhaltiPayment` helper, opens the real Khalti checkout via `Linking.openURL`, and polls `lookupKhaltiPayment` every four seconds until the transaction reports `Completed`, at which point it writes the unlock row and returns the professional to the now-unlocked booking.

**Full Airtable removal:**
The project had been fully migrated to Supabase in an earlier session, but Airtable references were left behind in three places, discovered by grepping the whole repository case-insensitively for "airtable". Deleted `api/airtable.ts` (an unused Airtable SDK client with no importers) and `app/(drawer)/AdminCreate.tsx` (an orphaned Firebase-Auth account-creation screen, unreferenced by any navigation path, that still called the Airtable REST API directly for career-applicant lookups). Renamed the misleadingly-named `api/helper/fetchBookingDataAirtable.ts` — which already queried Supabase internally, not Airtable, a naming leftover from the migration — to `fetchBookingData.ts`, and its exported `fetchBookingsFromAirtable` function to `fetchBookings`, updating all four call sites (`BookingHistory.tsx`, `BookingDetails_1.tsx`, `BookingDetails_2.tsx`, `SuperAdminHistory.tsx`). Removed the now-dead `EXPO_PUBLIC_AIRTABLE_*` environment variables from both `.env` and both `eas.json` build profiles, and uninstalled the unused `airtable` npm package. A full-repository search after the cleanup confirmed zero remaining "airtable" references anywhere in the codebase.

**Bug-fixing pass — eight defects across payment, auth, and notifications:**
Ran a systematic audit of the payment, login, booking-acceptance, and notification code paths and fixed every concrete bug found:

1. **Forgot-PIN flow was unusable** (`AdminChangePassword.tsx`) — the "Reset PIN" mode still required entering the *current* PIN to proceed, which is impossible for someone who forgot it. Replaced the current-PIN requirement in reset mode with a proper SMS OTP step: a "Send Verification Code" button looks up the phone across `admin`/`professional`, texts a generated 4-digit code via Sparrow, and the new PIN is only accepted after that code is verified — the existing logged-in "Change PIN" mode is untouched.
2. **Accepting a booking never locked it** (`BookingDetails_1.tsx`) — two professionals could both tap "Accept This Offer" on the same "New / Open" booking, since nothing changed its status until later. Added a `claimBooking` helper (`api/helper/updateBookingStatus.ts`) that does a conditional Supabase update (only transitions status if it still matches the expected `fromStatus`); if the claim returns no row, the second professional now sees "This booking was just accepted by another professional" instead of silently double-booking the customer.
3. **A failed unlock-write after a successful Khalti payment froze the UI forever** (`LeadPayment.tsx`) — `recordLeadUnlock` was inside the same `try` block as the payment-status poll, so if the Supabase insert failed after Khalti already confirmed the charge, the interval was already cleared and the screen was stuck on "Waiting for payment confirmation…" permanently. Split payment confirmation from unlock recording into a dedicated `finalizeUnlock` function with its own error state ("Retry") that re-checks `isLeadUnlocked` before re-inserting, so a retry can never double-charge the professional.
4. **A failed `professional` table insert during signup left an orphaned, unloginable account** (`api/PostApiCareer.ts`) — if the second insert (which holds the login PIN) failed after the first `workforce` insert succeeded, the error was silently discarded; an admin could approve the applicant, but they could never log in since no login record existed. Added explicit error handling that rolls back the `workforce` row and throws, surfacing the failure to the signup UI instead of leaving a broken half-created account.
5. **Wrong provider phone in the "Booking Accepted" push** (`api/notifications.ts`) — `notifyUsers` tried to read the accepting professional's phone number from Firebase Auth's `currentUser`, but this app authenticates admins and professionals via Supabase phone+PIN, never Firebase Auth, so the value was always `null` and the notification always showed the generic fallback "A service provider" even though the real phone number was already available in the calling code. Changed `notifyUsers` to accept the phone as an explicit parameter and updated `BookingDetails_1.tsx` to pass it through.
6. **A non-functional QR code implied a payment method that didn't work** (`KhaltiPayment.tsx`) — when Khalti's merchant API call failed or wasn't activated, the screen fell back to rendering a QR code that encoded nothing but the professional's bare phone number; scanning it does not open Khalti or trigger any payment, so the "scan to pay" UI was misleading. Changed the fallback to only render a QR when a real, scannable `payment_url` exists; otherwise it shows the phone number as plain labelled text with an explicit instruction to send payment manually.
7. **Promoting a professional to Admin left their old profile active** (`UserManagement.tsx`) — `handleAddAdmin` moved the row from `professional` to `admin` but never touched the matching `workforce` row, so the person kept receiving job-lead push notifications and SMS as an "Active" professional even after becoming an Admin. Added a `workforce.profile_status = 'Inactive'` update immediately after the table move.
8. **A dead SMS filter targeted a tag that is never set** (`api/notifications.ts`) — the unused `notifyProfessionals` function's SMS branch filtered on an OneSignal tag named `area`, but every login path only ever sets a tag named `city` (from `working_areas[0]`); the filter could never have matched any device. Corrected the tag key to `city` so the function will behave correctly if it is ever wired up.

All fixes were verified with `npx tsc --noEmit` (zero errors) and a targeted `eslint` pass on every touched file (no new warnings or errors beyond pre-existing, unrelated ones).

**Learning:**
The Lead Opening Fee feature reinforced a pattern already used elsewhere in the project — checking access on `useFocusEffect` rather than a one-time `useEffect` — because the unlock state can change from a screen the user navigates to and back from (the payment screen), and a mount-only check would show stale, pre-payment UI on return. The Airtable cleanup was a good reminder that a rename left half-finished (a file and function still named after a system it no longer talks to) is its own category of technical debt — it does not break anything at runtime, but it actively misleads the next person reading the code into thinking a dependency still exists. The bug-fixing pass reinforced that a fallback path (the "Payment Received" manual-confirm button, the phone-number QR, the Firebase Auth phone lookup) is often where correctness quietly degrades, because the primary path is exercised and tested far more often than what happens when it fails or was never fully wired up in the first place. Several of today's bugs — the double-accept race condition and the frozen payment-confirmation UI — were also reminders that any client-only architecture without a server-side transaction layer needs its state transitions written as explicit conditional updates ("only change status if it still matches X"), not unconditional writes, whenever more than one actor could plausibly act on the same record at the same time.

**Skills Gained:**
React Native / Expo Router — `useFocusEffect` for re-checking gated state on screen return, conditional Supabase updates as a lightweight optimistic-locking pattern in a client-only architecture · Payments — Khalti `epayment/initiate` + `epayment/lookup` polling lifecycle, designing a retry path that cannot double-charge by re-checking idempotent state before a repeat write · Security / privacy UX — name masking for pre-payment lead protection, replacing a misleading non-functional QR with an honest manual-payment affordance · Debugging methodology — whole-repository grep-driven dead-code and stale-reference auditing, tracing a "wrong data displayed" bug back to an authentication system the screen doesn't actually use · Node/Expo tooling — regenerating Expo Router's typed-route definitions by briefly running the dev server after adding a new screen, since `expo export` alone does not refresh `.expo/types/router.d.ts`

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) A newly added screen (`LeadPayment.tsx`) was rejected by `tsc` with a typed-routes error even though the route worked correctly at runtime. Root cause: Expo Router's `.expo/types/router.d.ts` is only regenerated by the interactive dev server's file watcher, not by `expo export`, so a route added and then only "export"-built stays absent from the type union. Fix: briefly ran `npx expo start` in the background until the type file regenerated with the new route, then stopped the server and re-ran `tsc --noEmit` clean. (2) The live Khalti payment kept failing with a Khalti-side "Invalid token" error despite the correct live secret/public keys being present in `.env`. Root cause: `api/khalti.ts` defaulted its base URL to `a.khalti.com`, Khalti's sandbox host, while the configured keys were `live_*` production keys — a live key presented to the sandbox host is rejected. Fix: added `EXPO_PUBLIC_KHALTI_BASE_URL=https://khalti.com/api/v2` to `.env` so the live keys hit the matching live host. (3) Recording a lead unlock after a successful payment could fail independently of the payment itself, and the original code had no way to distinguish "payment failed, safe to retry the charge" from "payment succeeded, only the record failed" — conflating the two risked a double charge on retry. Fix: introduced a dedicated `recordFailed` stage with its own retry handler that never re-invokes `initiateKhaltiPayment`, only re-attempts the Supabase write after confirming via `isLeadUnlocked` that it hasn't already succeeded.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's session had a different shape from most previous days — instead of one feature end to end, it was split between building something new (the lead payment flow), removing something that should no longer exist (Airtable), and hardening things that already existed but had quiet defects (the eight bugs). All three felt equally necessary: a monetisation feature is worthless if the payment confirmation can silently strand a professional who already paid, and a codebase with leftover references to a system it no longer uses makes every future change slower because the next person has to first figure out what's real. The bug-fixing pass in particular changed how I read code that involves money or account status — I now specifically look for what happens when the "everything worked" assumption is wrong, since that is exactly where today's most serious issues (the double-accept race and the payment-confirmation freeze) were hiding.

---

### Day 40 — 07 July 2026 (Wednesday)

**Progress:**
Today's session covered a full bug audit of the HomeSewa codebase, a large batch of correctness and access-control fixes, a security-hardening backend that was built and then deliberately reverted after a deployment blocker, and a second dead-integration cleanup pass.

**Lead-reject flow — from a dead button to a real feature:**
HR asked what happens when a professional rejects a lead after opening it. Investigating `BookingDetails_1.tsx` showed the "Reject" button was a no-op — it just navigated back to the booking list with no database write, no record of the rejection, and no effect on the booking's visibility to other professionals. Built the missing feature: a new `lead_rejections` table (`booking_id`, `professional_phone`, unique on the pair), `api/leadRejections.ts` (`recordLeadRejection`, `isLeadRejected`, `getRejectedBookingIds`), a confirm-dialog-gated "Reject" action in `BookingDetails_1.tsx` that records the rejection, and a filter in `BookingHistory.tsx` that hides a rejected booking from that specific professional's own list going forward — while leaving the booking's actual status untouched so it stays fully visible and acceptable to every other professional.

**Full codebase bug audit:** Ran a five-way parallel audit (booking/lead flow, auth/OTP, payments, notifications/admin, general screens) and verified roughly two dozen concrete bugs, ranging from a completely non-functional admin session guard to minor phone-formatting inconsistencies.

**App-code fixes applied:**
- `app/_layout.tsx` / `app/(drawer)/Admin.tsx` — the admin route guard checked `segments[0] === 'admin'`, but admin screens live under `(drawer)/admin/...`, so the condition was never true; it was also driven by a Firebase auth listener nothing in the app ever signs into. Replaced both with an `AsyncStorage`-based session check against the real Supabase phone+PIN login state, with the segment match corrected and `AdminLogin` explicitly excluded so a logged-out user can still reach the login screen itself.
- Added the missing `AsyncStorage`-based access guard (present on every other admin screen) to `SuperAdminHistory.tsx`, `HelpBox.tsx`, and `HelpBoxDetail.tsx`, which had none at all — any logged-in professional could previously open them.
- Corrected `AdminNotifications.tsx` and `ProfessionalVerification.tsx`'s "Super Admin only" checks, which were actually gating on "any admin-table account" (`adminRole === 'super_admin'` instead of `adminTable === 'admins'`); left `UserManagement.tsx`'s equivalent check as a wording-only fix, since its own code already intentionally allows plain Admins in for everything except the admin-account-management tab.
- `BookingDetails_1.tsx` — `handleAcceptOffer`'s `catch` block was navigating to the "accepted" screen even when the claim itself had thrown, showing a false success state. Now shows an error alert instead.
- `BookingDetails_2.tsx` — both the work-status update and the "Confirm & Notify Professionals" action were unconditional overwrites; two staff editing the same booking could silently clobber each other. Replaced both with the same conditional-update pattern (`claimBooking`) already used for professional accept-race protection.
- `BookingVerify.tsx` — the post-OTP screen told customers their booking was "confirmed," but the actual status set is `Pending Confirmation` pending staff review; corrected the copy.
- Deleted a five-screen dead cluster (`BookingConfirmation.tsx`, `GeneratePaymentDetails.tsx`, `PaymentDetails.tsx`, `PaymentMethod.tsx`, `KhaltiPayment.tsx`) after confirming via repo-wide reference search that nothing routes into it from any live flow — it ran on hardcoded dummy data, had a "Verify OTP" button that never checked the OTP, and showed a fake US bank account.
- `Partnership.tsx`/`PostApiPartnership.ts` — a key-name mismatch (`"eMail"` sent vs. `"Email"` read) meant every partnership application silently lost its email address.
- `HeadshotCropModal.tsx`/`Career.tsx` — the pinch/pan "crop" UI was purely cosmetic; `onSave` returned the original, uncropped image. Implemented a real crop using `expo-image-manipulator`, computing the crop rectangle from the viewport's "cover" fit plus the user's pan/zoom transform.
- `fetchServicesID.ts` — a failed or empty services lookup permanently cached itself as the result for the rest of the session; cleared the in-flight promise on failure so a later call retries.
- `bookingPdfTemplate.ts` — customer-entered fields (name, work description, etc.) were interpolated into the generated receipt's HTML with no escaping; added an `escapeHtml` helper across every interpolated field.
- `ProfessionalVerification.tsx` — `handleApprove` discarded the Supabase update errors and always showed "Approved," even when the account was never actually activated; now checks both errors and alerts on failure.
- Minor phone-format fixes: a stray space inside a `tel:` link in `Contact.tsx`, an unformatted emergency-contact number in `Career.tsx`, and a missing digit-strip before building the SMS destination in `helpboxOTP.tsx`.

**Security-hardening backend — built, then reverted:**
The audit also surfaced real security issues that can't be fixed in the client alone: PINs and OTPs compared client-side with no rate limiting, the Khalti and Sparrow SMS secret keys bundled directly into the app, and lead-unlock payments recorded without any server-side check that the payment actually completed for the right amount. Designed and built a Supabase Edge Functions layer (`admin-login`, `set-pin`, `send-otp`/`verify-otp`, `send-sms`, `create-professional-login`, `khalti-initiate-lead-payment`, `khalti-confirm-lead-unlock`) plus a SQL migration adding bcrypt `pin_hash` columns, a login-attempt lockout, an `otp_codes` table, and a uniqueness constraint on Khalti's `pidx` to prevent a completed payment being replayed across bookings. Wired the client screens (`AdminLogin.tsx`, `AdminChangePassword.tsx`, every OTP screen, `LeadPayment.tsx`, `PostApiCareer.ts`) to call these functions instead of doing the checks locally.

Attempting to deploy surfaced an access problem: the Supabase CLI account in use only has the `Developer` role on the project's organization, and `supabase secrets set`/`functions deploy` both require `Owner`/`Admin`. Since the two accounts with sufficient access weren't immediately reachable, and the client code as wired would fail (login/OTP/payments all calling functions that don't exist server-side yet) until someone deploys it, made the call to fully revert the client-side wiring back to the original direct-Supabase-call behaviour — restoring the pre-hardening bugs deliberately, as a known, accepted tradeoff — so the app keeps working today. The Edge Function code and SQL migration are left in place in `supabase/`, untouched and ready to be reconnected once deploy access is sorted; reconnecting is a small, well-defined task since the client-side call sites are already known from this session.

**Second dead-integration cleanup pass:** A diff review of `eas.json` surfaced leftover Cloudinary, Twilio, and Airtable environment variables. Confirmed Airtable was already fully removed in an earlier session (per Day 39's log) with zero remaining references anywhere. Grepped for Cloudinary/Twilio usage and found both `api/otp.ts` (a Twilio Verify OTP client) and `api/uploadToCloudinary.ts` were defined but never imported anywhere — dead code left over from an earlier, since-abandoned integration attempt. Deleted both files along with their four associated `EXPO_PUBLIC_*` env vars from both `eas.json` build profiles.

**Learning:**
The reject-flow investigation reinforced a pattern already established elsewhere in this codebase's pay-per-lead model: a per-professional action (rejecting, like unlocking) should only ever affect that professional's own view, never the shared booking record itself, since multiple professionals can independently be mid-flow on the same lead at once. The wider bug audit reinforced that a broken access-control check is often *worse* than a missing one, because it looks like protection is in place — the admin route guard and several "Super Admin only" checks had clearly-intentional code sitting right next to the screen, and would pass a quick glance, while actually gating on nothing at all. The security-hardening revert was the more difficult judgment call of the day: it's tempting to treat "the code is correct" as the finish line, but a fix that depends on infrastructure nobody can currently deploy is not actually a fix yet — shipping the client half of a two-sided change and leaving the server half permanently pending would have left the app broken, not improved. Reverting cleanly, while keeping the backend code intact and ready, preserves the work without pretending the job is done.

**Skills Gained:**
Supabase — Row Level Security scoping decisions (locking down a brand-new table fully vs. a read-heavy existing one with a read-only policy), Edge Functions as a trusted server boundary for PIN/OTP/payment verification, recognising when a database-level fix's blast radius exceeds what was actually asked · React Native / Expo — `expo-image-manipulator` crop-rectangle math from a "cover"-fit viewport plus pan/zoom transform, conditional Supabase updates as a lightweight optimistic-locking pattern · Debugging methodology — parallel multi-angle codebase auditing, verifying an access-control bug by tracing the exact route-segment/table-name condition rather than trusting that a guard "looks correct" · Engineering judgement — deciding to revert a completed but undeployable change rather than ship a half-working state, and structuring the revert to preserve the reusable parts of the work

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) A dead five-screen payment cluster (`BookingConfirmation.tsx` and friends) initially looked like it just needed its fake data wired to something real. A repo-wide reference search showed it was never reachable from any live screen at all — the correct fix was deletion, not repair, since "fixing" unreachable code would have been wasted, unverifiable effort. (2) After building and wiring the full security-hardening backend, `supabase functions deploy`/`secrets set` failed with an organization-permissions error. Diagnosed by checking the Supabase dashboard's Team page directly, which confirmed the active account was `Developer`-tier while two other accounts held `Administrator`/`Owner`. With neither reachable in time, reverted every client-side call site back to its pre-hardening implementation file-by-file, taking care to preserve the unrelated bug fixes that had been made to some of the same files (e.g. `BookingDetails_1.tsx`'s false-success fix, `BookingDetails_2.tsx`'s race-condition fix) rather than blanket-discarding the whole diff.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today had an unusual arc: build a real fix, discover it can't be deployed yet, and revert it deliberately rather than leave the app in a broken half-state. My instinct going in was that reverting completed work would feel like a step backward, but sitting on an undeployed dependency would have been worse — the app would have failed to log anyone in. I'm glad the backend code didn't have to be thrown away; it's sitting in `supabase/` ready to go the moment deploy access is sorted, which made the revert feel like a pause rather than a loss. The lead-reject flow and the wider bug-fixing pass were more straightforward, but the access-control bugs in particular were a good reminder to verify a security check actually fires, rather than trusting that its presence in the code means it works.
