## Week 8 — 08 July to 14 July 2026 (Wednesday – Tuesday)

---

### Day 41 — 08 July 2026 (Wednesday)

**Progress:**
Today's session focused entirely on the in-app notification system started the day before: fixing a layout bug on the new notification screen, reworking who can send versus only view notifications, extending notification visibility to logged-out visitors, and tracking down a real content bug that had been silently affecting every "New Professional Application" alert sent so far.

**Notification screen polish:** The new "My Notifications" screen had an oversized gap between the header bar and the title, caused by a leftover large `paddingTop` (`hp('9%')`) that duplicated spacing already provided by the shared header component; corrected it to match the `hp('2%')` convention used on other admin screens, and simplified the title text to "Notifications."

**Consolidating notification access by role:** Previously there were two separate entry points — a "My Notifications" history view for any logged-in professional/admin, and a fully separate "Send Notification" screen reachable by any admin-table account. Reworked this into a single "Notifications" entry in the drawer: tapping it routes a true Super Admin to the existing compose screen (`AdminNotifications.tsx`, tabs for targeting All Users/Professionals/Customers plus an "All Notifications" history tab), while every other logged-in role — plain Admin and Professional — is routed instead to the read-only history screen (`Notifications.tsx`). `AdminNotifications.tsx`'s access guard was tightened from "any admin-table account" to `adminRole === 'super_admin'` specifically, since the compose/send capability is no longer meant to be reachable by plain Admins at all — they get the same read-only list everyone else does.

**Extending Notifications to logged-out visitors:** HR asked for a general-announcements view to be visible even before logging in, scoped to HomeSewa-wide updates only — not personal leads or admin content. Added the same "Notifications" item to the logged-out drawer, and updated `Notifications.tsx` so an anonymous session queries only the `all`/`customer_all` audience rows instead of returning nothing. Since the logged-out drawer was already tightly packed with nine menu items plus two dividers, added a `compact` sizing variant to the shared `MenuItem` component (smaller padding, icon, and font size) applied only to the logged-out item set, so the extra entry fits without growing the drawer card's height.

**The "(N/A)" notification bug:** HR flagged that every "New Professional Application" push notification showed the applicant's expertise as "(N/A)" regardless of what they had actually selected on the form. Traced it to a key-name mismatch: `Career.tsx` saves the chosen services under the key `"Your Expertise"`, but `CareerOTP.tsx` — which builds the notification text right after signup — was reading a different, nonexistent key, `careerData['Positions']`. Since that key never existed on the object, the services array was always empty, and `notifyAdminNewProfessional`'s fallback (`'N/A'` when the list is empty) fired every single time, for every applicant, not just the one HR happened to notice. Fixed by reading the correct `"Your Expertise"` key.

**Learning:**
The "(N/A)" bug was a good example of how a silent fallback can mask a 100%-reproducing defect as if it were an edge case — because the notification never crashed and always showed *something* plausible-looking ("N/A" reads like a legitimate placeholder for someone with no listed expertise), nobody had reason to suspect it was firing on every single application rather than only the occasional edge case. The notification-access rework also reinforced that "who can view" and "who can send" are two different permissions that had been conflated in the same guard condition (`adminTable === 'admins'`); separating them (route by table for view-access, but gate the compose form specifically on `adminRole`) made the access model match what HR actually wanted rather than what was easiest to check for.

**Skills Gained:**
React Native — building a reusable `compact` size variant for a shared list-item component so one screen state (a longer, unauthenticated menu) can shrink without affecting every other consumer of the same component · Debugging methodology — treating a "wrong value shown" bug as a data-flow tracing problem (where is this string actually assigned, saved, and re-read) rather than assuming it's random or applicant-specific, which is what led directly to the key-name mismatch · Access control design — distinguishing view-access role checks from write/compose-access role checks instead of using a single shared guard condition for both

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Adding a "Notifications" entry to the already-crowded logged-out drawer risked reintroducing the same item-overlap bug fixed earlier in the internship (increasing card height to fit more items). Instead of growing the drawer again, added a `compact` prop to the shared `MenuItem` component and applied it only to the logged-out item set, keeping the card height fixed while giving those items smaller padding/icon/font sizes. (2) The "(N/A)" bug had no error or crash to point to — the notification was "working," just wrong. Resolved it by reading both ends of the data handoff (where `Career.tsx` builds the applicant object versus where `CareerOTP.tsx` reads it back out of `AsyncStorage`) side by side, which immediately surfaced the `"Your Expertise"` vs `"Positions"` key mismatch.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's fixes were smaller in scope than the last few sessions but reinforced a theme running through this whole notification-system pass: a feature can look completely fine in the UI while quietly sending wrong or incomplete data underneath, and the only way to catch that is to trace the actual data path end to end rather than trusting that "no crash" means "no bug." The access-control consolidation was also a useful exercise in separating two permissions that had been bundled into one check for convenience — it's a small change in the code but a much clearer match to what was actually being asked for.

---

### Day 42 — 09 July 2026 (Thursday)

**Progress:**
Today covered a wide spread of work: a notification-targeting rework, two separate silent data-loss bugs traced to their actual root cause and fixed, a User Management filtering/pagination/admin-tooling pass, a booking-detail calculation and formatting fix, an in-app document preview replacing a browser hand-off, a full dead-code audit, and — the largest piece — building a human-readable ID scheme across five different record types at HR's request.

**Notification audience rework:** The "Send Notification" screen's audience selector was a horizontal tab bar that visibly overflowed the screen width, cutting off the last option. Replaced it with a single dropdown (tap-to-select list, no search box, per HR's explicit preference for a plain dropdown over a searchable one) and added a "Select All" row to the existing service-type multi-select so an admin isn't stuck tapping 40 individual service checkboxes one at a time. Also added a service-filtered Customer targeting path (`notifyCustomersByService`, querying `booking.services` with `.overlaps()` the same way professional targeting already did) so HR can send an offer to, say, only customers who've booked AC servicing before, instead of broadcasting to every customer. Later in the day, expanded the audience model from four categories to five — Admin, Customer, Professional, Public, All — adding `notifyAdminsBroadcast` (filters the existing `role = admin` OneSignal tag) and `notifyPublic` (`role` tag `not_exists`, reaching installs that never logged in as anything) so the app's three existing tag values are now all individually reachable plus a true "everyone" option.

**Two silent data-loss bugs, same root cause:** HR reported that HelpBox's "Save Note" appeared to erase the note it had just saved. The database write itself was correct; the bug was that the `helpbox:update` event fired after saving only carried `status`/`modified_at`, not the note text, so the screen's local in-memory ticket list never got updated with the new note — reopening the same ticket without a full reload read the stale, pre-save value back out and looked like data loss. Fixed by including `reply`/`issue` in the event payload and merging them into local state, and separately changed "Save Note" to no longer navigate back to the list afterward (only "Mark as Solved" does now, since closing a ticket is the one action that should actually end the session). The exact same class of bug turned up independently in `UpdateProfile.tsx`: querying the `admin` table's schema directly confirmed a `photo_url` column already existed, but the admin save path only ever wrote `full_name`, and the load path never even selected `photo_url` back out — so a newly uploaded profile photo was silently discarded on every save, every time, for every admin account. Fixed both directions, and added the same missing "did this update actually match a row" verification (`.select()` + checking the returned row count) to both the HelpBox save and the profile save, since neither had ever checked for that before.

**Drawer avatar follow-on:** The profile-photo fix surfaced a second, related bug — `CustomDrawer.tsx` hardcoded `photoUrl = null` for any non-professional account, so even with the save path now fixed, an admin's photo could never appear in the drawer. Fixed to read `admin.photo_url`, with professionals still preferring their richer `workforce.headshot_url` but now falling back to `professional.photo_url` if no workforce headshot exists.

**User Management tooling:** Added City and Service dropdown filters to the Professionals tab and City and Area filters to the Customers tab, plus pagination across all three tabs (Professionals/Customers/Admins) so the lists stay usable as they grow past 15–20 rows. Built a debounced phone-number search inside the "Add Admin" form that looks up matching Professionals and Customers and pre-fills the name/phone fields on tap, and explained the underlying promotion logic to HR directly: an existing Professional's account is upgraded in place (their PIN carries over, their `professional` row is deleted, their `workforce` profile is deactivated), while a Customer has no login credentials at all today, so "promoting" one just pre-fills a brand-new Admin account that HR sets a fresh PIN for. Also added a per-admin City Access scheme at HR's request — a new `allowed_cities` array column on `admin`, assignable when creating an account and editable afterward per admin, defaulting to unrestricted when left empty. Flagged clearly to HR that this is currently assignment-only; no other admin screen actually filters its data by an admin's allowed cities yet, since that would touch several more files.

**Booking detail fixes:** "Approx Days to complete" was computing a plain calendar-day difference between the starting and ending date, which showed "0 Days" for any job scheduled to start and finish on the same day. Switched it to an inclusive day count so a same-day job now correctly reads "1 Day." At HR's request, reformatted the Booking/Starting/Ending date fields from "Wednesday July 8 2026" to "8 July 2026, Wednesday" — a single shared date-formatting helper feeds both booking-detail screens plus the PDF receipt and share text, so the change applied consistently everywhere in one edit. Also added spacing between the customer's name and phone number on both booking-detail screens, and changed the Accept/Reject buttons from a stacked, full-width layout to a single row, giving Accept more width than Reject per HR's follow-up.

**In-app document preview:** `ProfessionalVerification.tsx`'s "View ID / Document" button handed a short-lived signed Supabase Storage URL to `Linking.openURL`, opening the applicant's citizenship/ID photo in the device's system browser. Replaced it with an in-app, pinch-zoomable preview Modal so a private document link is never passed outside the app at all, addressing HR's explicit requirement that it stay in-app with no public/browser exposure.

**Dead-code audit:** Cross-referenced every source file against actual `import` statements, every route file under `app/` against real navigation call sites (Expo Router routes don't need to be imported by name to be "live," so this meant grepping for `router.push`/`Link href`/drawer entries/notification deep-links instead), and ran `npx depcheck` for unused npm packages. Verified and removed eleven files with zero references anywhere: a complete Firebase phone-auth subsystem (`firebaseConfig.js`, `firebaseOtp.ts`, `otpStore.ts`) long superseded by the app's real Supabase-edge-function OTP flow, a Redux scaffold (`store.ts`, `authSlice.ts`) that was never actually wired up anywhere (no `Provider`, no hook usage), a component that was a byte-for-byte duplicate of another under a different name, and five other zero-import orphans — confirmed clean with a full `tsc --noEmit` pass both before and after deleting.

**Human-readable ID scheme:** HR asked for booking, help, partnership, workforce, and customer records to each be identifiable at a glance by a leading letter, mirroring the "H0001" convention HelpBox tickets already used. Started with Booking as a display-only "B123" prefix — applied everywhere the raw number is actually shown to a user (both booking-detail screens, the PDF receipt, the share-sheet text) while deliberately leaving the real numeric `booking_id` completely untouched everywhere it's used programmatically: Khalti payment order IDs, `.eq()` database lookups, an `AsyncStorage` key, and the two booking-history screens' sort/search comparators. HR then asked for the prefix to actually live in the database for all five types, not just be computed at display time. Wrote a migration adding a `display_id` generated column to `booking` ('B'), `workforce` ('W'), and `partnership` ('P'), each derived from that table's existing numeric primary key via Postgres `generated always as (...) stored` — a real, permanent column, but one that can never drift out of sync with the source id and requires zero changes to any existing foreign key or lookup. Customers had no ID at all going in, since `booking` just stores phone/name directly with no customer table behind it — created one, backfilled it from existing bookings (keeping each phone's most recent name), and added an insert trigger so every future booking keeps a customer row in sync automatically, with no manual maintenance. Built a full Partnership Applications admin screen from scratch — list, search, pagination, and a detail sheet with services/photos/registration-certificate links — since no admin view of partnership applications existed at all before today. Surfaced the Workforce UIN as a "W123" pill in both the Verification screen and User Management's Professionals tab; it turned out `uin` had only ever been used internally as a database lookup key and had never actually been shown to a user anywhere in the app.

**Learning:**
The two "vanishing data" bugs (HelpBox notes, profile photos) were the clearest lesson of the day: both looked, from the outside, like a save operation quietly failing, but in both cases the write to the database was actually correct — the bug was downstream, in code that either never read the new value back or never told the rest of the screen it had changed. That's a different debugging instinct than chasing a failed write: it means checking the full round trip (write → re-read → re-render) rather than stopping once the `UPDATE` statement itself looks right. The ID-prefix work was a good exercise in scoping a request correctly before building it — the first, safe version (computing "B123" purely for display) shipped in minutes with zero risk, and only after confirming HR specifically wanted it in the database did the larger migration-plus-new-table version get built; jumping straight to the biggest possible interpretation of "add a prefix" would have meant touching Khalti payment IDs and foreign keys for no reason.

**Skills Gained:**
React Native / Expo Router — debounced search inputs with cleanup (`useEffect` + `setTimeout` + a `cancelled` flag) for live phone-number lookups, in-app pinch-zoomable image preview via `ScrollView`'s `minimumZoomScale`/`maximumZoomScale` instead of handing a URL to the system browser · Supabase / Postgres — `generated always as (...) stored` columns for deriving a human-readable id from an existing primary key with zero risk to foreign keys, `AFTER INSERT` triggers with `ON CONFLICT ... DO UPDATE` for keeping a derived table in sync automatically, introspecting a live table's real columns via a direct REST query rather than guessing from code alone · Debugging methodology — tracing a "the save didn't work" report as a full read/write/re-render round trip instead of stopping at "the database write looks correct," and recognising the same missing-verification bug pattern (`.update()` without `.select()` to confirm a row was actually matched) independently in two unrelated screens · Dead-code auditing — distinguishing an "orphaned file" from an "orphaned route" in a file-based router, since route files don't need an import statement to be live

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The HelpBox "Save Note" bug had no error, no failed request, and no obviously wrong code in the save function itself — the actual defect was in a separate event-listener callback that merged only *some* of the saved fields back into local state. Resolved by tracing the full data path from the save button through the emitted event to the screen that re-renders from it, rather than stopping at the `UPDATE` call. (2) HR's ID-prefix request escalated twice in scope over the conversation — first "just Booking," then "actually store it in the database," then "build all five types." Kept each escalation additive and low-risk by generating every new `display_id` column from an existing primary key rather than replacing it, so nothing that already depended on the real numeric id (payments, foreign keys, sorting) needed to change at any step.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today reinforced something I keep re-learning on this project: a bug report of "it looks like my data disappeared" is almost never actually about the database losing data — it's about some other layer (a local cache, an event payload, a query that never selects the new column) not knowing the data changed. Finding that same pattern twice in one day, in two completely unrelated screens, made it much faster to recognise the second time. The ID-prefix feature was also a useful lesson in not over-building on the first version of a request — shipping the safe, display-only version first meant HR could see it working immediately, and only the parts that were actually asked for afterward (the real database columns, the new Partnership screen, the Customer table) got the larger investment.

---

### Day 43 — 10 July 2026 (Friday)

**Progress:**
Today covered two separate HR requests: adding a Terms & Conditions consent step across every customer-facing form, and answering (then actually solving) the question of whether Google Analytics is set up in the app.

**Terms & Conditions checkbox:** HR shared a screenshot mockup of the Book a Service form with a checkbox reading "I Accept Terms & Conditions," the phrase "Terms & Conditions" linked out to `https://HomeSewa.app/terms`, and an instruction to keep this on every form. Built a single reusable `TermsCheckbox` component (`components/bookings/TermsCheckbox.tsx`) — a tappable checkbox icon plus label text, with the "Terms & Conditions" portion styled as an underlined link that opens the URL via `Linking.openURL`. Wired it into all three real submission forms (`Book.tsx`, `Career.tsx`, `Partnership.tsx`): added an `acceptedTerms` boolean to each screen's state, reset it alongside the rest of the form in each `clearAllFields`, and added it as the last check in each `handleSubmit` validation chain so the form cannot be submitted — no API call, no upload, no navigation — until the box has actually been tapped. Contact.tsx was deliberately left alone since it has no submittable fields, just static contact info.

**"Have we added Google Analytics?"** HR asked this directly. Investigating `package.json` and `app.json` showed `@react-native-firebase/analytics` and `@react-native-firebase/app` were already installed and registered as Expo plugins, and a real `google-services.json` in the project root ties the app to Firebase project `homesewa-ad5d9`. However, a repo-wide search for `logEvent`, `getAnalytics()`, and `logScreenView` came back completely empty — meaning the SDK was present and initialized, but nothing in the app code was ever calling it. Reported this distinction to HR: the infrastructure was there, but no custom business events were being tracked, only whatever Firebase collects automatically. HR then pulled up the actual Firebase Analytics dashboard and shared a screenshot showing real, live numbers (121 users over 30 days, 85 over 7 days, active users by country) — confirming the SDK genuinely was initialized and reporting on real devices, just with no custom instrumentation on top.

**Building the actual instrumentation:** Added a central `lib/analytics.ts` helper exposing two functions, `logEvent()` and `logScreenView()`, both wrapped in try/catch so an analytics failure can never crash the app, plus a `sanitizeParams()` helper that joins array values (like a selected-expertise list) into a comma-separated string and truncates to Firebase's 100-character-per-value limit, since GA4 only accepts primitive param types. Wired automatic screen tracking into `app/_layout.tsx` using expo-router's `usePathname()`, so every navigation is logged without touching individual screens. Then added real conversion events to the three forms: `booking_submitted` / `booking_limit_reached` / `booking_form_cleared` in `Book.tsx`; `career_application_submitted` / `career_form_cleared` in `Career.tsx`; `partnership_application_submitted` / `partnership_form_cleared` in `Partnership.tsx` — each success event carrying relevant context (service, city, budget, priority, business type, etc.) so the eventual Firebase reports can break conversions down by more than just a raw count.

**Chasing down deprecation warnings:** Running the app with the new instrumentation surfaced a Metro warning that the namespaced `analytics()` API (`analytics().logEvent(...)`) is deprecated in favor of Firebase's modular API. Migrated `lib/analytics.ts` to `getApp()` + `getAnalytics()` + the modular `logEvent` import. A second run still warned, specifically on `logScreenView`, even through the modular call — reading the library's own `modular.ts` source directly showed that `logScreenView` is deprecated in *both* API styles, since its implementation is just a thin passthrough to the same underlying deprecated native call. Replaced it entirely: `logScreenView()` in the helper now calls `logEvent(analytics, 'screen_view', { screen_name, screen_class })` directly, which is Firebase's documented long-term replacement.

**Learning:**
The Google Analytics question was a good reminder that "is X installed" and "is X actually being used" are two different questions with two different answers — the Firebase SDK had been silently collecting default session data the entire time, but there was no way to answer a real business question ("how many bookings actually complete?") until today's event instrumentation existed. Verifying against the live Firebase console rather than trusting the code/config alone was also the right call — it confirmed the SDK was truly initialized on real devices, not just theoretically wired up. The deprecation-warning chase reinforced that migration guides don't always cover every method symmetrically: switching to the "correct" new modular API pattern still produced a warning, and only reading the library's actual source code (rather than pattern-matching on the warning text) revealed that the method itself, not just the calling convention, was being retired.

**Skills Gained:**
Firebase Analytics — custom event instrumentation with `@react-native-firebase/analytics`, modular API migration (`getApp`/`getAnalytics`/`logEvent`), automatic screen-view tracking via expo-router's `usePathname()`, GA4 param sanitization for array/complex values · React Native — reusable consent/checkbox component with `Linking.openURL`, gating form submission on a boolean acceptance flag · Debugging methodology — resolving an ambiguous deprecation warning by reading the third-party library's own source rather than guessing from the warning text alone

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The first deprecation fix (moving to `getAnalytics()` + modular `logEvent`) didn't fully resolve the warning — `logScreenView` kept firing it even through the "correct" new API. Opened `node_modules/@react-native-firebase/analytics/lib/modular.ts` directly and found the function's own implementation comment: "This is deprecated for both namespaced and modular." The real fix wasn't a different calling convention, it was to stop calling that function at all and log the `screen_view` event name directly through `logEvent()`. (2) Some form fields (selected expertise, services offered) are string arrays, but GA4 event params only accept primitives — handled with a small `sanitizeParams()` helper so every call site could stay simple without each screen needing its own array-to-string conversion.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's task highlighted the gap between "the analytics library is installed" and "we can actually answer HR's questions with data" — the Firebase project had been live and reporting basic session numbers the whole time, but there was genuinely no way to see which forms people were completing until today's instrumentation went in. I also want to flag the debugging habit that helped most today: when the first fix for the deprecation warning didn't fully work, going straight to the library's own source code (instead of re-reading migration blog posts) gave a definitive answer in minutes rather than more trial and error.

---

### Day 44 — 11 July 2026 (Saturday)

**Progress:**
Today was spent on general bug fixing and error resolution across two areas: UI/display issues and the auth/login flow. Went through screens flagged for visual inconsistencies — incorrect spacing, misaligned elements, and formatting glitches — and corrected them so the affected views render as intended across different screen sizes. On the auth side, reviewed the login flow (OTP/PIN entry and the professional/admin login paths) for edge cases that were producing incorrect states or silent failures, and fixed the ones that reproduced consistently, re-testing each flow end-to-end afterward to confirm the fix held rather than just suppressing the symptom.

**Learning:**
Reinforced that UI bugs and auth bugs need different debugging instincts: a display issue is usually visible and reproducible just by looking at the screen, while a login/auth bug often depends on a specific state (expired OTP, wrong PIN attempt count, a race between two async calls) that only shows up when you deliberately try to recreate that state rather than just re-running the happy path.

**Skills Gained:**
Debugging methodology — isolating whether a reported issue is a rendering/layout problem versus a logic/state problem before attempting a fix · React Native — adjusting layout and spacing issues to hold up across different screen sizes · Auth flow debugging — reproducing edge cases in OTP/PIN and login state rather than only verifying the default path.

**Challenge & Solution:**
Several of today's issues didn't have an obvious single cause, so the main challenge was narrowing down whether a given report was a UI-only problem or a deeper state/logic bug wearing a UI symptom. Resolved by reproducing each issue directly rather than guessing from the report alone, then fixing at the layer where the actual defect lived instead of patching around the symptom.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today was more routine than most entries in this log — a general pass on UI polish and login-flow reliability rather than one large feature — but it kept the app's existing screens consistent and the login path solid ahead of further feature work.
