### Day 45 — 12 July 2026 (Sunday)

**Progress:**
Today's session was dedicated entirely to end-to-end manual testing of HomeSewa, starting from a completely fresh install and working forward through every role that touches a single booking, rather than testing screens in isolation. The goal was to build and execute a structured test pass across the Customer, Professional, and Admin/Super Admin roles together, so that any bug caused by one role's state not lining up with what the next role expects would actually surface.

**Onboarding:** Verified the three onboarding screens (`onboarding1.tsx` — "Welcome to HomeSewa," `onboarding2.tsx` — "What can you do?," `onboarding3.tsx` — "Lets, Begin!") in sequence, confirmed the "SKIP" control on the first screen correctly jumps straight to Home, and confirmed `hasSeenOnboarding` is written to AsyncStorage correctly — reopening the app after onboarding routes straight to `/Home` via `app/index.tsx` instead of re-showing onboarding.

**Customer booking flow:** Walked the full path through `Home.tsx` → `Service.tsx` → `Book.tsx`. Deliberately submitted the "Book a Service" form with required fields left blank first, to confirm the "Validation Error" alerts fire correctly, then filled a complete booking (name, phone, service, start/end date, preferred time, city, area, priority, budget) and submitted. Reviewed the read-only "Booking Summary" screen (`BookingDetail.tsx`), used "Edit Booking" once to confirm it correctly routes back to a pre-filled `Book.tsx`, then confirmed forward into `BookingOtp.tsx`. Tested "Resend Code" before entering the correct OTP, and confirmed the booking record is created with `Status: 'Pending Confirmation'` — verified directly against the `booking` table that it is not yet visible to any professional at this stage, which is the intended gated state until an admin reviews it.

**Professional registration flow:** Completed the "Join Now" application in `Career.tsx` with a full test profile (name, phone, gender, headshot upload, expertise selection, years of experience, ID document upload, preferred city/working areas, emergency contact), submitted through `CareerOTP.tsx`'s OTP step, and confirmed the application lands in `workforce` with `profile_status: 'Waiting for Verification'` and that `notifyAdminNewProfessional` correctly fires a notification to the Super Admin.

**Admin verification and dispatch:** Logged in as Super Admin and opened `ProfessionalVerification.tsx`, confirmed the pending-count badge matched the test applicant, reviewed the uploaded ID via the in-app document preview, and tested "Approve" — confirmed `workforce.profile_status` and `professional.status` both flip to `Active` and a credentials SMS goes out. Separately submitted a second test applicant and tested "Reject" to confirm that path updates status to `Rejected` correctly. Returned to the booking from earlier, located it in `BookingHistory.tsx` under the "Awaiting Confirmation" filter, and tested "Confirm & Notify Professionals" in `BookingDetails_2.tsx`, confirming the status transition from `Pending Confirmation` to `New / Open` and that the area/service-matched push notification actually reaches a professional device.

**Professional acceptance and completion:** On the professional account, confirmed the push notification was received, then tested "Accept This Offer" in `BookingDetails_1.tsx`, confirming the atomic claim moves the booking to `Pending`, notifies the customer, and notifies other matching professionals that the job is no longer available. Finally, tested the "Work Status" → "Completed" path, confirming the completion OTP SMS fires and the booking correctly lands under the "Completed" filter in Booking History once verified.

**City-access re-check:** As a secondary pass, re-verified the `allowed_cities` admin city-assignment feature built earlier this week (single city, multiple cities, and "All Cities" when left empty) still saves and displays correctly in `UserManagement.tsx`, and re-confirmed for the record that it is display/assignment-only today — no other screen yet filters bookings by an admin's allowed cities, so this remains a flagged follow-up rather than something in scope to fix during today's testing pass.

**Learning:**
Running every role through one continuous booking — rather than testing the Customer form, the Career form, and the Admin screens as separate, disconnected checklists — surfaced how much of HomeSewa's correctness actually lives in the *transitions* between roles rather than inside any single screen: a booking is only correct if it stays invisible to professionals until admin confirmation, an applicant is only correct if their status change actually unlocks the right login, and a job is only correct if accepting it correctly locks out every other professional at the same time. None of those are checkable by looking at one screen in isolation; they only show up when the full chain is actually walked in order, end to end, the way a real user, professional, and admin would experience it across the same booking.

**Skills Gained:**
QA / Test Design — structuring a manual test pass around cross-role state transitions instead of per-screen checklists, so a booking's full lifecycle (`Pending Confirmation` → `New / Open` → `Pending` → `Completed`) is exercised as one continuous chain · Manual testing methodology — deliberately testing negative paths (empty-field validation, OTP resend, reject flows) alongside the happy path rather than only confirming the default flow works · Full-stack tracing — confirming each UI action's effect directly against the underlying Supabase table state (`booking.Status`, `workforce.profile_status`, `professional.status`) rather than trusting the UI alone.

**Challenge & Solution:**
The main challenge was keeping three separate logged-in sessions (Customer, Professional, Super Admin) coordinated on the same test booking without cross-contaminating state — for example, confirming a booking as Super Admin before the professional test account had actually been approved would have made the "professional receives the job" step untestable. Solved by sequencing the test strictly in the order a real booking would actually flow (Customer → Admin confirms → Professional accepts → Admin/Professional completes) instead of jumping between roles out of order, and re-verifying each table's state in Supabase before moving to the next role's step.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today didn't add new features but was valuable for a different reason: it validated that the last two weeks of individual fixes — the booking-date correction, the notification-audience rework, the profile-photo save fix, the in-app document preview, the Terms & Conditions gate, and the admin city-access assignment — all still hold up correctly when exercised together as one continuous flow rather than as isolated patches. The one open item confirmed again during this pass is that city-access is still assignment-only and not yet enforced anywhere else in the app; happy to pick that up as an actual enforcement feature whenever it's prioritized.

---

### Day 46 — 13 July 2026 (Monday)

**Progress:**
Yesterday's full test pass paid off immediately — it surfaced a long list of concrete, reproducible bugs instead of vague symptoms, and today was spent working through that list one root cause at a time: a keyboard that hid whatever the user was typing on every form, a booking-flow crop tool whose gestures silently did nothing, a field that stole keyboard focus mid-form, a photo picker that refused to open, an OTP input that couldn't be pasted into, a notification feed that never showed a customer their own booking updates, a Windows-only native build failure, a status bar that turned black depending on the device's dark-mode setting, and — the most serious find of the day — a database trigger that had been silently blocking every single booking submission in the app.

**Keyboard covering form fields:** HR reported that on every multi-field form (Career, Partnership, Book a Service, Admin Login, Change PIN), the on-screen keyboard hid whatever field was currently focused, especially the lower fields and the final Submit button. Traced it to `react-native-keyboard-aware-scroll-view`, a dependency last updated in 2020 that predates React Native's New Architecture and doesn't reliably compute scroll offsets under Fabric. Replaced it app-wide with `react-native-keyboard-controller` (actively maintained, New Architecture-compatible), added a `KeyboardProvider` at the app root, and removed several manual scroll-to-field workarounds that were only there to compensate for the old library's unreliability. Since this is a native module, it required a fresh `expo prebuild`/Gradle rebuild rather than a JS-only reload.

**Career/Partnership "already registered" bug:** A test signup with a phone number that should have been rejected instead sailed through validation and only failed at the very last step, after OTP verification, with a raw Postgres error ("duplicate key value violates unique constraint professional_phone_key") shown directly to the user. The pre-submit duplicate check was querying the `workforce` table, but the actual unique constraint lives on `professional` — the table that gates login — so a phone already registered there but missing a `workforce` row slipped past validation entirely. Fixed the check to query `professional` directly, and added a fallback in `createCareer()` that catches a Postgres unique-violation (`code 23505`) and surfaces a friendly "already registered, go to login" message with a working button instead of the raw database error, in case of a race between two near-simultaneous signups.

**Headshot crop tool doing nothing:** The "Crop Your Profile Picture" modal's hint text promised pinch-to-zoom and drag, but neither gesture did anything — only the +/− buttons worked. Root cause: `HeadshotCropModal` renders through React Native's built-in `<Modal>`, which opens a separate native window on Android that doesn't inherit the `GestureHandlerRootView` the rest of the app gets implicitly from the drawer navigator, so `react-native-gesture-handler`'s pinch/pan detectors never received any touches inside it. Fixed by wrapping the modal's own content in its own `GestureHandlerRootView` — a one-line, JS-only fix once the actual cause was found.

**Area field stealing focus, and a phantom permission prompt:** On the Book a Service form, typing in the Area field and then tapping anywhere else would sometimes silently move the keyboard down to the Message box at the bottom of the form instead of closing it. Traced to the Area suggestion dropdown's `keyboardShouldPersistTaps="handled"` setting, which stops the keyboard from auto-dismissing on an outside tap — with no explicit dismiss and the keyboard left "dangling," Android was auto-refocusing the next real text input in the tree to keep the keyboard attached to something. Fixed by explicitly calling `Keyboard.dismiss()` the moment the Area field blurs. Separately, the "Photos" upload button was showing a false "Permission needed" alert even after photo access had already been granted — the code was calling `requestMediaLibraryPermissionsAsync()`, which by default asks for read *and* write access, but Android's modern Photo Picker (which `launchImageLibraryAsync` already uses) needs neither; removing the redundant permission check fixed it entirely.

**OTP input paste and backspace bugs:** Every OTP/PIN screen in the app (5 verification screens, Admin Login, and all 4 PIN rows in Change PIN) hand-rolled the same "one input box per digit" pattern, which had two real bugs: pasting a copied code truncated to a single character because each box capped input at 1 character, and deleting a filled box in the middle of the code required pressing Backspace twice — once to clear it, once more to actually move back — instead of behaving like a normal text field. Rather than patch each of the 7 screens separately, built one shared `OtpInput` component that treats the whole code as one value: a paste of any length now distributes correctly across the remaining boxes, and Backspace on a filled box clears it in one press like it should.

**Notifications: customer visibility, mark-as-read, and a real access-scoping check:** HR asked for the in-app Notifications screen to actually show whatever the device receives as a push, keep view-access properly restricted (only Super Admin sees the full feed), and add a "Mark all as read" option. Investigating why a customer's own "Booking Accepted" push never appeared in their in-app list found that plain customers have no login session at all, so the screen had no way to know "which phone is this device" in order to pull back their personal notification rows — fixed by having `BookingOtp.tsx` remember the device's own phone locally right where it already registers it with OneSignal, and having the Notifications query include that phone's `customer_specific` rows alongside general broadcasts. Re-checked the existing role-based query logic carefully and confirmed it was already correctly scoped (a regular Admin never sees Super-Admin-only rows, Professionals only see leads matching their own city/services), so no change was needed there. Added "Mark all as read" as a per-device feature, since a single notification can be sent to many recipients at once and a shared "read" flag on the row itself would incorrectly mark it read for everyone — the read state is tracked locally per device instead, with an unread-count badge and per-card unread styling.

**Windows-only native build failure:** After adding the keyboard-controller native module, `./gradlew assembleRelease` started failing with `ninja: error: ... Filename longer than 260 characters`. Measured the actual failing path at 360 characters and confirmed mathematically that shortening the project folder alone could never bring it under Windows' 260-character limit — the library's own internal folder structure is already longer than that on its own. Traced the real cause to the Android SDK's bundled CMake 3.22.1 shipping ninja 1.10.2, which predates ninja's long-path support (added in 1.11). Installed CMake 3.31.6 (bundles ninja 1.12.1, with the fix, while staying off CMake 4.x's stricter minimum-version policy that can break other bundled native modules) and pinned `app/build.gradle`'s `externalNativeBuild.cmake.version` to it. Rebuilt cleanly afterward with zero errors.

**Status bar and header sizing:** Every shared header component had a hardcoded `paddingTop` guess for status bar height and a `<StatusBar backgroundColor>` prop that's silently ignored under Android's mandatory edge-to-edge display — fixed by reading the real height from `useSafeAreaInsets()`/`StatusBar.currentHeight` instead. A second, trickier report — the status bar rendering solid black instead of the app's green on the Home screen specifically — turned out to have nothing to do with layout at all: two screenshots of the identical screen, taken in different system dark-mode states, showed black in one and correct green in the other. The app's theme extends `Theme.AppCompat.DayNight` and `app.json` had `userInterfaceStyle: "automatic"`, so the whole UI (which has no actual dark palette designed anywhere) was inheriting whatever theme the device happened to be in. Forced light mode at the native level via `AppCompatDelegate.setDefaultNightMode(MODE_NIGHT_NO)` in `MainApplication.kt` and updated `app.json` to match, making the app's appearance consistent regardless of the device's own setting.

**The customers table bug — every booking was silently failing:** The most serious find of the day: submitting a booking failed at the final OTP step with "new row violates row-level security policy for table customers," on every single booking, for every user. A trigger added in an earlier session (`sync_customer_from_booking`, keeping a `customers` table in sync with every new booking) ran with the *calling* role's own privileges rather than an elevated one; once Row Level Security was enabled on `customers` with no policies — most likely via Supabase's own dashboard prompt to enable RLS on all public tables — the trigger's insert started failing, and since a trigger failure rolls back its entire transaction, the booking itself was never created either. Fixed with a new migration marking the trigger function `SECURITY DEFINER` (with `search_path` pinned, standard practice to avoid the search-path-hijacking risk that comes with that privilege) so it bypasses RLS for its own write regardless of caller, and properly secured `customers` for the first time with an explicit `SELECT`-only policy for the admin tooling that reads it directly.

**"Invalid Date" on Booking History:** Admin's Booking History cards showed the literal text "Invalid Date" instead of a booking's submission date. `fetchBookingData.ts` already formats the raw timestamp into a human-readable string like "13 July 2026, Monday" before handing it to the card component — but `BookingCard.tsx` was then wrapping that already-formatted string in `new Date(...)` a second time, and JavaScript's date parser can't reliably re-parse its own formatted output. Fixed by rendering the already-formatted string directly instead of re-parsing it.

**Learning:**
Several of today's bugs shared the same shape: something that looks like "the feature doesn't work" turns out to be a *side effect from an unrelated part of the stack* rather than a bug in the feature's own code — the keyboard-controller build failure was a Windows toolchain version problem, not anything wrong with the library integration; the black status bar was a system dark-mode setting, not a layout bug; and the customers-table failure was a database permissions change, not anything wrong with the booking form itself. In every one of these, the fix that actually worked only came after ruling out the code that was directly "in the room" and looking one layer further out — the build tool, the OS theme setting, the database policy. The other recurring theme was root-causing before patching: the OTP paste/backspace bug could have been patched seven times in seven files, but building one correct shared component instead means the fix (and any future fix) only has to happen once.

**Skills Gained:**
React Native / Android — diagnosing New-Architecture-era native module incompatibilities (`react-native-keyboard-aware-scroll-view` vs. `react-native-keyboard-controller`), understanding why RN's `<Modal>` opens a separate native window that needs its own `GestureHandlerRootView` on Android, forcing app-wide light mode via `AppCompatDelegate.setDefaultNightMode` · Android build tooling — diagnosing a Windows-specific CMake/ninja long-path failure by measuring actual path lengths rather than guessing, understanding ninja's own version history for long-path support, and pinning a project's CMake version independently of the Android SDK's default · Supabase / Postgres — recognizing that a trigger function without `SECURITY DEFINER` runs with the *caller's* privileges and can be silently broken by an unrelated RLS change on a table it writes to, and the standard fix (`SECURITY DEFINER` plus a pinned `search_path`) · Debugging methodology — treating "same bug, seven copies" as a sign to build one shared, correct component instead of seven local patches; distinguishing a report that *looks* identical (two screenshots of the same screen) but differs in an external variable (system dark mode) neither screenshot showed directly.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The Windows build failure's error message pointed at ninja and a long file path, which could have been "fixed" by just moving the project to a shorter folder — except the math showed that wouldn't have been enough on its own, since the library's own internal folder structure already exceeds the 260-character limit before the project path is even added. Solved by finding the actual mechanism (ninja's own long-path support, tied to its version, bundled by CMake) instead of trying path-shortening tricks that couldn't have worked. (2) The black-status-bar report looked like a straightforward layout bug and got two JS-only fix attempts (adjusting padding, then the height calculation) before either one visibly changed anything — the breakthrough was noticing the two comparison screenshots showed different shuffled service cards, meaning they were two different app sessions, not two views of a static bug, which reframed the question from "why is this element too short" to "what's different between these two sessions" and pointed straight at system dark mode.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's list was long, but almost every item on it came directly out of yesterday's structured test pass rather than from new guesswork — which is exactly what that kind of end-to-end testing is supposed to produce. The customers-table bug is the one I'd flag most urgently: it meant every booking submission in the app was failing at the last step, silently, until today, and it's a good reminder that a Supabase dashboard convenience prompt (enabling RLS broadly) can break a feature with zero code changes on our end. Also want to note the Windows build issue for the record, since it's environment-specific — anyone else building this project on Windows would hit the identical CMake/ninja failure until the same fix is applied on their machine.

---

### Day 47 — 14 July 2026 (Tuesday)

**Progress:**
Today split between two very different kinds of work: closing out the notification system's remaining gaps and a real database security hole, and then a long, hard-won detour into local Android build infrastructure after the project's cloud build quota ran out.

**Notification history — the last gap and its real cause:** A screenshot showed a push notification ("HomeSewa — False Ceiling in Kathmandu") arriving on a device but never appearing on the in-app Notifications screen, despite that screen's audience-filtering logic having shipped just two minutes earlier. Reviewing the code confirmed the client-side logic was already correct; the actual defect was in the database. Connecting directly to the live Supabase project (read-only, via `supabase db query --linked`) showed the `notifications` table had Row Level Security enabled with zero policies attached — almost certainly from the dashboard's "enable RLS on all public tables" convenience prompt, the same mechanism that had already broken the `customers` table earlier this internship. With RLS on and no policy, the anon key's insert from `logNotification()` was failing silently (caught by its own try/catch) on every single push sent, and the read-side query was coming back empty too — so the screen was rendering correctly, but the table backing it had been empty the whole time. Wrote a migration adding the missing `select`/`insert` policies, and found the identical problem independently affecting `lead_rejections` (silently breaking the "reject this lead" flow for professionals) via the same root cause, fixed with a matching migration. Separately, widened the Notifications screen so a Super Admin sees every notification across every audience (customer, professional, admin, public) in one place, rather than only the admin-category subset — matching what "only Super Admin can see all notifications" was actually supposed to mean. Also found and fixed a real bug in `BookingHistory.tsx`: its header back-arrow was wired to a full account logout instead of navigation (every other screen's back arrow calls `router.back()`), and that logout was missing the `authChanged` event emit that the root layout's session gate depends on — so a second back-navigation could still reach protected screens like Update Profile with a blank, logged-out-looking state, since the gate's in-memory session flag never actually cleared.

**Local Android build infrastructure:** With the free-tier cloud build quota exhausted, moved to building locally via `eas build --local`, which only runs on Linux/macOS — not Windows directly. Set up a full WSL2 Ubuntu environment (Java 17, Node via nvm, Android SDK with the exact platform/build-tools/NDK versions the project needs, eas-cli) and then worked through a genuine cascade of environment blockers, each with a distinct root cause: WSL treating the project's Windows-drive git repo as untrusted ("dubious ownership") until explicitly marked safe; a false "every file modified" `git status` caused by Windows and WSL git disagreeing on CRLF/LF line-ending conversion; the WSL2 VM's default memory allocation being too small for a Kotlin/Gradle build on an 8GB machine, fixed via a `.wslconfig` memory cap plus disabling Gradle's parallel workers; a hard CMake version pin in `build.gradle` (3.31.6, chosen specifically for a newer bundled `ninja` with long-path support) that had never actually been installed via `sdkmanager`; and — the most subtle one — Linux's `/tmp` being a RAM-backed `tmpfs` capped at 2.5GB regardless of the machine's real disk space, which every `eas build --local` run was using as its entire scratch/work directory, resolved by redirecting `TMPDIR` to a real disk path.

**A real infrastructure incident, and its recovery:** An attempt to run the long build process as a fully automated background task repeatedly killed the WSL virtual machine mid-write while heavy Gradle/native-build I/O was in flight, which corrupted the underlying ext4 filesystem into an emergency read-only state — compounded by discovering the Windows C: drive itself was down to about 44MB free, the more fundamental cause, since the VM's virtual disk grows dynamically and had nowhere left to grow into. Recovered without any data loss: freed real space first (clearing npm/pip/yarn caches), installed a temporary rescue WSL distro (Debian, since the environment had no second distro available), attached the corrupted disk to it as a raw block device via `wsl --mount --vhd --bare`, and ran `fsck.ext4 -y -f` against it directly, which found and repaired genuine journal and block-accounting corruption. Verified full recovery — write access, and every previously-installed tool (JDK, Android SDK, CMake, Gradle's dependency cache) — before resuming.

**Multi-account keystore migration:** Once local builds were working, the next requirement was building the same live app under a different Expo account, since the original account's monthly quota was spent. Confirmed via `eas credentials` that Android signing keys are portable between EAS projects/accounts as long as the exact same keystore file and passwords are reused (Play Store cares about the app's package name and signing certificate, not which EAS project built it). Used `eas project:transfer` via the Expo dashboard to move the live project's ownership to a second account outright (preserving its project ID, and therefore its existing OTA update channel), and separately walked through linking `app.json` to an entirely different, second project under a third account and uploading the identical keystore there via `eas credentials` → Upload an existing keystore, so that project can also produce a validly-signed build against the same live Play Store listing.

**Learning:**
Today reinforced a pattern that's shown up repeatedly this internship: a bug that looks like a client-side rendering problem is often actually further downstream, and the fastest way to find out is to check the layer the UI can't see — in this case, going straight to the live database with a read-only query rather than re-reading already-correct React Native code. It also produced the most expensive lesson of the internship so far on the infrastructure side: an automated process killing a virtual machine mid-write is a real corruption risk, not just an inconvenience, and a nearly-full system disk can cause failures that look completely unrelated to disk space (a filesystem going read-only, an unrelated distro install failing) until you actually check free space directly instead of assuming the error message points at the real cause.

**Skills Gained:**
Supabase / Postgres — diagnosing a client-side "it's not showing up" bug by connecting directly to the live database with `supabase db query --linked` rather than trusting application code alone, recognizing the specific signature of RLS-enabled-with-no-policy (silent insert failures, empty reads, no errors surfaced to the user) · Linux / WSL2 systems administration — `.wslconfig` memory tuning, Gradle parallelism/worker configuration for constrained environments, diagnosing a `tmpfs`-vs-real-disk space issue via `mount`/`df`, and — the most involved skill of the day — ext4 filesystem recovery: attaching a corrupted virtual disk to a second Linux instance as a raw block device (`wsl --mount --vhd --bare`) and running `fsck.ext4` against it directly to repair journal and block-accounting corruption without data loss · EAS / Expo tooling — the mechanics of Android keystore portability across projects and accounts, `eas project:transfer` for preserving a project's ID and OTA channel during an ownership change, and `eas build:version:set` combined with temporarily disabling `autoIncrement` to pin an exact versionCode for a specific release.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The notification "not showing up" bug had no error anywhere in the application layer to chase, since the push itself was delivering fine — the breakthrough was treating "it's empty" as a database question rather than a UI question and querying Supabase directly, which immediately showed RLS enabled with zero policies and explained both the silent insert failures and the empty reads in one finding. (2) The WSL corruption forced a genuine recovery under real constraints — no second Linux environment available and almost no free disk space to work with. Solved in the correct order: free real disk space first (since nothing else could proceed without it), then stand up a minimal rescue environment specifically to repair the broken one rather than defaulting to a full wipe-and-reinstall, which would have cost significantly more time and lost the whole day's environment setup.

**Message to Mentor:**
The notification and `lead_rejections` RLS gaps are worth flagging clearly: both were silently broken in production before today, with no error visible to any user, and both trace back to the same root cause already seen once before this internship (a Supabase dashboard convenience action enabling RLS broadly with no policies attached) — it may be worth a one-time full audit of every table for this exact pattern rather than continuing to find them one at a time. On the infrastructure side, today cost real time recovering from a corrupted WSL environment, but the recovery itself — diagnosing down to "the Windows disk is nearly full," then repairing rather than reinstalling — is exactly the kind of systems-level debugging I don't get as much practice with day-to-day, and I'd rather have hit and solved it now than have it resurface unexplained later in the project.

---

## Week 9 — 15 July to 21 July 2026 (Wednesday – Tuesday)

---

### Day 48 — 15 July 2026 (Wednesday)

**Progress:**
Today was spent designing and building an entirely new feature end to end: "RoadBlock," an interstitial pop-up banner that HR wants to use for flash offers and announcements, shown to users when they open the app — plus a full admin screen to create and manage it, and several rounds of revision once HR started reviewing it against a real reference (a competitor app's own promo pop-up).

**Design exploration before writing any code:** HR shared a screenshot of a ride-hailing app's promo pop-up (image, countdown, message, CTA button) and asked for something similar for HomeSewa, along with the exact columns HR wanted in the underlying table — banner name, countdown timer, image, message, button text (from a fixed list of ten options plus a free-text "Other"), button link, start/end dates, and who uploaded it. Before writing any Supabase schema, put together five distinct full visual directions as design mockups — a screen-printed ticket-stub look, a torn-newspaper classified clipping, a hand-painted rickshaw-signboard style, a POS service-receipt reframe, and a wedding-invitation card — deliberately built as genuinely different visual languages rather than five recolors of the same layout, so the choice would be a real design decision and not a formality. HR picked the ticket-stub concept.

**Locking the data model with HR before building:** Rather than guess at ambiguous requirements, asked HR four direct yes/no questions before touching the database: whether the countdown is a shared deadline or a per-user timer that starts when each person opens the pop-up (per-user), whether more than one banner can be live at once (no, always just one), whether posting should be restricted to Super Admin like the existing notification-compose screen (no, opened to regular Admins too), and whether the banner needs city/service audience targeting (no, always shown to everyone). Each answer changed the actual schema, so getting them up front avoided a rebuild later.

**Building the feature:** Wrote `supabase/migrations/0008_road_blocks.sql` — a `road_blocks` table with a `button_text` check constraint enforcing HR's exact ten-option list plus "Other," a `button_text_custom` field required only when "Other" is chosen, a per-user `countdown_seconds` duration column instead of a shared deadline timestamp, an `updated_at` trigger, and the same permissive-RLS-plus-app-level-gating pattern the existing `notifications`/`helpbox` tables already use, since this app authenticates admins via custom phone+PIN rather than Supabase Auth. Built `api/roadBlocks.ts` for the CRUD layer, `components/RoadBlockCard.tsx` as the actual pop-up UI, and a full `app/(drawer)/admin/AdminRoadBlock.tsx` screen with a Compose tab (image upload via the existing storage helper, message, a button-text picker, link field, countdown toggle, start/end date pickers, and a live preview using the real card component) and an All Banners tab with computed status pills (Scheduled/Live/Ended/Disabled) and per-row Edit/Enable-Disable actions. Wired it into the admin route stack, added a drawer menu entry visible to any logged-in admin (not gated to Super Admin, per HR's answer), and mounted a global `RoadBlockPopup` component in the app's root layout that fetches whichever banner is currently active and in its date window and displays it everywhere except inside the admin section.

**Redesigning after HR reviewed it against a real app:** HR came back with a specific correction after comparing it to the reference screenshot again: banner image should be square (1080×1080), a short amount of text below it, then the button — and, more specifically, HR didn't want the flash-sale-style countdown board at all, instead wanting the countdown moved onto the close button itself, the way many websites show a "skip in 10 seconds" style countdown before letting a visitor dismiss a pop-up. Rebuilt the card around that: dropped the split-flap countdown board entirely, changed the hero image to a true square aspect ratio, simplified the layout to image → title/message → button, and replaced the static close (×) button with one that shows a live countdown number and is genuinely non-tappable until it reaches zero, at which point it swaps to the real × icon. The `countdown_seconds` column's meaning changed with it — from "how long a flash-sale banner stays live" to "how many seconds the close button stays disabled" — so updated the admin form's countdown section (now defaulting on, at 10 seconds) and its copy to match, and updated the migration's own comments so the schema documents what it actually does now rather than what it used to do.

**Matching the app's real brand instead of an invented palette:** HR flagged that the banner's colors (an orange/marigold ticket-stub palette carried over from the design-exploration phase) didn't look like the rest of HomeSewa. Rather than guess at replacement colors, searched the actual customer-facing screens for every hex value in real use and confirmed HomeSewa has no separate accent/discount color anywhere — the whole UI is teal-on-white (`#295C59`/`#1E4542`/`#3D7A76`, `#1C2B2A` text, `#D6E8E7` borders). Rebuilt the card entirely around that real palette, including giving the CTA button the exact same two-stop teal gradient the drawer's own profile header already uses, so the button reads as part of the same app rather than a separate visual system.

**Smaller correction rounds:** Removed a pair of decorative "ticket notch" circles once they showed up as plain black dots in the admin's live preview — they only look right when their fill color exactly matches whatever is directly behind the card, which held for the dark pop-up backdrop but broke immediately against the light preview background, so removed them rather than chase an exact-match dependency that was never going to be robust. Converted the Button Text selector from a wrapping row of tap-chips into a proper searchable-style dropdown sheet, matching the picker pattern the existing notification screen already uses elsewhere in the admin panel. Resized the close button to 150% of its original size on request. Removed the pop-up's per-device "don't show again" memory entirely after HR asked for it to appear on every single app open rather than only once per device. Built a "Browse app pages" picker for the Button Link field — a searchable list generated at runtime from the app's real service catalog and every genuinely linkable screen (skipping OTP/mid-flow/admin screens that can't work as a cold deep link), so an admin creating a banner can find "Plumbing Repair — Service Details" or "Plumbing Repair — Book Now" instead of having to know or ask for the exact route string.

**A live-database bug from an out-of-order migration:** After the table had already been created against the real Supabase project, adding the `title` column to the local migration file didn't retroactively apply to the live table — publishing a banner failed with a schema-cache error the moment the app tried to write a `title` value the database didn't have a column for. Wrote a short follow-up migration (`0009_road_blocks_add_title.sql`) that adds the column with a temporary default so it can be applied safely even though the table already has rows, then drops the default afterward so the column behaves exactly like the rest of the required fields going forward.

**Learning:**
The four up-front questions to HR (per-user vs. shared countdown, one banner vs. many, who can post, audience targeting) turned out to be the single highest-leverage part of the day — each answer changed an actual column or constraint, and getting them before writing the migration meant zero schema rework later, compared to the alternative of guessing and finding out mid-build. The palette correction was a good reminder that a design can be internally well-crafted and still be wrong for the project if it wasn't actually checked against the codebase's real values — the fix wasn't picking new colors from taste, it was searching the app for the colors that already exist and using those. The migration-ordering bug was the same lesson this internship has surfaced before in different forms: a schema change made only in a local file doesn't exist until it's actually run against the live database, so "already created" tables need an additive follow-up migration, not an edit to the original file.

**Skills Gained:**
React Native — building a self-contained countdown-gated close button (a disabled control that becomes tappable only once an internal timer reaches zero), square-aspect-ratio image layout with `expo-image`'s `contentFit`, a searchable picker modal pattern reused across two different fields (button text, button link) · Supabase / Postgres — a `check` constraint enforcing a fixed option list plus a conditional "custom value required" constraint, a partial index scoped to `where is_active` for the "what's live right now" query, and the correct additive-migration pattern (`add column ... default`, then `drop default`) for safely adding a `not null` column to a table that already has rows · Product/requirements work — converting an ambiguous verbal design request into four concrete yes/no questions before writing schema, and auditing a codebase directly for its real design tokens instead of assuming or inventing a palette.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The countdown feature meant two structurally different things over the course of the day — a flash-sale deadline, then a close-button skip-delay — and the column name `countdown_seconds` stayed the same through that change while its actual behavior did not. Resolved by treating it as a full redesign rather than a patch: removed the old countdown board UI entirely, rebuilt the close button around the new meaning, and rewrote every comment and admin-form label that referenced the old behavior so the code and the schema comments describe what the column does today, not what it used to do. (2) Hit a genuine "column doesn't exist" production error because the live table had been created before `title` was added to the migration file locally. Rather than edit the already-run migration (which wouldn't retroactively apply), wrote a small additive follow-up migration instead, which is the safe pattern for a table that may already hold real rows.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's build went through more revision rounds than most single-day features in this log, but each round was a direct, specific correction from HR reviewing the actual working screen against a real reference rather than abstract feedback — square image, countdown on the close button, real brand colors, dropdown instead of chips, always show on launch — which made every fix fast and unambiguous to apply. The one thing I'd flag for next time: locking the visual direction and the exact countdown behavior with a reference screenshot before the first build, the way the four schema questions were locked before the first migration, would likely have saved one of today's larger revision passes.

---

### Day 49 — 16 July 2026 (Thursday)

**Progress:**
Today was a mix of one substantial feature (audience targeting for yesterday's RoadBlock banner), a round of small HR-requested polish across three separate admin screens, a documentation exercise that ended up catching a real gap in my own understanding rather than the code, and two independent data-consistency bugs that surfaced while chasing a single reported typo.

**RoadBlock audience targeting:** HR handed over a hand-drawn wireframe for a three-section targeting flow — City (one/many/all), User Type (Public/Customer/Workforce/Admin, one/many/all), and a Profession picker that only appears when Workforce is selected — with the explicit example "Kathmandu + Workforce + Plumber should only reach Kathmandu plumbers." Added `target_cities`/`target_roles`/`target_professions` columns to `road_blocks` (migration `0010_road_blocks_targeting.sql`), following the same null/empty-array-means-"all" convention `admin.allowed_cities` already established. The harder part was the client side: `RoadBlockPopup.tsx` has no login session to key off for a Public/Customer viewer, so it resolves who's actually using the device — checking `adminPhone` against the `workforce` table for a Workforce match (with their real `preferred_city`/`services`), falling back to Admin if `adminPhone` exists without a workforce row, then `customerPhone` for Customer, else Public — and `fetchActiveRoadBlock` now matches a banner's targeting against that resolved viewer before showing it. Extracted the duplicated `MultiSelectDropdown` component out of `AdminNotifications.tsx` into its own shared file so the new targeting UI in `AdminRoadBlock.tsx` didn't need a second copy of the same ~110 lines.

**Documenting the lead-payment flow — and a gap in my own diagram, not the code:** Built a flowchart of the pay-to-unlock-a-lead flow to hand to HR. HR came back having drawn their own version by hand, with one box circled: an "Admin approves" step between a booking being created and professionals being notified, which my diagram had skipped straight past. Before touching any code, traced the actual booking-creation and confirmation logic to check which of us was right — and the gate already existed: `BookingOtp.tsx` creates every booking as `Status: 'Pending Confirmation'`, and only `BookingDetails_2.tsx`'s "Confirm & Notify Professionals" action (`handleConfirmBooking`) flips it to `New / Open` and fires the actual push via `pushAreaProfessionals`. So the code was already correct — the gap was entirely in the diagram I'd drawn, not a missing feature, and the fix was correcting my own documentation rather than the app.

**Small polish requests across admin screens:** Removed "About Us" and "Contact" from the customer drawer menu and renamed "Admin Login" to "Login" (`CustomDrawer.tsx`), per an annotated screenshot. Reworked `BookingHistory.tsx`'s header so the search control is a small icon next to the shield/calendar icons that expands into a full-width inline search bar on tap instead of always occupying its own row, and converted the row of filter-status pills into a single dropdown. Decluttered `UserManagement.tsx`'s list rows on HR's request — professionals no longer show their profession/city line or a Disable button inline, and customers no longer show a Block button inline — both actions already existed in the detail modal that opens on tap, so nothing was actually lost, it just moved to the "populated window" instead of cluttering every row in a long list.

**Two data-consistency bugs found while chasing one reported typo:** HR flagged a single professional showing "Painter" instead of "Painting" as their service. Tracing it back showed the actual root cause: the current signup form (`Career.tsx`) writes correct service-catalog names, but roughly 180 legacy migrated `workforce` rows were seeded using the old job-title vocabulary ("Painter," "Electrician," "Mason") instead — meaning any of those professionals were invisible to real bookings/notifications/filters for their actual service, since every matching query compares against the catalog name, not the job title. Wrote a normalization migration (`0011_normalize_legacy_service_names.sql`) mapping all ~37 legacy names to their catalog equivalents. While building the mapping I found a second, independent bug: `src/data/Data.ts`'s own service list had "Lift/ Elevator Repair" (missing a space) while the actual catalog used by the signup form had "Lift / Elevator Repair" — a one-character mismatch that would have silently broken matching for that one service alone, and which I'd already unknowingly carried into the RoadBlock profession picker built earlier today. Fixed the source list and the migration together so both point at the one true spelling.

**A pricing change that turned out to touch three files, not one:** Asked to change the lead-unlock fee from NPR 49 to NPR 99. The client-facing constant (`src/constants/leadFee.ts`) was the easy part, but the fee turned out to also be hardcoded separately inside two different Supabase Edge Functions — `khalti-initiate-lead-payment` (the amount actually charged at checkout) and `khalti-confirm-lead-unlock` (the amount checked afterward to validate the payment) — meaning updating only one would have made every future payment fail with an amount mismatch. Updated all three, and moved both Edge Functions onto a shared `LEAD_FEE_PAISA` environment variable so the real, enforced fee can be changed from the Supabase dashboard alone going forward, without a redeploy or a repeat of today's three-file hunt.

**Status bar investigation:** Asked why a screenshot showed no phone status bar (clock/battery) above the app header. Traced it to Android 15's forced edge-to-edge behavior, which makes `android:statusBarColor` a no-op unless a project explicitly opts out — and confirmed the correct opt-out (`android:windowOptOutEdgeToEdgeEnforcement`) was already committed in a previous session's fix. The real explanation was in `build.log`: the last local EAS build had failed outright from a corrupted Gradle transforms cache, so no build with that fix had actually been produced yet. No code change was needed here either — just confirmation that the fix already exists and is waiting on a clean successful build to ship.

**Learning:**
Two moments today were really the same lesson from opposite directions: when HR's hand-drawn diagram disagreed with mine, the instinct to actually go read the booking-creation and confirmation code before changing anything — rather than assume the diagram was right and start "fixing" a feature that already worked — saved a wasted change. And when a single reported typo turned into two unrelated bugs and a three-file pricing hunt, the common thread was that a value repeated in more than one place is a bug waiting to happen the moment only one copy gets updated — the fix in each case wasn't just correcting the value, it was finding every other place it was silently duplicated first.

**Skills Gained:**
Supabase / Postgres — writing a data-normalization migration that rewrites only the matching elements inside an array column (`array_agg` over `unnest` with a `case` mapping), scoped with an `&&` overlap check so it only touches rows that actually need it and stays safe to re-run · React Native — resolving an anonymous device's effective identity client-side (Workforce vs Admin vs Customer vs Public) from stored session keys and a fallback table lookup, to drive audience-matching logic with no server-side session to key on · Cross-runtime configuration — using a Supabase Edge Function secret as the single authoritative, no-redeploy control point for a value (a payment amount) that also needs a separate, manually-synced display copy on the client, since a Deno function can't import from the Expo app directly · Debugging discipline — verifying a claimed gap against the actual code before writing a fix, and treating a value found hardcoded in one place as a reason to search for every other copy of it rather than stopping at the first one found.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The lead-fee change looked like a one-line constant edit and was actually a three-file, two-runtime change (client display, checkout-initiation amount, payment-validation amount) — updating only the obvious client constant would have shipped a UI that displayed NPR 99 while Khalti still charged and validated NPR 49. Resolved by grepping for every existing reference to the old amount before editing anything, finding all three locations up front instead of discovering the second Edge Function only after payments started failing. (2) The "Painter" report initially looked like a simple typo fix. Resolved by tracing where the value actually came from (a legacy migrated row using an old vocabulary) rather than patching the one visible instance, which surfaced the full list of ~37 similarly-affected legacy rows and a second, unrelated spelling bug in the same data file.

**Message to Mentor:**
The most useful moment today wasn't a build, it was a check that didn't lead to a code change — reading the actual booking/confirmation code before "fixing" the gap HR circled in their diagram, and finding the feature already worked correctly. I'd rather report "already correct, here's the proof" than quietly patch something that didn't need it. Separately, the lead-fee change is worth flagging on your side too: the real, enforced amount is now controlled by a single `LEAD_FEE_PAISA` secret shared by both Khalti Edge Functions, so any future price change should be made there first, with the client's display constant updated to match by hand afterward.

### Day 50 — 17 July 2026 (Friday)

**Progress:**
Asked to do a full, unscoped pass over the whole app looking for bugs — not a specific feature request, just "is anything broken." That single ask led to the day's real work: a security-first audit that surfaced the most serious payment bug found so far this internship, a chain of three separate, independently-discovered bugs stacked on top of each other in the PIN-change flow, and a recurring UI annoyance that turned out to be a one-line mounting mistake.

**The initial audit — static checks, then a search for what they can't catch:** Started with the boring, reliable layer first: a full `tsc --noEmit` (clean) and `expo lint` pass (a handful of pre-existing warnings, nothing new). Those pass on code that compiles and lints cleanly but can still be badly wrong, so followed up by grepping the repo for anything a compiler can't flag — and found several: a live Khalti payment secret key, a Sparrow SMS token, and a OneSignal REST API key all sitting in `.env` under an `EXPO_PUBLIC_` prefix, meaning Expo bundles all three straight into the shipped app, extractable by anyone who inspects the APK or the network traffic. Also found the Android signing keystore, its passwords, and a base64-encoded Firebase config committed into git history from the project's very first commit, despite `.gitignore` correctly excluding all three today — they'd been added before the ignore rule existed.

**The lead-payment flow was completely broken — and, once checked against the live database, worse than it first looked:** `LeadPayment.tsx` (the "pay to view a customer's contact" screen professionals use) was calling Khalti directly from the client with the exposed secret key above, then writing the unlock straight into a `lead_unlocks` table itself. A properly-built pair of Edge Functions already existed for exactly this (`khalti-initiate-lead-payment`, `khalti-confirm-lead-unlock`, doing the same Khalti call and the same write, but server-side with a service-role key) — they'd simply never been wired into the actual screen. Rewrote `api/khalti.ts`, `api/leadUnlocks.ts`, and `LeadPayment.tsx` to call those two functions instead of touching Khalti or the table directly. While confirming the fix against the live project (`supabase db query --linked`, read-only), found the real, more serious issue underneath: a policy named `lead_unlocks_insert` on the live table — `INSERT`, role `public`, `with_check: true` — that let anyone with the app's own public anon key insert a fake unlock row for any booking, any phone, any amount, with zero payment check, and that policy didn't exist in any migration file in the repo, meaning the migration comments claiming this was already blocked had never actually been true on the live database. Dropped the policy, then proved the fix three ways directly against the live API: the Edge Function still works (service role bypasses RLS), a normal read still works, and a forged direct insert now gets rejected with a Postgres RLS violation.

**No permission to set the one thing the fix actually needed — and a different way to get there:** Both Edge Functions need the real Khalti secret key server-side, but neither the Supabase CLI nor the dashboard would accept a new secret on this project ("account does not have the necessary privileges") — a genuine account/plan restriction, not a mistake on my end, confirmed by testing it with a second, throwaway secret name that failed identically. Direct SQL access to the live database via `supabase db query --linked` did work, though, which opened a different path: Supabase Vault, an encrypted secrets store inside Postgres. Stored the Khalti key there, then wrote a small `SECURITY DEFINER` SQL function (`get_vault_secret`) that only `service_role` can execute — verified that grant explicitly (`anon`/`authenticated` both confirmed unable to call it) — and had both Edge Functions read the key through it instead of an environment variable. Deployed both functions and proved the whole chain live: a real `curl` call to `khalti-initiate-lead-payment` returned a genuine Khalti `pidx` and payment URL, and the confirm function correctly reported the transaction as still pending.

**A second built-but-never-wired-up Edge Function, and the same "check the live account, not the code" habit paying off again:** A related, older bug: `AdminChangePassword.tsx`'s "Change PIN" screen compared and wrote PINs in plaintext directly against the `admin`/`professional` tables, even though a proper `set-pin` Edge Function already existed to hash PINs with bcrypt server-side — never called from anywhere. Rewired the screen to call `send-otp` (for the reset-by-SMS path) and `set-pin` (for both the "prove you know the old PIN" and the actual update), removing the plaintext comparison and write entirely. Deploying `set-pin` for the first time immediately failed with a bare `BOOT_ERROR` — isolated the cause with a minimal throwaway function importing nothing but the PIN-hashing library, which confirmed `bcryptjs` (in both its `esm.sh` and `npm:` specifier forms) simply doesn't boot on this project's Edge Runtime. Swapped to `bcrypt-ts`, a pure-TypeScript implementation, and verified it wasn't just "booting" but actually producing hashes the rest of the system already trusts: generated a real bcrypt hash with Postgres's own `crypt()`/`gen_salt('bf')` (the exact function the original PIN-hashing migration used) and confirmed `bcrypt-ts` correctly matched the right PIN and rejected the wrong one against it, before ever touching a real account.

**Real-device testing surfaced two more bugs the audit alone hadn't caught:** After the fix shipped, a live test on-device came back "Current PIN is incorrect" for what should have been a valid PIN. Rather than assume user error, checked the actual account directly: generated a real Postgres bcrypt hash for a known test value, confirmed `bcrypt-ts` reads it correctly (ruling out the library), then found the specific account's `pin_hash` column no longer matched its own current plaintext `pin` — stale, because the *old*, now-replaced Change PIN screen used to write the new PIN straight to `pin` and never touched `pin_hash` at all. Fixed the one drifted account, then closed the gap for good with a database trigger (`sync_pin_hash`) on both `admin` and `professional` that recomputes `pin_hash` automatically any time `pin` is inserted or changed — tested against a disposable test row on both the insert and update path before trusting it, and in the process caught the same class of gap waiting in `UserManagement.tsx`'s "Add Admin" flow, which creates new admins with a `pin` and no `pin_hash` at all. A second on-device report then landed — "it changes the pin but it doesn't change in Supabase" — which traced to a genuinely different bug in the same function: `set-pin`'s update statement only ever wrote `pin_hash`, never the plaintext `pin` column that the real login screen (`AdminLogin.tsx`) actually authenticates against, so a "successful" PIN change never changed what the user could actually log in with. Fixed the update to set both columns together, verified with a full round-trip on a disposable test account (change PIN, confirm the new plaintext value is readable and matches the hash), and manually repaired the one real account that had been caught in the gap during testing.

**A recurring popup, and a one-line mounting mistake:** Separately reported: a promotional banner popup was reappearing every time the user moved between pages, instead of once per app session as intended. The banner component (`RoadBlockPopup`) was being conditionally *mounted* in the root layout based on whether the current route was inside the admin section — and since that condition is recalculated on every navigation, crossing in or out of the admin area was unmounting and remounting the whole component, re-running its fetch-and-show logic from scratch each time. Fixed by keeping the component permanently mounted for the app's whole lifetime and passing the admin-area check in as a prop instead, with the component tracking internally (via a ref) whether it's already shown the banner once this session — it now shows at most once, regardless of how much navigation happens afterward, confirmed on-device.

**Learning:**
Today's biggest lesson was one this internship keeps circling back to from a new angle each time: a migration file's comment describing what it's *supposed* to do is not proof of what the live database *actually* does, and the only way to know for certain is to query the live system directly — that's what turned "payment succeeds but the unlock write fails" into the much more serious real finding (an unauthenticated free-unlock hole that predated anything I touched). The PIN bugs added a second, related lesson: fixing a bug that's been broken for a while can surface *earlier* bugs that were quietly compensating for it or hiding behind it, and each one is worth root-causing on its own rather than assuming the first fix was the whole story.

**Skills Gained:**
Supabase / Postgres — using Supabase Vault plus a `SECURITY DEFINER` RPC as a secrets-storage alternative when the platform's own secrets API isn't available, and explicitly verifying role grants (`anon`/`authenticated` denied, `service_role` allowed) rather than assuming a `revoke`/`grant` statement did what it says; writing a `before insert or update` trigger to keep a derived column (`pin_hash`) permanently in sync with its source (`pin`) regardless of which code path writes it; diagnosing a `gen_salt(unknown) does not exist` error down to a trigger function's `search_path` excluding the schema an extension's functions actually live in (`extensions`, not `public`) · Deno / Edge Functions — isolating a bare `BOOT_ERROR` with a minimal single-purpose test function rather than guessing at the cause inside a larger file, and knowing to distrust an npm package's advertised Deno-compatibility until it's actually been booted on the target runtime · React Native — the difference between conditionally *rendering* a component's output and conditionally *mounting* the component itself, and why the latter silently resets all of a component's internal state and re-runs its mount-time effects every time the condition flips.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Neither the Supabase CLI nor the dashboard would accept the one thing both Khalti fixes actually depended on — a new Function secret — with no path to escalate the account's own privileges from inside this session. Resolved by finding a different, already-available privilege (direct SQL access to the live project) that could reach the same outcome through Supabase Vault instead, rather than treating the blocked path as a dead end. (2) The PIN-change bug looked "fixed" three separate times before it actually was — first the plaintext comparison, then a stale-hash account caught by on-device testing, then a completely different bug (the plaintext column never being updated) caught by a second round of on-device testing. Resolved by treating each new symptom as its own investigation rather than assuming the earlier fix must have been incomplete in the same way, which is what actually surfaced that they were three unrelated bugs stacked in the same flow, not one bug needing a second attempt.

**Message to Mentor:**
The `lead_unlocks` finding is the one I'd flag most urgently: it means the pay-to-unlock-a-contact feature was, until today, forgeable for free by anyone with the app's own public key, with no payment required at all — worth confirming no bookings were actually exploited this way while it was open. Everything today was verified directly against the live project before being called done (real Khalti API calls, real SQL consistency checks, a real forged-insert attempt confirmed rejected) rather than just confirmed to compile, which is also how the second and third PIN bugs got caught — each one only showed up because a claim was actually tested against the live account instead of trusted on the strength of the code looking right.

---

### Day 51 — 18 July 2026 (Saturday)

**Progress:**
A direct continuation of yesterday's audit turned into the single largest security pass of the internship so far — closing every remaining plaintext-credential and unauthenticated-write gap the previous session's `lead_unlocks`/PIN findings had only partially covered, discovering an entire class of "built but never deployed" bugs underneath them, and finishing the day by designing and shipping a real login-session system from scratch after proving the earlier fixes still had a hole in them.

**Picking up the security audit — closing the auth gaps yesterday's pass had flagged but not yet fixed:** `AdminLogin.tsx` was still comparing plaintext PINs against the `admin`/`professional` tables client-side, even though those tables are open to the anon key for every other column — meaning anyone who extracted the public key from the app bundle could `select phone, pin` directly and dump every account's login PIN with no login required at all. Rewired the screen to call the already-built-but-unused `admin-login` Edge Function (bcrypt against `pin_hash`, rate-limited, server-side only) instead. The same pattern repeated for the Career/professional signup flow (`PostApiCareer.ts` was inserting into `professional` directly with the anon key, which the table's own RLS had already been separately locked to block, silently breaking every new application) and, most seriously, for five separate OTP "verification" screens — Booking, Helpbox, Career, Partnership, and Work-Completion — which were all generating their own 4-digit code client-side, texting it via a Sparrow token embedded in the app, and comparing the entered code against local component state. That's not verification at all: the code being checked against lives on the same device that's trying to pass the check. Rewired all five to the existing `send-otp`/`verify-otp` Edge Functions, which store a hashed code server-side with a 5-minute expiry and a 5-attempt cap.

**A quieter but much bigger discovery underneath all of it: half the Edge Functions the app now depended on had never actually been deployed.** After wiring five screens and a login flow to call `verify-otp`, `admin-login`, and `create-professional-login`, ran `supabase functions list` against the live project as a sanity check before declaring any of it done — and found only 4 of the roughly 9 functions referenced by the code were actually `ACTIVE` on Supabase. The rest existed as files in the repo, correctly written, and had simply never been pushed. Every fix made up to that point would have failed silently in production despite compiling and looking correct. Deployed the missing functions directly (`supabase functions deploy <name>`, no Docker required, confirmed working) and made a point of re-running `supabase functions list` after every subsequent Edge Function change for the rest of the day, rather than trusting that writing a file was the same as shipping it.

**Removing the SMS and push-notification secrets from the client bundle:** `EXPO_PUBLIC_SPARROW_TOKEN` and `EXPO_PUBLIC_ONESIGNAL_REST_API_KEY` were both shipping straight into the compiled app — anyone extracting them could send arbitrary SMS on the project's account or push arbitrary notifications to every real user. Found a `send-sms` Edge Function already built for exactly this and never wired up (same pattern as the day's earlier finds), fixed it to read the Sparrow token from Supabase Vault instead of an unset Function secret, and built a new `send-notification` proxy that forwards the client's existing OneSignal payload server-side with the real key injected there instead. Rewired every remaining client call site (`CareerOTP.tsx`, `ProfessionalVerification.tsx`'s approval/rejection SMS, `BookingDetails_1.tsx`'s acceptance SMS, and all ten-plus functions in `api/notifications.ts`) to go through the two proxies, then confirmed live that SMS and push both still worked exactly as before.

**Locking down the PIN columns at the database level, not just the app layer:** fixing `AdminLogin.tsx` closed the exploitation path through the app's own UI, but `admin`/`professional`'s `pin`/`pin_hash` columns were still directly readable and writable by the anon key via a raw REST call, entirely bypassing the app. Rather than move the whole table behind Edge Functions (a much bigger change touching ~15 call sites), used Postgres column-level `revoke` — narrower, and leaves every other column exactly as accessible as before. That meant the three remaining client call sites that still touched `pin` directly (the professional-approval PIN reveal, and both branches of "Add Admin") needed their own small Edge Functions (`approve-professional`, `admin-create`) instead.

**The same lockdown for verification status — and a worse bypass hiding directly underneath it:** while scoping the equivalent fix for `workforce.profile_status` and `professional.status`, found that `UserManagement.tsx`'s Active/Inactive toggle writes both columns together — and `professional.status` is the exact column that gates real login. Anyone could have PATCHed their own `professional.status` to `'Active'` directly via raw REST and logged in for real, since they'd already know their own PIN from signing up — a full verification bypass, not just a fake-notification nuisance. Closed both columns the same way (column-level `revoke` plus a DB default so new applications still land as "Waiting for Verification" without the client needing to set it), and built `reject-professional`/`toggle-professional-status` to cover the two remaining legitimate write paths.

**Hitting a real architectural wall on `booking`, and refusing to fake a fix:** the same approach doesn't work for `booking` — the admin app genuinely needs to read the same customer PII (name, phone, address) an attacker would extract, and this project has no real login session to tell the two apart. Designed a DB-level status-transition guard as a partial mitigation, then checked every actual `claimBooking`/`updateBookingStatus` call site before writing it and found `BookingDetails_2.tsx`'s own admin dropdown already allows flipping any booking to any status from any prior status as an intentional manual override — meaning a "guard" would either break that existing feature or let through the exact same unrestricted update an attacker would make, doing nothing real either way. Shipped only the one genuinely safe fix available (`Book.tsx`'s daily-limit check was requesting every column on a `head: true` count query that never returns row data anyway) and said plainly that the actual PII exposure stays open until there's a real session to check against, rather than shipping something that looked like a fix and wasn't.

**A second pass over the Security and Performance Advisor lists, once real findings started surfacing beyond RLS:** revoked public `EXECUTE` on `sync_customer_from_booking`, a `SECURITY DEFINER` trigger function that doesn't need it (trigger functions fire via the trigger definition itself, not a role-level function call, so this only closed an unnecessary direct-RPC surface with zero effect on the booking trigger it's meant for). Found the `uploads` storage bucket had a public `SELECT` policy allowing anyone to *list* every file in it — confirmed the app only ever calls `getPublicUrl()` (a client-side string builder with no RLS check at all, since the bucket's own public-CDN path bypasses policies entirely for known-path downloads) before recommending its removal, so deleting the listing policy cost nothing functionally. Also found and removed two sets of genuinely duplicate RLS policies (`lead_unlocks`, `workforce`) flagged under Performance Advisor rather than Security Advisor — same role, same condition, same effect, just doubling the policy evaluation cost on every query for no reason.

**A full-project bug sweep, run the same way as the initial audit — automated checks first, then a background agent over everything untouched:** `tsc`/`lint` came back clean, and a fresh review of every Edge Function written today caught a real, separate bug: several of them fired an SMS send or a database insert without `await`-ing it immediately before returning success. Edge/serverless runtimes aren't guaranteed to keep a promise alive once the response is sent — worst case, `approve-professional`'s confirmation SMS (a newly-approved professional's *only* way to learn their login PIN) could silently never go out. Fixed all four instances to `await` properly. Separately, a background sweep of every screen untouched by the day's security work turned up a genuine, months-old data-loss bug unrelated to any of it: `Partnership.tsx` uploads company photos and registration certificates and collects a services-offered list, but `PostApiPartnership.ts`'s insert never read any of those three fields — every partnership application ever submitted had silently lost its attachments and services, uploaded to storage and then never referenced, with the admin review screen rendering those sections empty for every applicant since before this session started. Fixed the mapping, including a shape mismatch (`{url}`-wrapped objects vs. plain strings) the admin screen's parser didn't expect, and a `|| null` on the employee-count field that would have silently dropped a genuine `0`.

**The big one: proving the day's earlier Edge Function fixes still had a hole, and building a real session to close it.** Revisiting `admin-create`/`approve-professional`/`reject-professional`/`toggle-professional-status` after they'd shipped surfaced an uncomfortable fact: none of them checked who was calling them. Moving a write behind an Edge Function only adds security if that function can tell a real admin from an attacker — these couldn't, so anyone holding the anon key could still call them directly and approve/reject/promote at will, just through a different door than the one already closed. Rather than patch that in isolation, designed a full session-token system: a new `admin_sessions` table, a shared `verifySession()` helper for Edge Functions to check, `admin-login` issuing an opaque 30-day token on success, a real `logout` function that revokes it server-side (not just clearing local state), and `set-pin` invalidating every other session on a PIN change the same way changing a password would. Mapped every existing `AsyncStorage` admin-session read across the whole app first (roughly 30 sites) before writing any of it, specifically to confirm the fix could be purely additive — the new token layers underneath the app's existing UI-gating logic rather than replacing it, so only the four vulnerable functions and their exact four call sites needed to change. Confirmed each function's actual required privilege level from the UI itself (super-admin-only vs. any-admin) rather than assuming, since guessing wrong in either direction would have been its own new bug.

**Learning:**
The day's clearest recurring lesson was the same one from yesterday, sharpened: a fix that "looks" secure — the right server-side function, the right database write — isn't actually secure until you ask exactly what mechanism stops an unauthorized caller from reaching it, and answer with something more specific than "it's an Edge Function now." Column-level `revoke` and hashed-PIN comparison both hold up under that question because they remove the sensitive value from the client's reach entirely; a bare Edge Function with no caller check doesn't, and the only way that gap surfaced was going back and asking the question a second time after the first fix already shipped. The `booking` wall taught the inverse lesson: sometimes the honest answer to "can this be fixed" is "not without a bigger prerequisite," and saying so plainly is worth more than shipping a guard that only looks like protection.

**Skills Gained:**
Supabase / Postgres — column-level `grant`/`revoke` as a narrower alternative to full RLS policy rewrites when only specific columns (not whole rows) need locking down, and confirming it holds up correctly for both direct-column and `select(*)`-style queries; designing an opaque DB-backed session table as the revocable alternative to a stateless signed token, specifically because logout and PIN-change both need to actually invalidate a session server-side, not just wait out an expiry · Deno / Edge Functions — the specific failure mode of an un-awaited promise racing the runtime tearing down its isolate after a `Response` is returned, and why "fire and forget" patterns that are safe in a long-lived client process are not automatically safe in a serverless one · Security review methodology — auditing not just "is this table locked down" but "does the thing that replaced the open table access actually verify the caller," which is a different and easy-to-skip question; mapping every existing usage of a piece of state before designing its replacement, specifically to keep a large security change additive instead of a risky wholesale rewrite.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Fixing five OTP screens and a login flow correctly still didn't make any of them work, because the Edge Functions they called had never been deployed — a gap invisible to `tsc`, lint, or a code review, only found by directly querying the live project's function list. Resolved by making that query a standard step after every Edge Function change for the rest of the day, not just a one-time check. (2) The session-token design touched roughly 30 existing places that read admin state across the app, which risked becoming a much larger, riskier rewrite than the actual security fix required. Resolved by mapping all 30 first and confirming the new token could sit underneath the existing UI-gating logic as a pure addition — only the four vulnerable functions and their four call sites needed to change, and the other ~26 sites were correctly left untouched.

**Message to Mentor:**
The most important finding to flag is the one that came from re-checking my own work rather than a new area: the four Edge Functions built earlier in the day to close the PIN/status exposure had no caller authentication at all, meaning the underlying gap was still open through a different door until the session-token system shipped at the end of the day. I'd rather report that sequence honestly — found it, proved it, fixed it properly — than only mention the final state. Also worth flagging directly: `booking`'s customer PII is still openly readable by the anon key, and I don't think it can be closed correctly without this app having a real login session to check against, which the new `admin_sessions` table and `verifySession()` helper now make possible as a next step, but haven't yet been applied to.
