### Day 60 — 27 July 2026 (Monday)

**Progress:**
A full project-wide bug and security audit, followed by fixing and deploying every real finding the same day rather than just writing them up.

**Running the audit itself:** started with the baseline static checks (`tsc --noEmit` clean, `expo lint` at 0 errors/98 warnings, same as every prior audit day), then split the real review into four parallel deep-dive passes — auth/OTP/session flows, the booking/payment lifecycle, Supabase RLS and Edge Function security across all 29 migrations, and the customer-facing features shipped in the last two weeks — each briefed to report only concrete, triggerable bugs with a file, line, and failure scenario, not lint-adjacent complaints. Verified every high-severity claim myself against the live code before reporting anything as fact: read the actual migration files, traced the exact `isBroadBroadcast` logic in `send-notification`, and confirmed via `git log` that a handful of narrative bug-fixes described in this very report's earlier entries (a Realtime chat dedupe fix, a shared favorites context, a rating-average `rater_role` filter) don't correspond to anything actually in the codebase — `Chat.tsx` is polling-based, no shared favorites context exists, and there's no rating-average computation anywhere. Reported that discrepancy plainly rather than letting a fabricated-sounding narrative stand unchallenged.

**What the audit found, ranked by what mattered most:** `set-pin`'s PIN-change mode had zero brute-force protection at all — no lockout check, no failed-attempt counter, unlike `admin-login`'s otherwise-identical PIN check — a real account-takeover path for anyone who knew a victim's phone number. Every failed-login and OTP-attempt counter across `admin-login`, `verify-otp`, and `set-pin` was a read-then-write race, not an atomic increment, so parallel guesses could bypass the 5-attempt lockout entirely. `booking_messages` and `booking_ratings` shipped with no RLS at all, which meant more than just open reads — anyone holding the anon key could insert a chat message impersonating a real professional's phone, or fabricate a rating attributed to any number. `send-notification`'s broadcast gate had a real gap letting a `role='admin'` filter reach every admin device with no login. `send-sms` had no rate limit, making it a free SMS-bombing relay. `WorkCompletionOTP.tsx` wrote its "Completed" status unconditionally instead of through `claimBooking`'s guard, and consumed the completion OTP before the photo upload/status write — the riskiest step — happened, so a transient failure there locked the professional out and forced the customer to read out a brand-new code. `notifyUsers()` wasn't isolated in its own try/catch, so a failure there after a successful accept produced a false error and a wrongly-worded retry message. And `deal_amount`/`deal_note` — meant to be internal-only — were readable by anyone with the anon key, since `booking` carries no RLS.

**Fixing and deploying all of it, not just listing it:** wrote four new migrations (atomic Postgres RPCs for the attempt counters, an SMS rate-limit log table, RLS policies on the chat/ratings tables that require the row's phone fields to match the booking's actual customer/professional phone, and a column-level revoke on `deal_amount`/`deal_note`), added a new session-checked `get-deal-terms` Edge Function so the revoked columns still reach the one screen that legitimately needs them, and updated six existing Edge Functions plus the client-side booking-accept, work-completion, and completion-photo code. Confirmed with `supabase db push --dry-run` that only the four new migrations would apply before pushing for real, then redeployed all six affected functions and re-ran `migration list`/`functions list` to confirm the remote project matched local exactly — this was after explicitly checking with HR-side sign-off before touching the live production project, since deploying migrations and functions is a shared-system change, not a local one.

**Verifying nothing broke, not just assuming it didn't:** re-ran `tsc`/lint clean after every change, then specifically traced the highest-risk changes against real call sites rather than trusting the logic in isolation — confirmed `Chat.tsx` and `RateProfessional.tsx` already send exactly the phone values the new RLS policies require (no formatting mismatch), grepped every anonymous notification caller to confirm none of them use the link/button fields the new stripping logic removes, and confirmed the one other direct `booking` table query in the codebase explicitly selects columns rather than `select('*')`, so it's unaffected by the new column-level revoke.

**Learning:**
The clearest thread today was that "already known and accepted" isn't the same as "actually fine" — several of the worst findings (the `set-pin` brute-force gap, the chat/ratings RLS gap) were sitting in code whose own comments explained the historical reasoning for why they were built anon-open, and that reasoning was still valid, but nobody had gone back to ask whether a narrower, still-compatible fix existed once the requirement was "close the write-side abuse, not the whole read model." The second lesson was about verification discipline in the other direction: after making a real batch of security fixes, the instinct is to call it done once static checks pass, but the fixes that touch *behavior* (RLS matching, field stripping, reordered async flows) need their actual callers traced, not just their own logic reviewed — which is exactly what caught that none of today's changes would break a real, currently-working flow before saying so with confidence.

**Skills Gained:**
Postgres — writing atomic increment/lockout logic as a `security definer` RPC function instead of a client-side read-then-write, and using `WITH CHECK` subqueries against a related table to constrain inserts on a schema with no `auth.uid()` to key policies on · Supabase Edge Functions — the `supabase db push --dry-run` / `migration list` / `functions list` verification loop for confirming a production deploy matches local exactly, and treating "deploy to the live project" as a shared-system action requiring a deliberate go-ahead rather than folding it silently into a fix · Security engineering — recognizing that closing an abuse vector on an intentionally-open table doesn't require adding the auth model that was deliberately left out, only constraining the specific fields an attacker could forge · Regression verification — tracing a security fix's actual call sites (not just its own code) to confirm real, currently-working flows still satisfy a newly-added constraint before claiming nothing broke.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Fully closing the `send-notification` role='admin' broadcast gap would have required rebuilding every legitimate anonymous caller around a fixed server-side template — a much larger refactor than a single review pass justified. Resolved by implementing the bounded, real fix available today (stripping every clickable/link field from unauthenticated broadcasts) and stating plainly that this mitigates rather than fully closes the gap, instead of either skipping it or overstating the fix. (2) The chat/ratings RLS fix carried a real risk of silently breaking the one thing it was meant to protect, if the app's actual phone values didn't exactly match what the new policy required. Resolved by grepping the real call sites and confirming the values already matched byte-for-byte, rather than assuming from the schema alone.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today closed real gaps rather than just documenting them — all four migrations and six Edge Function updates are deployed and confirmed live, with your go-ahead before touching production. The `set-pin` brute-force gap is the one most worth your attention: it was a full account-takeover path with zero rate limiting, sitting right next to a PIN check that already had the correct lockout, which is exactly the kind of gap that's easy to miss because the "same" logic looks protected two lines away. Separately, and unrelated to any bug: this audit is also what surfaced that a few of this report's own earlier entries describe fixes that don't exist in the actual codebase (the chat Realtime dedupe, the shared favorites context, the rating-average filter) — flagged plainly since this report is a factual record for you, not just a working log for me.

### Day 61 — 28 July 2026 (Tuesday)

**Progress:**
Today the Zoho Invoice plan from two days ago went from a written proposal to an actual, verified-live integration, followed by an app-wide performance audit prompted by a slow-app / late-notification complaint, and a small but recurring dark-mode UI bug fixed at its shared root instead of screen-by-screen.

**Zoho Invoice — built, deployed, and debugged live end-to-end:** with HR's go-ahead on Sunday's guidelines document, today was building the actual pieces: a migration adding `zoho_invoice_id`/`zoho_sync_status`/`zoho_sync_error` tracking columns to `booking` (with the same column-level revoke pattern used on `deal_amount`/`deal_note`), and a new `zoho-invoice-sync` Edge Function doing the real work — OAuth token refresh, find-or-create Zoho contact, find-or-create Zoho catalog item, then invoice creation — reading every credential from Supabase Vault rather than `Deno.env`, since this project's account still can't set real Function secrets. Before writing the API calls, checked Sunday's draft against Zoho's actual live documentation rather than trusting it blindly, and found two real inaccuracies worth correcting: Zoho's invoice line items require an existing catalog `item_id` (there's no ad-hoc `{name, rate, quantity}` line, which the original draft had assumed), so a find-or-create Item step had to be added that wasn't in the original plan at all. Deployed the function, then walked HR through the Zoho-side setup directly — creating the Self Client in Zoho's API Console, generating the OAuth grant token with the right scopes, exchanging it for a refresh token, and wiring a Supabase Database Webhook on `booking`'s `UPDATE` event to call the deployed function, guarded by a new shared-secret header since a webhook's own auth token alone isn't real protection (anyone holding the public anon key could otherwise trigger it directly).

**The live debugging that followed was the real work of the day.** First manual test failed outright with a Postgres error — I'd deployed the Edge Function but never actually pushed the new migration to the remote database, caught immediately by a failed test query and fixed by pushing it before retrying. Second failure was a genuine Zoho API rejection — error code 57, "not authorized" — specifically on the Items endpoint only, while the contact lookup succeeded fine on the same token, which narrowed it to a scope problem rather than a broad auth failure. Assumed the scope was simply missing from the grant token at first; regenerating it didn't fix it, which was the signal that the scope name itself was wrong — cross-checked against Zoho's own API docs and found that Items live under the `ZohoInvoice.settings.*` scope, not the far more obvious-looking `ZohoInvoice.items.*`, which isn't a real scope at all and fails silently at grant time with no error until the endpoint is actually called. Alongside that, hit a second, unrelated bug while exchanging grant codes for refresh tokens: a refresh token pasted from PowerShell's console output kept failing with `invalid_code` despite being correct, traced to PowerShell auto-wrapping long string output across multiple lines with padding, which got copied in verbatim as embedded whitespace and newlines — caught by comparing the stored secret's actual character length (88, then later 71) against the real token's length (70), and fixed by copying it via `Set-Clipboard` with an explicit `.Trim()` instead of selecting wrapped console text by hand. With both the scope name and the token corrected, reran the sync and got a real, correct invoice back from Zoho — then confirmed the Database Webhook itself (not just a manual retry) fired automatically on a second, untouched booking, which is what actually proves the pipeline is live rather than just working when manually triggered.

**Closed the loop on documentation accuracy:** updated the guidelines document to mark the integration's status as live rather than planned, corrected the two inaccuracies the live debugging had surfaced (the Items scope name, and the Vault-based secret storage this account requires), and added an "operating it going forward" section — how to rotate the refresh token, retry one failed sync manually, and check sync health — since the original document was written as a build plan and needed to become an operating reference instead. HR separately asked whether the integration should also create Zoho Quotations (pre-work estimates) alongside Invoices; gave a considered view rather than building anything speculative — HomeSewa's own booking flow doesn't have a distinct "quote" stage to map a Quotation onto (the customer's rough budget and the professional's final agreed price are the only two numbers that exist, and the second one is already final by the time anything syncs), so recommended holding off unless there's a concrete business reason, rather than doubling the integration's surface for a distinction the app itself doesn't make.

**An app-wide performance audit, prompted directly by a complaint that the app feels slow and notifications arrive late:** rather than guess at a fix, found one confirmed bug first — the booking-accept handler in `BookingDetails_1.tsx` was awaiting a push notification and then an SMS send sequentially, each a real network round-trip through a separate Edge Function, before letting the professional's screen navigate away — then ran three parallel deep-dive audits covering every customer screen, every admin screen, and all cross-cutting infrastructure (every Edge Function, the shared Supabase client, the notification fan-out helpers) to check whether that was an isolated bug or a pattern. It was a pattern, and a specific one: four different screens (`Chat.tsx`, `RateProfessional.tsx`, both booking-details screens) were each fetching the *entire* `booking` table just to find one row client-side by id; every notification-targeting query (which professionals to push, which customers to SMS) filtered on `workforce.services`, `workforce.preferred_city`, `workforce.profile_status`, and `booking.services` with no index backing any of them, across all 34 existing migrations; and nothing anywhere in the network stack — not the shared Edge-Function-invoke helper, not the Supabase client itself — had a timeout, so one slow or cold-starting function call could hang a screen indefinitely instead of failing fast.

**Fixed the highest-impact findings the same day, deployed the server-side half immediately:** added a migration indexing the four columns above (checking first, by grepping every `.eq('phone', ...)` in the codebase, that a fifth candidate column wasn't actually filtered anywhere before deciding not to add an unjustified index); added a single shared `fetchBookingById` helper and swapped all four full-table-fetch call sites to it; rewrote the booking-accept handler to fire the notification and SMS in the background instead of blocking navigation on them; added a 15-second timeout to the shared Edge-Function-invoke helper; merged two sequential rate-limit count queries in the SMS-sending function into one; parallelized two independent database updates in both the professional-approval and -rejection functions; and cut an unnecessary extra network hop out of the Zoho function's own admin-alert path by calling the push provider directly instead of routing through a second Edge Function. Bounded several previously-unlimited table fetches in the admin user-management and notifications screens, parallelized a few more independent queries there, and replaced an expensive full-payload JSON serialization — done purely to check whether anything had changed — inside a 15-second admin polling loop with a cheap id-and-status comparison instead. Verified with a clean full-project TypeScript compile after every change, deployed and redeployed every touched Edge Function, and pushed the new migration — but was upfront about the one thing this environment genuinely can't verify: there's no Android/iOS simulator available here, and the app's web target has never actually worked at all (a pre-existing native-only dependency unrelated to anything touched today), so the client-side changes rest on a clean typecheck and careful manual tracing, not an on-device run.

**A recurring dark-mode bug, fixed once at its shared root instead of per-screen:** got a screenshot showing the "Select Service" dropdown's search box on the Book a Service screen rendering as a plain white box against an otherwise fully dark-themed form. Traced it to the two shared dropdown components used by every searchable dropdown across the entire app — both styled the search input's border and text color from the active theme but never actually set its background color, so the underlying UI library's own hardcoded white default was showing through underneath. Fixed both shared components directly (one background-color line each), plus set the placeholder text color explicitly too, since the library exposes a dedicated prop for that which neither component had ever used — meaning every form with a searchable dropdown inherits the fix from two files, not one screen at a time.

**Learning:**
The Zoho scope bug was the sharpest lesson of the day: `ZohoInvoice.items.CREATE` looks exactly like a real scope name, reads as obviously correct, and Zoho's own grant-token flow doesn't reject it or warn about it at all — it just silently grants nothing, and the only signal is a generic "not authorized" error the first time the real endpoint is called. The only way to actually find the right answer was to stop assuming the scope just needed regenerating and instead go back to Zoho's documentation and check the literal string. The performance audit reinforced something from earlier in the internship from a different angle: three separate screens had independently reinvented the same "fetch everything, filter client-side" shape without any of them being wrong in isolation — it took looking across all of them at once, not any single code review, to see it was one root cause wearing four faces, and that's exactly why the fix was one new shared helper, not four separate patches.

**Skills Gained:**
API integration — reading a third-party API's actual scope documentation directly rather than trusting an intuitive-sounding name, after confirming a token was otherwise valid by isolating exactly which endpoint it failed on and which one it didn't · Debugging methodology — diagnosing a "valid-looking but rejected" secret by comparing its actual stored character length against the expected length, rather than assuming the value itself must be wrong, which is what surfaced a shell-output copy-paste artifact instead of a real credential error · Database performance — recognizing that a missing index is invisible until a table grows, and that the right fix is indexing the exact columns a query filters on (confirmed by grep, not assumption) rather than indexing speculatively · System design — treating "the app feels slow" as a pattern-finding exercise across the whole codebase rather than a single-file fix, and consolidating four independent instances of the same anti-pattern into one shared, reusable fix.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The Zoho Items endpoint failure had no distinguishing error beyond a generic authorization code, which could plausibly have meant a bad token, a bad org ID, or a bad scope — resolved by isolating that the *same* token succeeded on the contacts endpoint moments earlier, which narrowed the search from "something about auth is wrong" to "something specific to this one endpoint's permission is wrong," and from there to checking the exact scope name against source documentation instead of guessing. (2) The mangled-refresh-token bug looked identical to the scope bug from the outside — both produced an "invalid" response from Zoho — which risked chasing the wrong fix twice. Resolved by checking the boring, mechanical explanation first (does the stored value's length match what was actually generated) before assuming the more interesting explanation (a wrong scope) applied to both failures.

**Message to Mentor:**
The Zoho Invoice integration is genuinely live now, not just planned — a real invoice was created in the Zoho org today, and I confirmed the Database Webhook fires on its own for a fresh booking, not only when manually triggered. Getting there took three separate live bugs (a missed migration push, a wrong Zoho scope name that Zoho's own console doesn't warn about, and a shell copy-paste artifact), and I've corrected the guidelines document to reflect what was actually true rather than what the original plan assumed. On the performance side: the fixes are deployed on the server, and the client-side ones pass a clean typecheck and careful tracing, but I want to be direct that I can't hand you an on-device confirmation from this environment — that's the one piece I'd ask you to check on a real phone before calling it fully closed.

## Week 11 — 29 July to 04 August 2026 (Wednesday – Tuesday)

---

### Day 62 — 29 July 2026 (Wednesday)

**Progress:**
Today started with a direct HR conversation about the Zoho Invoice integration's scope — a follow-up chain of three questions on top of Tuesday's live-firing sync: whether the system should also generate Zoho Quotations before work starts, whether Supabase or Zoho was the real source of truth, and whether a customer's initial budget range gets overwritten by the professional's finalized deal amount in Zoho. Answered each directly from the actual implementation rather than the general Zoho product description: no Quotation function exists because HomeSewa's booking flow has no distinct "quote" stage to map it onto; Supabase remains the single source of truth for every booking record and always will, Zoho only ever receives a one-way snapshot at the moment a deal is confirmed; and only the single finalized `deal_amount` is ever sent — the customer's original budget range is never submitted to Zoho at all, so there was no "overwrite" happening, there was simply nothing there to begin with.

That last answer led directly to a real requirement: HR asked that both numbers — the customer's original form budget and the professional's finalized deal amount — be visible on the Zoho side going forward, with a coupon feature flagged for later but explicitly out of scope for today. Implemented this as an additive change rather than a schema change: `booking.budget` already existed as a column but was never read by the sync function, so I added it to the `zoho-invoice-sync` Edge Function's type and select statements and appended it to the invoice's `notes` field alongside the existing deal note, keeping the line-item rate as the sole billed amount exactly as before. Deployed immediately at HR's request.

**A full project-wide bug audit, run and fixed end-to-end again today, this time on HomeSewa specifically:** asked directly to check the whole project "from beginning to end," so split the 174-file codebase into two parallel deep-dive passes — the frontend (every screen, component, and the shared UI layer) and the backend (every Supabase Edge Function, every migration, and the API helper layer) — each briefed to report only concrete, reproducible bugs with a file, a line, and a failure scenario. Personally verified the two most serious backend claims against the live code before reporting them as fact rather than trusting the sub-review at face value: read `_shared/cors.ts` directly to confirm `x-admin-session-token` really was missing from the allowed-headers list, and read `send-otp/index.ts` directly to confirm there genuinely was no daily cap behind the 45-second resend cooldown.

**What the audit found:** `send-otp` let an attacker request a fresh OTP indefinitely after each 45-second cooldown, resetting `verify-otp`'s 5-guess lockout every time — a real account-takeover path against PIN reset for anyone who knew a phone number. The shared CORS headers list was missing the custom `x-admin-session-token` header that ten different session-gated admin functions (approve/reject professional, toggle status, road-block writes, etc.) actually authenticate with, meaning the admin panel would silently fail on a web build even though it works fine on native. `zoho-invoice-sync` had a genuine TOCTOU race — its null-check on `zoho_invoice_id` reads data from before the multi-second Zoho API sequence, so a duplicate webhook delivery or a double-clicked manual retry could both pass the check and both create a Zoho invoice for the same booking. Two application forms (`Career.tsx`, `Partnership.tsx`) had no double-submit guard at all, unlike every other form in the app. And `AdminLogin.tsx` carried dead `isLoggedIn`/`handleLogout` code left over from an earlier refactor.

**Fixed and deployed all five the same day:** wrote two new migrations — an `otp_send_log` table enforcing an 8-per-day cap per phone+purpose (mirroring the existing `sms_send_log` pattern), and a `claim_zoho_sync` atomic Postgres RPC that flips a booking's sync status to `'syncing'` in a single UPDATE, so only one caller can ever win the race regardless of how many concurrent sync attempts land. Added the missing header to `_shared/cors.ts` and redeployed all ten affected functions. Added an `isSubmitting` guard to both application forms, matching the pattern already used by the booking form. Removed the dead login-state code from `AdminLogin.tsx`.

**A real environment blocker, worked around rather than pushed through blindly:** `supabase db push` failed outright with a generic, unhelpful spawn error. Traced it methodically rather than guessing — first ruled out a corrupted CLI binary by running the 121MB executable directly and confirming it works standalone, then ruled out a path-with-spaces theory by testing from a space-free mapped drive, before concluding the CLI's remote push genuinely shells out to Docker even for a database it isn't running locally, and Docker isn't installed on this machine. Rather than requiring a Docker Desktop install just to run two short SQL statements, applied both migrations directly through the Supabase SQL Editor, then independently verified each was actually live before trusting it — probed `claim_zoho_sync` as an anonymous caller and got a clean `null` back (proof the function exists and ran, not a "does not exist" error), and confirmed `otp_send_log` returned a valid empty result set rather than a relation-missing error. Only deployed the two dependent Edge Functions after both checks passed, specifically to avoid shipping code that would silently break OTP sending or invoice syncing in production against database objects that didn't exist yet.

**Learning:**
The CORS finding was a reminder that a security fix and a functional bug can be the exact same line of code depending on which direction you're looking from — the missing header wasn't a vulnerability, it was a silent failure, but it needed the same kind of direct verification (reading the actual allowed-headers list, not assuming it was fine because native worked) as any of today's real security gaps. The environment blocker was the sharper lesson: a generic, unhelpful error message is not a reason to guess or give up — isolating it methodically (binary works standalone → not corrupted; space-free path still fails → not a path bug; therefore an internal dependency) turned an opaque failure into a specific, explainable one, and from there a decision — work around it safely rather than blocking the day's fixes on installing Docker Desktop.

**Skills Gained:**
Systems debugging — isolating a generic runtime error to its actual root cause by testing each hypothesis independently (binary integrity, path handling, external dependency) rather than accepting the first plausible-sounding explanation · Database engineering — the same atomic-RPC pattern from Monday's audit applied to a new, unrelated race condition, recognizing it as the same class of bug rather than inventing a new fix shape · Deployment discipline — verifying a database migration actually landed via an independent, side-effect-free probe (an RPC call, a REST select) before deploying code that depends on it, instead of trusting that a manual SQL Editor run succeeded · Client communication — translating three increasingly specific HR questions about an accounting integration into direct, accurate answers grounded in the actual code path, without overstating or hedging past what the implementation actually does.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The `supabase db push` failure gave no actionable information on its own — just a generic spawn error, identical whether run through Node, PowerShell, or the raw binary directly. Resolved by testing each layer independently until only one explanation remained unfalsified, rather than either giving up or attempting a risky workaround (like extracting stored credentials from the OS keychain) to force the CLI to work. (2) Deploying `send-otp` and `zoho-invoice-sync` before their migrations landed would have broken OTP sending and invoice syncing outright in production. Resolved by holding both deploys back and independently confirming the new database objects were live — via harmless probe calls, not by re-reading the SQL Editor's own success message — before deploying either function.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today combined a direct HR request (making both the customer's budget and the finalized deal amount visible in Zoho, shipped the same day) with a second full project-wide audit, this time on HomeSewa, which surfaced and fixed a real OTP account-takeover gap, a silent admin-panel-on-web failure, and a Zoho duplicate-invoice race — all deployed and independently verified live today, not just written up. The one part worth flagging directly: `supabase db push` doesn't work from this machine at all — it requires Docker, which isn't installed here — so I applied both of today's migrations manually through the Supabase SQL Editor instead, and verified each one was actually live with a harmless probe call before deploying anything that depended on it. Functionally nothing is degraded, but our migration history is a step out of sync with what the CLI would show, and I'd like your view on whether that's worth installing Docker to properly fix, or leaving as-is since the workaround is repeatable.

### Day 63 — 30 July 2026 (Thursday)

**Progress:**
HR's instruction today was direct and simple on its face — "QA whole works for this week" — but turned into two distinct deliverables once it was actually scoped: a risk-ordered internal QA checklist, and a customer-facing "What's New" release note, both built off the same underlying commit history rather than written from memory of what felt shipped.

**Building the QA checklist against real commits, not a vague sense of "this week":** started from `git log --since="7 days ago"`, which surfaced six commits from Jul 23–29 (OTP brute-force and auto-send fixes, the booking-accept race, the deal-confirmation modal's numeric input and button sizing, the chat/ratings/deal-terms RLS lockdown, Zoho Invoice sync going live plus its own duplicate-invoice race fix, and the admin CORS/form double-submit guards). Rather than hand back a generic "test the app" list, grouped the checklist by the actual mechanism each fix targeted — rate-limit and lockout behavior for OTP, concurrent-accept and double-submit races for bookings, cross-account access checks for chat/ratings/deal-terms, invoice-count and budget-field checks for Zoho, browser-specific CORS checks for admin, contrast checks for the dropdown dark-mode fix — so each item maps to a specific failure mode a fix was meant to close, not a screen to click around on.

**Writing the release notes exposed that "this week" was the wrong boundary entirely:** asked to write new "What's New" copy in the same style as a previous release's bullets, and initially scoped it the same way as the QA list — the last 7 days. Corrected after being told explicitly not to limit it to this week, which meant finding the *real* boundary: where the previous release's own bullets actually stopped. Matched each of the previous release's six bullets back to the specific commit it described (completion photos and dark mode/Nepali to `65b4a35`, the drawer/calendar fix to `233ad24`, the dark-mode-inconsistency fix to `2bc223d`) and confirmed the true cutoff was Jul 21, one commit earlier than the 7-day window had assumed (`3ef9328`, Jul 22, was missed by the calendar-week scoping but correctly belonged in the new notes). That one-commit gap was the actual point of the correction, not just a stylistic redo.

**Deciding what belongs in public release notes, not just what shipped:** the Zoho Invoice sync (`e213a34`, `b23ed35`, and half of `bc580a5`) was the single largest change by line count in the whole window, and would read as an obvious release-note headline. Checked `ZOHO_INVOICE_INTEGRATION_GUIDELINES.md` before including it and found its own stated intent in the first section: a background, admin-only system built explicitly "without changing anything visible to customers or professionals." Left it out of the public notes entirely on that basis, rather than assuming a feature that big must be worth advertising.

**Learning:**
The clearest lesson was that "this week" is a leaky proxy for "since the last release" — a calendar window and a release boundary only agree by coincidence, and the one commit that fell in the gap (`3ef9328`, one day before the 7-day cutoff) would have been silently dropped from the release notes if the previous release's actual content hadn't been checked bullet-by-bullet against real commits instead of trusted as a rough date. The second lesson was that "shipped this week" and "worth telling the customer about" are different questions with different sources of truth — the answer to the second one was already written down in the feature's own design document, and checking that was more reliable than judging visibility by the size of the diff.

**Skills Gained:**
Release management — anchoring "what's new" to the actual content of the previous release rather than a rolling time window, so a commit landing just outside a calendar week doesn't get silently dropped from the next release's notes · Product communication — building two different artifacts (a risk-ordered internal QA checklist, an impact-ordered customer changelog) from the same commit set, for two different audiences · Requirements-reading — treating a feature's own design document as the authority on what's user-visible, rather than inferring visibility from how large or important the underlying change looks in the diff.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Scoping "QA whole works for this week" literally by calendar date missed `3ef9328`, a commit one day outside the 7-day window that still belonged in the new release. Resolved by re-deriving the boundary from the content of the previous release notes themselves — matching each existing bullet to the commit it came from — rather than trusting a fixed day count a second time after being corrected once already. (2) Zoho Invoice sync was large and recent enough that it would have been an easy default headline for the release notes. Resolved by checking the feature's own guidelines document, which explicitly rules out any customer/professional-facing visibility, before deciding to leave it out.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today was QA-and-communication work rather than new code, per your instruction to QA the week's work: I built a risk-ordered checklist off the six real commits from Jul 23–29 (OTP hardening, the booking-accept race, the deal-confirmation modal, chat/ratings/deal-terms access control, Zoho invoice sync, admin CORS) and separately drafted "What's New" release notes in your preferred format. Worth flagging directly: I initially scoped both to a 7-day window, which would have silently dropped one real commit (`3ef9328`) from the release notes — caught only because I re-anchored to where the previous release's own bullets actually stopped rather than trusting the calendar. I also deliberately left Zoho Invoice sync out of the public notes despite it being the week's biggest change, since its own guidelines document states it's admin-only with zero visible change — let me know if you'd rather it get a line anyway.

### Day 64 — 31 July 2026 (Friday)

**Progress:**
HR asked for a set of six features on RocketSingh, the sibling project positioned as a HomeSewa competitor: a Nepal flag indicator, an updated contact number, auto-detection of the user's country, a Nepal-specific city list, a HomeSewa-style address flow, and OTP delivery via Sparrow SMS. Before writing any code, spent today scoping each item against RocketSingh's actual codebase rather than taking the request at face value, since the six items as first written could be read two different ways — either "re-skin this as a Nepal-only app" or "add a switch between two countries" — and building the wrong one would mean redoing the address and city work from scratch.

**Auditing RocketSingh against HomeSewa item by item:** went through the six requests one at a time and found each is at a different stage of readiness. The flag has a reusable image slot already in place across `NumberBar.tsx`, `Book.tsx`, `Career.tsx`, and `Partnership.tsx` — currently pointed at a generic placeholder rather than any flag — so wiring in a real Nepal flag asset is close to a drop-in change once the country logic exists. The contact screen has no phone number at all today (only location, email, and website cards), so the new number is a straightforward addition rather than an edit. Country auto-detection and Sparrow SMS both don't exist anywhere in the codebase yet — RocketSingh currently authenticates OTP through Firebase Phone Auth, and its own resend-code handler literally alerts that it "needs to be linked to your custom SMS gateway," which is a useful confirmation that this was already a known gap on their end, not something I'm introducing. The city list is still `['Chennai', 'Other']` left over from the app's original India branding. The address form has city and area as two independent, unlinked dropdowns, unlike HomeSewa's linked city-then-filtered-area typeahead.

**A clarifying exchange with HR changed the actual scope:** initially planned this as swapping RocketSingh's India data for Nepal data outright. HR corrected that directly — "we are adding flag and country changing option, not changing the app" — meaning the India data (RocketSingh's existing Chennai cities and areas) stays, and Nepal is added alongside it as a second, switchable option, with a flag toggle deciding which set of cities and address fields is active at any moment. Confirmed with HR that the address behavior should match HomeSewa's linked city→area pattern exactly, just duplicated per country instead of hardcoded to one.

**Two open items flagged for HR rather than guessed at:** RocketSingh has no India-side phone number yet, only the new Nepal one HR gave me — didn't assume it should apply to both flags. And Sparrow SMS is a Nepal telecom gateway built around `+977` numbers; before wiring OTP delivery through it, want HR to confirm it can actually deliver to `+91` Indian numbers, since guessing wrong here would mean silently broken OTP for half the app's users.

**Learning:**
The best moment to catch a scope misread is before writing code, not after: my first read of "add a Nepal flag and Nepal address" was a plausible interpretation of the literal request, but it was the wrong one, and HR's correction ("not changing the app") only came because I asked instead of just starting the address rework. Auditing the existing codebase file-by-file before proposing a plan also surfaced a bug HR hadn't mentioned — RocketSingh mixes `+91` and `+977` prefixes across different screens today — which is worth fixing as part of this work regardless of which scope interpretation had been correct.

**Skills Gained:**
Requirements clarification — treating an ambiguous feature request as having multiple valid readings and confirming the actual one with the stakeholder before scoping work, rather than picking the first plausible interpretation · Comparative codebase auditing — using a sibling project (HomeSewa) as a reference implementation to judge how much of a requested feature already exists versus needs to be built from scratch · Dependency risk-spotting — recognizing that a third-party service (Sparrow SMS) built for one country's phone numbering may not silently work for another, and raising it before integration rather than after a failed OTP in production.

**Challenge & Solution:**
My first pass treated all six HR requests as "localize RocketSingh to Nepal," which would have meant deleting RocketSingh's existing India/Chennai city and area data. Resolved by asking HR directly what "add the address" meant before touching the booking form, which surfaced the real requirement: a country toggle that keeps both India and Nepal data sets and switches between them, not a one-way rebrand.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today was planning only, no code changed, since RocketSingh's booking form and OTP flow are both worth getting right on the first pass rather than reworking twice. I audited all six requested features against the existing code and against HomeSewa as the reference implementation, and caught that my first interpretation of the request — replacing RocketSingh's India data with Nepal data — was wrong; you corrected me to a country-switchable model (flag toggle deciding between India's existing data and a new Nepal data set), which I've now scoped correctly for tomorrow. Two things I'd like your input on before I start building: whether India needs its own contact number alongside the new Nepal one, and whether Sparrow SMS can deliver OTPs to Indian (`+91`) numbers, since it's a Nepal-focused gateway and I'd rather confirm than assume.

### Day 65 — 01 August 2026 (Saturday)

**Progress:**
Picked up where yesterday's planning left off. HR confirmed the address behavior should work exactly like HomeSewa's and sent over the Nepal office address and a first contact number, so I built the actual country-switching infrastructure: a `CountryContext` (`src/context/countryContext.tsx`) modeled on the existing `userContext.tsx` pattern, a `countryData.ts` constants file holding per-country address/map/tagline data, a flag toggle wired into `Header2.tsx`, and the Contact screen (`Contact.tsx`) reading address, map link, and tagline from whichever country is active instead of the hardcoded Chennai text.

**A stale dev client turned into a real environment investigation:** ran the app to check the change and hit `Cannot find native module 'ExpoDocumentPicker'` in what turned out to be the plain Expo Go app. Traced this to RocketSingh being a bare/prebuilt project with native config (Firebase, OneSignal, a committed `android/` folder) that Expo Go's generic client doesn't support at all — it needs a custom dev client built via `expo run:android`. Kicked off that build, which then surfaced a second, unrelated problem: the machine only has 7.74 GB of RAM, and the Gradle daemon crashed with a genuine JVM out-of-memory error (`Native memory allocation (malloc) failed... Chunk::new`) partway through. Retried scoped to a single CPU architecture (`x86_64`, matching the emulator) and hit a second OOM crash in a different place — `clang++` itself dying mid-compile on `react-native-reanimated`'s C++ source, because Ninja was running one compiler process per CPU core (12 of them) with no memory headroom to spare. Both were genuine resource-exhaustion failures, not code bugs, so the fix was reducing concurrency, not changing anything in the project.

**Auditing a stakeholder's plan against the real codebase before building it:** HR sent an expanded six-item spec referencing "Sparrow SMS" and a HomeSewa `areasByCity` structure as if both already existed or were directly portable. Before writing anything against either claim, grepped the repo: zero references to "Sparrow" anywhere — RocketSingh's actual OTP path is Firebase Phone Auth (`signInWithPhoneNumber` in `BookingOtp.tsx` and `helpboxOTP.tsx`), which already works for any country code without a Nepal-specific gateway. Flagged the mismatch directly instead of quietly building toward a service that isn't there; HR confirmed to skip the SMS item entirely and proceed with the rest. Also confirmed `Data.ts` already had a real, working Chennai city/area dataset before deciding to reuse it as India's data set rather than duplicating or discarding it.

**Building the linked city→area picker and finishing the memory-constrained rebuild:** extended `countryData.ts` with a `cities`/`areasByCity` shape per country, wired `Book.tsx`'s City and Area dropdowns to reset and re-filter off the active country (`areasByCity[selectedLocation]`), and added country auto-detection via device timezone (`Asia/Kathmandu` vs. default) rather than literal SIM or IP lookup — there's no SIM-reading native module in this project, and adding one meant another risky native rebuild on a machine that had already OOM-crashed twice that day; an IP-based lookup would have added a live third-party network dependency for the same result. Went back to HR with this tradeoff explicitly rather than silently substituting a weaker implementation for what was asked. HR then supplied the real neighborhood/ward lists for Kathmandu, Bhaktapur, and Lalitpur (over 250 entries for Kathmandu alone) to replace what I'd left as an explicit placeholder rather than inventing plausible-sounding Nepali place names myself — ported all three into a new `src/data/NepalAreaData.ts`. Finished the interrupted native build afterward: freed memory by asking the extra VS Code windows to be closed and stopping the Android emulator myself (build doesn't need it running, only the final install does, and the emulator alone was holding ~870 MB), then rebuilt fully serially (`--max-workers=1`, `CMAKE_BUILD_PARALLEL_LEVEL=1`) so only one compiler process ran at a time. It completed, and I installed the resulting APK on the emulator and relaunched the app to confirm the original `ExpoDocumentPicker` crash and its cascading "missing default export" warnings on the Book/Career/Partnership routes were gone.

**Live device feedback changed two UI decisions after they were already built:** HR had explicitly asked for "both flags visible as a tappable picker" the day before, which I built and shipped. Once HR saw it running on a real screenshot, the instruction reversed — back to a single flag that toggles on tap — and I reverted it. The same screenshot session surfaced a real bug I hadn't introduced but hadn't caught either: the Home screen's "Help" number bar (`NumberBar.tsx`) used a leading icon that turned out to be a static India-flag PNG (`assets/header/right.png`), reused as a generic "phone icon" and never actually country-aware; swapped it for the live flag emoji. While in that file I also found and fixed the helpbox-submission function hardcoding `+91` regardless of which country was active — a real correctness bug now that the app serves two countries, not something HR had flagged, but directly in scope of the work.

**A recurring phone-number instruction changed three times, and each change got a direct question instead of a silent guess:** the number moved from `98520 24 365` to `98520 25 735` "in all the place," and then a later message reintroduced `98520 24 365` specifically "for the contact page," which conflicted with the still-standing "everywhere" instruction. Rather than picking one interpretation, asked directly whether the Contact page and the Help bar were meant to show two different numbers — confirmed yes — and split the data model accordingly: a `contactPhone` field for the Contact page (`98520 24 365`) separate from `phone` for the Help bar (`98520 25 735`), plus a shared `formatLocalPhone()` helper producing the `98520 25 735`-style 5-2-3 grouping HR had actually been typing every time, instead of the generic 3-3-4 grouping I'd defaulted to initially.

**Learning:**
The build failures were a good reminder that not every red error is a code problem — both crashes were the JVM and the native compiler honestly reporting that the machine ran out of memory, and the fix was entirely about concurrency and available RAM (closing windows, stopping the emulator, capping worker counts), not anything in RocketSingh's source. Separately, treating a stakeholder's technical claim ("Sparrow SMS," "port HomeSewa's areasByCity") as something to verify against the actual repo before building around it — rather than as settled fact — caught one dependency that doesn't exist in this codebase at all and one dataset I genuinely don't have access to, both before any code was written against either assumption.

**Skills Gained:**
Native build environment debugging — reading a JVM out-of-memory crash log to its actual cause (`malloc` failure, not a code defect) and reducing Gradle/CMake parallelism to fit a fixed, small memory budget rather than assuming the build itself was broken · Claim verification against source of truth — grepping for a named third-party service before designing around it, and confirming an existing dataset's shape before deciding to reuse rather than duplicate it · Iterative UI correction from live device feedback — treating a shipped design decision as provisional until seen running on an actual screen, and reverting cleanly when a screenshot changed the stakeholder's mind · Data modeling under conflicting instructions — resolving three successive, partly-contradictory phone-number instructions by asking one direct scoping question rather than guessing, then encoding the real answer (two numbers, two fields) instead of overwriting one value repeatedly.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Two separate native-build OOM crashes on a 7.74 GB machine already running an emulator and multiple IDE windows. Resolved by treating each as a resource-budget problem rather than a code problem: scoped the build to one CPU architecture, capped Gradle to one worker and CMake to one compile job at a time, and physically freed memory (closing extra windows, stopping the emulator during compilation) before retrying — the build succeeded once concurrency matched what the machine could actually hold. (2) HR's phone-number instruction changed three times over the day, with the last change conflicting with an earlier "everywhere" instruction rather than clearly superseding it. Resolved by asking directly whether the two numbers belonged on two different screens instead of assuming either "most recent wins" or "original instruction wins" — confirmed they did, and modeled it as two distinct fields instead of picking a side.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today took yesterday's approved plan all the way to a working, live-tested feature: the country toggle, per-country address/city/area data (including HomeSewa's linked city→area pattern in the booking form), and the Nepal dataset you provided are all in and running on the emulator. Two things worth flagging directly: first, "auto-detect via SIM or IP" is actually implemented via device timezone — there's no SIM-reading native module in this project and I didn't want to risk a third native rebuild adding one, so let me know if true SIM/carrier detection is a hard requirement rather than a nice-to-have. Second, today also involved a fair amount of non-feature work — two native-build memory crashes that had nothing to do with the code and needed diagnosing on their own — which is normal for a first dev-client build on this kind of project, but ate a real chunk of the day before any of the country-switcher work could be tested on-device.

### Day 66 — 02 August 2026 (Sunday)

**Progress:**
Picked up the country-switcher work from yesterday and closed out the remaining gaps before treating the six-item spec as done. Wired the real Kamal Pokhari map screenshot HR sent (`assets/otherImages/map-kamalpokhari.png`) in as the Nepal map image on the Contact screen, replacing the San Francisco placeholder that had been sitting there — traced that placeholder back to an orphaned asset from HomeSewa itself (same file, same checksum) that had never been referenced in either codebase, confirmed against HomeSewa's own source before reusing it. The Contact page address went through one more round of back-and-forth: HR's screenshot pin read "SRIYOG," which didn't match the "Rem.Work" text already in `countryData.ts`, so I asked which was correct rather than guessing, updated to SRIYOG for the address and map link, then HR reversed that specific instruction back to "Rem.Work, Kamalpokhari, Kathmandu, Nepal" a message later — reverted the address field only, left the SRIYOG-based map link alone since that's still the more accurate pin location.

**A full-codebase audit turned up the same static-flag bug in three more places than I'd caught yesterday:** yesterday's fix only covered the Home screen's `NumberBar.tsx`. HR asked directly whether "flags and number" changed correctly everywhere when switching to Nepal, which was reason enough to grep the whole project rather than assume yesterday's one fix was the only instance. Found the identical pattern — a static `assets/header/right.png` icon standing in for a country flag — repeated in the phone number fields on `Book.tsx`, `Career.tsx`, and `Partnership.tsx`. Book.tsx already had `useCountry()` wired in for its city/area dropdowns, so fixing it was a one-line swap; Career.tsx and Partnership.tsx didn't import the country context at all, so both needed the hook added before the flag icon could be made live. All three now render `countryInfo.flag` instead of the static image.

**Closed the SIM-vs-timezone gap flagged in yesterday's mentor message:** yesterday I'd noted that "auto-detect via SIM or IP" was actually implemented via device timezone only, and asked whether true SIM/IP detection was a hard requirement. Given the choice between the two, IP geolocation was the better fit — it reflects where the device is actually connecting from rather than relying on the phone's timezone setting ever having been configured for Kathmandu, and unlike SIM detection it needs no native module or rebuild, which mattered after yesterday's two OOM-crashed builds. Added a `detectCountryFromIP()` call (via `ipapi.co`, three-second timeout so a flaky connection can't stall startup) that runs first, with the existing timezone check kept as an automatic fallback if the network call fails or times out.

**Also spent time on git/GitHub mechanics rather than app code:** walked through what a proper commit message should look like for the day's changes, and flagged before staging that a blanket `git add .` would have swept in an unrelated `.env.example` deletion and two stray JVM crash log files (`android/hs_err_pid20832.log`, `android/replay_pid20832.log`) alongside the real feature work — recommended scoping the `git add` instead. HR committed and pushed to both project remotes anyway; the push to the personal `Samek55/RhinoSingh-Tackles` fork succeeded, but the push to `BroadPress/RocketSingh` failed with a 403 ("Write access to repository not granted"). Walked through why that's a GitHub permissions/credentials issue rather than anything wrong with the commit itself — the same local repo pushed cleanly to one remote and was rejected by the other, which only happens when the authenticated account or token lacks write access to that specific org repo, not from anything in the diff.

**Learning:**
Being asked directly "is this all done?" was more useful than it first looked — it forced a re-audit of a fix I'd already considered finished, and that re-audit found three more copies of a bug I'd only fixed in one place the day before. The general lesson: a targeted fix for a reported instance of a bug doesn't confirm the pattern is gone elsewhere, and a grep across the whole project is cheap insurance against exactly that. Separately, walking through the 403 push error was a reminder that not every failure after a successful git operation is a git problem — the commit was fine, one push worked, and the second push's failure was entirely about which GitHub account was authenticated, not the repository state.

**Skills Gained:**
Pattern-complete bug fixing — after finding one instance of a static-asset-instead-of-live-data bug, searching the entire codebase for the same pattern rather than assuming a single fix closed the issue · Revisiting a flagged tradeoff with a concrete upgrade — turning yesterday's "timezone only, not SIM/IP" caveat into an actual IP-geolocation implementation with a graceful fallback, once given the go-ahead · Git hygiene under real-world pressure — distinguishing which untracked/modified files belong in a feature commit versus incidental repo noise (deleted env file, crash logs) before a blanket `git add .` · Diagnosing GitHub push failures — reading a 403 "Write access to repository not granted" error as an authentication/permissions issue scoped to one specific remote, not a defect in the commit or local repository.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) HR's address instruction changed direction twice in quick succession — first to "SRIYOG" to match a map screenshot, then back to "Rem.Work" one message later, with no explicit statement about what should happen to the map link in between. Resolved by treating the two as separable: reverted only the address text field to Rem.Work as instructed, while intentionally leaving the map URL pointed at SRIYOG since that remained the more geographically accurate target for "Open in Maps." (2) A push to `BroadPress/RocketSingh` failed with a 403 immediately after an identical push to a different remote succeeded from the same commit. Resolved by explaining the failure mode precisely — a credentials/permissions issue scoped to that one GitHub repo — rather than suggesting anything be redone on the git side, since nothing about the local commit was actually at fault.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today closed out the country-switcher feature end to end: the real Kamal Pokhari map screenshot is wired in, the address field matches HomeSewa's exact text, and — worth flagging specifically — asking "is this all done?" led to finding that yesterday's static-flag fix only covered the Home screen, with the identical bug still present in the Book, Career, and Partnership forms; all three are fixed now. I also converted yesterday's "timezone only" caveat into a real fix: country auto-detection now tries IP geolocation first and only falls back to timezone if that call fails, so it no longer depends on a device's timezone having been set correctly. Separately, today included some git/GitHub troubleshooting rather than app work — a push to the `BroadPress/RocketSingh` remote is failing with a 403 permissions error even though the identical commit pushed cleanly to the personal fork, which points to a GitHub account/token access issue on that org repo rather than anything in the code; flagging it since it'll need to be resolved on the GitHub side before that remote is up to date.

### Day 67 — 03 August 2026 (Monday)

**Progress:**
HR revisited the Sparrow SMS item that had been closed out as "skip" on Day 65, this time with a narrower and different reason for it: not as a replacement for OTP verification (Firebase Phone Auth already handles that correctly for both `+91` and `+977` numbers, and reopening a decision HR had already made without a new reason would have been the wrong call), but as a transactional notification channel — booking-confirmation and professional-assignment SMS for Nepal numbers, the same fan-out RocketSingh's sibling project HomeSewa already runs through its own `sms_send_log`-backed Edge Function. Since that's a real, previously-nonexistent capability rather than a duplicate of something already working, today was spent planning the implementation properly before writing any code, the same way Day 64's six-item audit was scoped before it was built.

**Auditing what RocketSingh actually has today before designing around Sparrow:** grepped the whole project for any existing SMS-sending code and found none — the only OTP path is Firebase's `signInWithPhoneNumber`, called directly from `BookingOtp.tsx` and `helpboxOTP.tsx`, with no server-side layer in between. More importantly, checked `.env` directly and found `EXPO_PUBLIC_TWILIO_BASE_URL`, `EXPO_PUBLIC_VERIFY_SERVICE_SID`, `EXPO_PUBLIC_INFOBIP_API_KEY`, `EXPO_PUBLIC_INFOBIP_BASE_URL`, and `EXPO_PUBLIC_2FACTOR_API_KEY` — five keys for three different SMS/OTP providers, all unused in the current codebase, and all prefixed `EXPO_PUBLIC_`, meaning every one of them ships bundled inside the compiled app rather than staying server-side. That's a real finding worth flagging on its own: whatever those keys were for, they're currently sitting exposed in a public app bundle for no active feature. It also settled the main design question for Sparrow before writing a line of code — a Sparrow API token must **not** go into `.env` the same way, or it would be extractable from the APK by anyone, the same problem those three leftover keys already have.

**Drafting the actual implementation plan, reusing HomeSewa's proven shape rather than inventing a new one:** RocketSingh already uses Supabase for every other write path (`api/supabase/createBookingSupabase.ts` and siblings), but has no Edge Functions of its own yet — there's no `supabase/functions` directory in this repo at all, unlike HomeSewa. The plan is to add one: a `send-sparrow-sms` Edge Function that reads the Sparrow token from Supabase Vault (not a Function env var, matching why HomeSewa's own Zoho and OTP functions read secrets from Vault rather than `Deno.env` on this account), calls Sparrow's token-based HTTP endpoint server-side, and logs every send to a new `sparrow_sms_log` table keyed by phone number for the same per-day rate-limit pattern HomeSewa uses on `otp_send_log` and `sms_send_log` — so a retry storm or a bug in the calling code can't run up an unbounded SMS bill. Routing is the one piece that doesn't need a new decision at all: `countryData.ts` already has a `dialCode` field distinguishing `+91` (India) from `+977` (Nepal) on every `CountryInfo` record, so the client-side trigger only ever calls the Sparrow function when the active country's `dialCode` is `+977` — which also quietly resolves the open question from Day 64 about whether Sparrow can deliver to Indian numbers, since the plan now never asks it to.

**What's still open before any of this gets built:** whether RocketSingh's Supabase project already has CLI/dashboard access set up for adding Edge Functions (HomeSewa's account needed the Vault workaround because Function secrets don't work there either — worth checking whether RocketSingh's project has the same limitation before assuming it does), and the actual Sparrow account details — API token and the registered sender/from ID — which only HR can provide. Wrote both up as direct questions rather than guessing or blocking today's planning on them.

**Learning:**
The sharpest thing today surfaced wasn't about Sparrow at all — it was that `.env` already holds three unused, fully client-exposed provider keys (Twilio, Infobip, 2Factor) from what were presumably earlier, abandoned attempts at exactly this kind of SMS integration, and none of them were ever moved server-side even though they're inert now. That's a pattern worth remembering going forward on this project specifically: check `.env` for prior art before assuming a third-party integration is being built from a clean slate, and don't repeat the same exposed-secret shape a second time just because it's already the local convention.

**Skills Gained:**
Secrets architecture — recognizing that a working feature (Firebase OTP) and a public secret leak (three unused provider keys) can coexist in the same file, and treating "does this key get used anywhere" as a separate question from "is this key safe to expose" · Third-party gateway integration planning — scoping a new SMS provider around an existing, already-correct piece of routing data (`dialCode`) instead of adding new country-detection logic for the same decision · Cross-project pattern reuse — treating HomeSewa's `sms_send_log`/Vault-secret Edge Function as a reference architecture to copy deliberately, rather than redesigning the same problem from scratch on RocketSingh · Scope discipline — confirming a stakeholder's revised request targets a genuinely new use case before reopening a decision that had already been explicitly closed two days earlier.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Sparrow SMS had already been marked "skip" on Day 65 with HR's explicit sign-off, so building it today without checking further would have meant silently overriding a closed decision. Resolved by confirming the new ask is a different feature entirely (transactional notifications, not OTP) before planning anything, so today's work extends the earlier decision instead of contradicting it. (2) RocketSingh has no backend layer today, and the obvious shortcut — add `EXPO_PUBLIC_SPARROW_API_TOKEN` to `.env` the same way Twilio and Infobip were added before — would reproduce a real, already-present secret-exposure bug rather than fix anything. Resolved by planning a Supabase Edge Function with a Vault-stored secret from the start, using HomeSewa's already-proven version of the same architecture instead of a client-side shortcut.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today was planning only, following up on the Sparrow SMS item from Day 65 now that it's come back with a clearer purpose — transactional booking/assignment SMS for Nepal numbers, not an OTP replacement. Worth flagging directly: auditing `.env` for this turned up three unused, fully client-exposed SMS/OTP provider keys (Twilio, Infobip, 2Factor) left over from what looks like earlier unfinished attempts — inert today, but worth a decision on whether to remove them, since they serve no active feature and sit in the public app bundle regardless. For Sparrow itself, the plan is a new Supabase Edge Function with the token in Vault and a rate-limited send-log table, mirroring HomeSewa's SMS pattern exactly, routed only to `+977` numbers via the `dialCode` field already in `countryData.ts` — so it never touches Indian numbers at all. Two things I need from you before I can start building tomorrow: the actual Sparrow API token and registered sender ID, and confirmation of whether RocketSingh's Supabase project already supports adding Edge Functions or needs that set up first.

### Day 68 — 04 August 2026 (Tuesday)

**Progress:**
HR came back with both open items from Day 65's list — the Sparrow API token and the registered sender ID (`RocketSingh`) — which reopened the Sparrow work, but auditing the actual OTP path before building on top of it overturned yesterday's planning assumption: Firebase Phone Auth was **not** already handling verification correctly, for either country. `signInWithPhoneNumber` was called from `BookingOtp.tsx` and `helpboxOTP.tsx`, but nothing in the actual submit flows (`BookingDetail.tsx`, `NumberBar.tsx`) ever triggered it — both wrote straight to Supabase and skipped past the OTP screens entirely, leaving `globalBookingFirebaseConfirmation` permanently `null`. That changed the scope of today's work from "add a notification channel" to "build the OTP verification RocketSingh never actually had, using Sparrow, for Nepal only."

**Client-side vs. Edge Function — revisiting Day 67's own plan:** Day 67 had explicitly planned to keep the Sparrow token out of `.env`, routing it through a Supabase Edge Function backed by Vault instead, specifically to avoid repeating the exposed-secret pattern already sitting in three unused provider keys. Raised that conflict directly before writing anything. The call made was to ship the client-side version now — token in `.env` as `EXPO_PUBLIC_SPARROW_TOKEN`, called directly from the app — accepting the same bundle-exposure tradeoff as those three leftover keys, rather than standing up Edge Functions + Vault on a Supabase project that doesn't have either configured yet. Documented as a deliberate, informed tradeoff rather than an oversight, so it doesn't get silently "fixed" back to the secure version by mistake later.

**`src/services/sparrowOtpService.ts` — new shared OTP layer:** Built one small service used by both flows: `generateOtp()` (4-digit, matching HomeSewa's OTP box convention), `sendOtp(fullPhone, otp, greetingName?)` which POSTs to Sparrow's SMS endpoint and — this was the specific bug HomeSewa had to patch in after the fact — checks `response.ok` and logs the full response body instead of letting `fetch` silently swallow a non-2xx response, and a tiny in-memory pending-OTP store keyed `'booking' | 'helpbox'` that replaces the two dead `globalBookingFirebaseConfirmation`/`globalFirebaseConfirmation` singletons.

**Wiring Nepal into the booking flow:** `BookingDetail.tsx handleSubmit` now branches on `useCountry()` — Nepal generates and sends an OTP, stores it pending, and routes to `BookingOtp`; India's path is untouched, still a direct Supabase write. `BookingOtp.tsx` was rewritten off Firebase onto the Sparrow store (6 boxes → 4), and picked up a real resend instead of the old "needs to be linked to your custom SMS gateway" stub alert. Rebuilding it also surfaced a payload gap worth fixing while in there: `BookingOtp.tsx`'s booking object never had `city` or `add_photos_picture`, even though `BookingDetail.tsx`'s did — since this screen is now the actual booking-creation path for Nepal, that gap would have silently dropped city and uploaded photos on every Nepal booking.

**Wiring Nepal into the Help-box flow:** Same pattern for `NumberBar.tsx` → `helpboxOTP.tsx`. `NumberBar.tsx`'s Help button never navigated to `helpboxOTP.tsx` at all before today — routed straight to `otpVerifiedHB`. Rewired it to send a Sparrow OTP and navigate to the OTP screen for Nepal, and switched `helpboxOTP.tsx`'s param reading from React Navigation's `useRoute()` to expo-router's `useLocalSearchParams()` to match how `router.push({ params })` actually delivers them — the old code was reading params in a way that was never exercised by any real navigation call.

**Two unrelated bugs found and fixed while testing the Nepal flow end-to-end:** (1) The Select Budget dropdown on `Book.tsx` showed "INR" amounts even with City set to Kathmandu. Added a `currency` field to `CountryInfo` (`INR`/`NPR`), turned the static `budget` array in `Data.ts` into `getBudgetOptions(currency)`, and reset the selected budget on country switch the same way City/Area already did. (2) Phone number inputs across the app (`Book.tsx`, `NumberBar.tsx`, `Partnership.tsx`, and both fields on `Career.tsx`) were hard-coded to India's 3-3-4 digit grouping even for Nepal, which has its own documented 5-2-3 convention (`98520 24 365`) already implemented once, as `formatLocalPhone`, but only for display of the fixed office number — never applied to live typing. Added `formatPhoneInput(cleaned, country)` next to it and replaced all four duplicated inline formatters.

**First real send — IP whitelist, again:** Testing an actual Nepal booking returned the exact failure class flagged as a risk on Day 65/67: `403 Invalid IP Address`, this time from `103.129.135.67`. The token's Allowed IPs list showed a single entry, `0.0.0.0` — which is the literal address, not the CIDR-notation `0.0.0.0/0` needed to mean "any IP." Since a mobile app has no fixed outbound IP, no finite IP list will work here; this needs the portal entry corrected to `0.0.0.0/0` (or the restriction removed outright) before any real OTP can be verified end-to-end. Confirmed this is an account-config issue, not a code bug, and flagged it back rather than trying to work around it client-side.

**Learning:**
A plan written the day before is a hypothesis, not a fact — Day 67's "Firebase already handles OTP correctly" was a reasonable-sounding conclusion from reading the code that called `signInWithPhoneNumber`, but it didn't check whether anything actually reached that call at runtime. Auditing reachability, not just presence, is the part that would have caught it a day earlier.

**Skills Gained:**
Reachability auditing — distinguishing "this function exists and is called correctly" from "this function is ever invoked," which is a different and easy-to-skip check · Deliberate tradeoff documentation — recording a security-vs-speed decision (client-side token) explicitly enough that it reads as a choice, not a bug, to whoever looks at the code next · Payload-parity checking across duplicated code paths — catching that a rebuilt screen (`BookingOtp.tsx`) was missing fields the original submit path (`BookingDetail.tsx`) already had · Country-aware UI conventions — treating currency and phone-number grouping as data tied to the active country the same way `dialCode` and city lists already were, instead of hard-coding one country's convention everywhere.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Yesterday's plan assumed Firebase OTP already worked, which would have made building Sparrow OTP redundant. Resolved by tracing the actual call graph from the submit buttons instead of trusting the presence of `signInWithPhoneNumber` in the file, which is what surfaced that neither `BookingDetail.tsx` nor `NumberBar.tsx` ever triggered it. (2) The Sparrow account hit the same class of IP-whitelist failure HomeSewa hit before, but with a subtler cause than last time — not a missing whitelist entry, but a literal `0.0.0.0` where CIDR `0.0.0.0/0` was intended. Resolved by reading the raw 403 response body (which the new `sendOtp` now always logs, unlike the original `fetch` call it replaces) rather than guessing at the cause.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today turned into a bigger scope change than expected: what Day 67 planned as "add Sparrow as a notification channel, OTP already works" became "build the OTP verification RocketSingh never actually had," once tracing the real code path showed the existing Firebase confirmation was dead code for both countries. Sparrow OTP is now wired for Nepal end-to-end — booking flow and Help-box flow both send a real 4-digit code and verify it before writing to Supabase — while India's flow is untouched. Also fixed two smaller Nepal-specific bugs found while testing (INR showing instead of NPR on the budget field, and phone numbers using India's 3-3-4 grouping instead of Nepal's 5-2-3 convention). One blocker before this can be verified for real: the Sparrow account's Allowed IPs is set to the literal `0.0.0.0` rather than `0.0.0.0/0`, so every real send is still being rejected with a 403 — need this corrected on the Sparrow portal (by HR or whoever has account access) before end-to-end testing can complete.
