### Day 78 — 14 August 2026 (Friday)

**Progress:**
HR's next request looked small on the surface — "make this one phone number a superadmin, and give us an admin table in Supabase like HomeSewa has" — but checking it against the actual data model showed it wasn't a small patch. RocketSingh's internal staff console (career/admin/superadmin) was still running on Firebase Auth plus a Realtime Database role lookup, a completely separate system from the professional phone+PIN accounts built the day before, while HomeSewa's superadmin runs on that same Postgres phone+PIN pattern. Rather than bolt one Postgres row onto a Firebase-authenticated session, flagged the mismatch and confirmed the real ask was a full hard cutover. Planned it properly and executed: new `admin`/`admin_sessions` tables (RLS on, zero policies, service-role only, matching the existing professional-table convention), seven new Edge Functions covering login, PIN change/reset, account creation, approve/reject, status toggling, and logout, ten existing Edge Functions migrated from Firebase-token verification to the new Postgres session token, and a client-side rewrite spanning roughly fifteen files. Kept the two shared role-guard hooks' external return shape unchanged during the rewrite, which meant the eighteen screens that depend on them needed zero edits — only the hooks' internals moved from a Firebase lookup to an AsyncStorage-backed session check. Deleted the old Firebase Auth login code once the new path was verified end-to-end, rather than leaving both running side by side.

Created the requested superadmin account directly through the new system. Got a follow-up request to also store PINs in plaintext in the database "to keep a personal record of who has which PIN" — didn't implement that as literally asked, since a plaintext PIN sitting next to its own bcrypt hash defeats the hash entirely. Explained the risk and asked what the underlying need actually was instead of just refusing outright; it turned out to be an external reference for HR, not an application requirement, which the existing "show the PIN once at creation/reset time" pattern already covers without weakening anything in the database.

Next request was local build instructions for an APK — instructions only, not an actual build. Read the real Android project config and found a genuine gap: the release build type still points at the debug keystore rather than the real one already sitting in the repo, so `assembleRelease` as configured would silently produce a debug-signed artifact. Gave both paths (quick debug build, and the release-signing gap that would need to be closed with the keystore's own alias/passwords before a real release build could happen locally). When the release build was actually attempted, it failed mid-compile with an NDK out-of-memory crash. Diagnosed properly instead of guessing at a quick fix: the machine has 8 GB of RAM total, and `gradle.properties` was set to build all four CPU architectures in parallel on only a 2 GB/512 MB JVM heap — each architecture spawns its own native C++ compile job, and that was genuinely enough to exhaust available memory (the NDK's `clang++` crashed with an out-of-memory error, `ninja` crashed separately with an unrelated exception, and the Gradle daemon itself logged a metaspace warning, all in the same failed run). Fixed it by raising the heap to 3 GB/1 GB metaspace and restricting local builds to just `arm64-v8a` — confirmed via `build.gradle`'s own `ndkVersion` reference that the project only actually needs one of the five NDK versions installed on the machine — since `arm64-v8a` covers virtually every real device anyway.

Tried `eas build --local` next and hit "Unsupported platform, macOS or Linux required," since local EAS builds don't run natively on Windows. Explained that limitation and pointed at the cloud build path instead, since the `production` profile in `eas.json` already builds an app bundle without any local signing setup — EAS manages the release keystore itself. The first cloud attempt failed with `ENOSPC` while uploading the project tarball. Traced it to the actual constrained drive rather than assuming the project's own drive was the problem: the project lives on `D:` with 200+ GB free, but EAS stages its upload through a temp folder on `C:`, which had only 2.85 GB free. Surveyed what was actually consuming space instead of guessing, found roughly 11 GB in the global Gradle cache and 21 GB in the Android SDK folder, broke the SDK folder down further to separate genuinely unused pieces (four old NDK versions the project's own `build.gradle` doesn't reference) from ones still in active use, got explicit sign-off on scope before deleting anything, and cleared the Gradle and npm caches for a real ~10.7 GB recovered — enough headroom for the upload to succeed.

Got a request to stop the navigation drawer from scrolling and make it "like HomeSewa." First pass just swapped the `ScrollView` for a plain `View`, which fixed the literal scrolling symptom but not the actual design — a fixed-item list with no scroll fallback can still clip on a small screen. When asked to look at HomeSewa's drawer "properly," found HomeSewa's real mobile app repository on the same machine (distinct from a HomeSewa marketing website with a similar folder name) and read its actual `CustomDrawer` component instead of guessing at intent from the name alone. The real mechanism wasn't "no ScrollView," it was a fixed-height card sized to 90% of the screen with the menu laid out via `justifyContent: 'space-evenly'` so items self-distribute across whatever height is available, plus icon-chip menu items and a login/logout control pulled out of the list into its own bottom bar. Rewrote RocketSingh's drawer to match that same structural approach while keeping RocketSingh's own role model, session logic, and brand color untouched.

Last request was to make sure the header's icons look identical on every page. Rather than assume a single shared header component covered the whole app, audited every header component that exists and found five, only one of which had actually been brought up to the clean, no-background-pill icon style done the day before — the other three still had the older 28px/41px icons sitting inside bordered, shadowed pill buttons. Normalized all three to the same plain 40×40 button treatment used by the reference header, so every screen that renders a header — home, services, booking, help-box, and every admin screen — now shows the same icon styling with no per-page changes required, since they all just consume the shared components.

**Learning:**
A request phrased narrowly ("make this number a superadmin") can carry a much larger implied scope once checked against the actual data model instead of the words alone — and the same pattern showed up twice today in smaller form: "stop the drawer from scrolling" read literally produced a worse design than actually going and reading the reference implementation the user meant. Checking the real target before writing code is worth the extra step even when the request sounds simple.

**Skills Gained:**
Recognizing when a narrowly-worded request implies a full system migration rather than a small patch, by checking it against the real data model before writing any code · designing and shipping a hard cutover between two authentication systems (Firebase Auth/RTDB to Postgres phone+PIN) without breaking any of the eighteen screens that depend on the role-guard hooks sitting in front of it · holding a line on a security regression (plaintext PIN storage) by asking for the underlying need instead of either silently complying or flatly refusing · diagnosing a native build out-of-memory crash to its real cause (parallel multi-architecture compilation against an undersized JVM heap on an 8 GB machine) instead of treating the first error message as the whole story · tracing a disk-space failure to the drive where the tool actually stages its work rather than the drive the project happens to live on · reading a sibling codebase's actual source instead of guessing at a design from its name or a screenshot, and reproducing its real structural mechanism instead of a surface-level imitation · auditing every instance of a duplicated component across a codebase before declaring a visual-consistency fix complete.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The Android release build failed with a cryptic NDK out-of-memory crash with no obvious single cause in the log. Resolved by cross-referencing the machine's actual total RAM against the build's configured architecture count and JVM heap size, which showed the real problem was parallel multi-architecture native compilation on a memory budget that was never sized for it, not a broken toolchain. (2) "Make the drawer like HomeSewa" could have been satisfied with a much shallower fix (just removing the scroll), but that wouldn't have matched what was actually being asked for. Resolved by locating and reading HomeSewa's real drawer implementation on disk before rewriting anything, rather than inferring its design from the name of the request.

**Message to Mentor:**
A wide-ranging day. HR's ask to make one phone number a superadmin turned out to require finishing what Day 77 started — a full cutover of RocketSingh's internal staff logins from Firebase to the same Postgres phone+PIN system the professional accounts now use, which is done and verified end-to-end, including the requested admin table. Pushed back on one follow-up request (storing PINs in plaintext) rather than complying, and resolved it a safer way once the real need was clear. Spent a good part of the day on build tooling: gave local APK/AAB build instructions, then diagnosed and fixed a real out-of-memory crash in the local Android build (the dev machine's memory budget couldn't support building all four CPU architectures at once), and separately diagnosed and fixed an EAS cloud-build upload failure that traced back to the system drive being nearly full rather than anything wrong with the project itself. Closed the day with two UI-consistency fixes — rebuilt the navigation drawer to actually match HomeSewa's real implementation (found and read its source directly instead of guessing) rather than just silencing the scroll symptom, and audited every header component in the app to bring three that had been missed up to the same icon styling as the one already fixed.

### Day 79 — 15 August 2026 (Saturday)

**Progress:**
Today was dedicated to testing the real app rather than writing new features — putting yesterday's admin auth cutover, drawer rewrite, and header normalization in front of an actual device build instead of trusting that a clean diff meant a clean app. Bumped `versionCode` from 18 to 20 and reverted `android/gradle.properties` back to building all four architectures (`armeabi-v7a`, `arm64-v8a`, `x86`, `x86_64`) instead of just `arm64-v8a`, since a real test pass needs to cover the actual range of devices the app ships to, not just the fast local target used for build-debugging the day before.

Rather than only clicking through the app by hand, went through the code paths touched by yesterday's migration line by line, looking specifically for the kind of bug that a quick manual tap-through wouldn't surface — silent failures with no error and no crash. Found two.

First, in `toggle-customer-block/index.ts`, the block/unblock action available from the Customers tab writes `blocked_by: session.uid` — but the admin session object returned by the shared `verifyAdminSession()` helper only ever carries `phone` and `role`; there is no `uid` field anywhere in the new Postgres session system (every other edge function written during the migration correctly reads `session.phone`). The block itself still succeeds, so a tester tapping "block" sees exactly the expected result and no error. What's actually broken is invisible at the UI layer: the `blocked_by` audit column silently gets written as `null` every time, so there is no record of which superadmin blocked a given customer. Confirmed this is the only `.uid` reference anywhere in the `supabase/functions` tree, and that it wouldn't be caught by `tsc` because `supabase/functions` sits outside the app's `tsconfig.json`.

Second, and more consequential: in `BookingDetails_2.tsx`, marking a job "Completed" shows two optional fields — Deal Amount and Deal Note — which only render when `workStatus === 'Completed'`. But `handleSubmit` checks for the Completed status first and immediately routes to `WorkCompletionOTP` with `router.push(...)` followed by `return`, before the function ever reaches the line that reads `dealAmount`/`dealNote` into the payload sent to Supabase. `WorkCompletionOTP.tsx`, the screen it navigates to, has no reference to deal amount or note anywhere in it. A staff member can type a deal amount, add a note, tap through the OTP confirmation to mark the job complete, and the values are simply discarded — no error, no warning, the job completes normally. Per the code's own comment, recording a deal amount is what's supposed to trigger the Zoho invoice sync added in yesterday's professional-marketplace work, which means that sync can never actually fire from the normal "mark job completed" flow — the exact path it was built for.

Beyond those two, went back through the rest of the migration surface specifically to rule out the failure modes a real device test would be expected to catch: re-checked both shared role-guard hooks against all eighteen dependent screens for a missing `if (!authorized) return null` guard on any code path (none found), and re-checked all five header components for any that still carried the old bordered/shadowed icon-pill style instead of the normalized 40×40 button (none found — all five are now consistent). Ran `npx tsc --noEmit` across the app itself, which came back clean, confirming both real bugs found today live in places type-checking structurally can't reach: one is a field-name mismatch in an untyped edge function outside the app's `tsconfig.json`, the other is a control-flow `return` that is completely valid TypeScript but silently skips business logic.

**Learning:**
A clean TypeScript compile and a bug-free manual tap-through are both necessary and not sufficient — today's two real bugs were each invisible to one of those checks specifically because they don't throw: a wrong field name on an untyped Supabase Edge Function just writes `null` instead of failing, and an early `return` that skips a payload field is completely valid control flow that no compiler flags. The only way to catch either was reading the actual data path end to end — where a value is captured, where it's supposed to be sent, and confirming it's still there at the point it's used — rather than confirming the screen "looks right" or that the build "compiles clean."

**Skills Gained:**
Testing a fresh authentication/data migration by tracing individual field values through their full life cycle (input → local state → network payload → database write) rather than relying on UI smoke-testing or type-checking alone · recognizing that `tsconfig.json` exclusions (like a `supabase/functions` directory sitting outside the app's compiler scope) create a real blind spot where type errors like a wrong field name go undetected · spotting an early-return control-flow bug that silently drops form state gathered earlier in the same function · distinguishing a silent-failure bug (succeeds with wrong side effects, no error surfaced) from a crash, since the former is invisible to both the tester and the compiler and only shows up by reading the code.

**Challenge & Solution:**
Neither of today's two real bugs produced any error, crash, or visibly wrong UI — both looked, from a tester's perspective, like the feature working correctly. Resolved by not stopping at "does tapping this button do the right thing on screen" and instead reading each new/changed function's actual data flow against what the rest of the migration expected it to do — comparing `toggle-customer-block`'s field name against the `AdminSession` type used everywhere else in the migration surfaced the `.uid`/`.phone` mismatch, and reading `handleSubmit` top-to-bottom instead of assuming the `Completed` branch reached the same code as every other status surfaced the discarded deal-amount payload.

**Message to Mentor:**
Spent today testing yesterday's admin-auth cutover and UI fixes on a real multi-architecture build rather than assuming the passing compile and clean diff meant the app was actually correct — bumped the version and restored the full four-architecture build for proper device coverage. Manual testing alone wouldn't have caught either issue found today, since both are silent: `toggle-customer-block`'s edge function writes `blocked_by: session.uid`, which doesn't exist on the admin session object and is always `null`, so every customer block/unblock loses its audit trail with no visible error. Separately, `BookingDetails_2.tsx` collects a Deal Amount and Deal Note when marking a job Completed, but the function returns to the OTP confirmation screen before it ever reads those two fields, so they're silently thrown away — which also means the Zoho invoice sync from yesterday's work can never fire from the normal completion flow it was built for. Re-verified the rest of yesterday's migration (role-guard hooks on all 18 screens, all 5 headers, a full `tsc --noEmit` pass) came back clean, so these two are flagged as the concrete fixes still needed before the next release build.

---

### Day 80 — 16 August 2026 (Sunday)

**Progress:**
Today started as a single bug report — the HomeSewa partnership form wasn't working — and turned into a full audit of SMS/OTP delivery across all three projects. Didn't take the report at face value: pulled up the actual code path first (form submit → OTP screen → `send-otp` → Sparrow) before guessing at a cause. First real finding was migration drift on HomeSewa's Supabase project — two migrations existed locally but had never actually been applied to the remote database, confirmed with `supabase migration list` rather than assuming the local repo matched what was live, and pushed them. That turned out not to be the actual bug, since `send-otp`'s Sparrow call had no response check at all — it could fail silently and still report success to the app. Fixed that check, redeployed, and the very first real test after the fix exposed the actual problem: Sparrow was rejecting every request from Supabase's outbound IP with an explicit "Invalid IP Address" error, even though HR's dashboard screenshot showed the token's allowlist set to `0.0.0.0/0`. Confirmed the contradiction was real rather than a stale screenshot by hitting Sparrow directly from two different Supabase invocations and getting two different rejected IPs both times — proof the block was IP-range-based, not a simple missing whitelist entry a single fix could patch.

With HR's account-side options exhausted for the moment, worked through the actual tradeoffs before picking a fix rather than jumping at the first workaround: calling Sparrow straight from the client would dodge the IP block but put the SMS token in the app bundle and undo the OTP brute-force hardening from earlier in the week; a relay server would keep the server-side security intact but was real infrastructure to stand up on a guess that Sparrow's IP block was even fixable that way. Laid out both options plainly, including what each one actually cost, and let that decision get made deliberately rather than defaulting to whichever was easiest to build. Once client-side delivery was chosen, kept the design narrow: the server still does 100% of the rate-limiting and, for security-sensitive purposes, the code generation and hashing — only the final Sparrow HTTP call moved to the client. Verified that split held by testing the full loop for real: requested a code, delivered it through Sparrow from outside Supabase's network, and confirmed `verify-otp` accepted the exact code that arrived by SMS.

Applied the identical pattern to RocketSingh and NEPAL Motor rather than treating HomeSewa's fix as one-off. RocketSingh needed more care — it has two different OTP trust models (client-generated codes for booking/helpbox vs. server-generated-and-hashed codes for PIN reset/work-completion) plus a separate SMS path that delivers an actual login PIN on account approval, not just a verification code — so the admin's own client, not the recipient's, ends up doing that delivery. Got the real Sparrow token from HR rather than guessing or reusing HomeSewa's, since each app has its own account-level token, and set it in both the local `.env` and EAS's cloud environment variables so builds pick it up. NEPAL Motor turned out to be the deepest one: digging into the actual OTP code before applying the same fix showed it had never been deployed at all — every Sparrow-related file in the separate `live-nepalmotor-com` backend was untracked in git. Fixed it anyway using the same client-delivery pattern (this one is stateless — no database, a code derived from phone + secret + time window — so handing it back to the client for delivery cost nothing extra in risk), verified the whole loop locally with a real SMS, then had to get it actually shipped: a force-pushed, history-rewritten remote (very likely from HR purging a leaked `.env.local` out of git history) turned a plain `git pull` into a real rebase conflict. Rather than fight through conflicts on commits that were probably already superseded by newer work on the remote, cherry-picked just the one commit that mattered onto the fresh history and pushed that — clean, no lost work either side.

Found one more thing worth stopping for rather than declaring the day done at "pushed": the live NEPAL Motor deploy pipeline turned out to be Cloudflare Pages, not Vercel like the code's own comments claimed, and its build is currently failing in zero seconds — a config-level failure, not a code one. No Cloudflare access from here to see the actual log, so left it clearly flagged rather than guessing at a fix blind. Also flagged a real operational risk before calling any of this "done": neither HomeSewa nor RocketSingh has OTA updates configured, so the server-side change is already live while the Play Store binaries are still on the old client code — meaning current installs now get a false "success" response with no SMS ever arriving, which is worse than the error they showed before today. That needs a real release, not just a merged fix, before it's actually resolved for existing users.

**Learning:**
A bug report ("the form doesn't work") and its actual root cause can be several layers apart, and each layer looked like the answer until tested — migration drift looked like the fix, then a missing response check looked like the fix, and the real cause (an IP-range block that contradicts the account's own dashboard settings) only showed up because every fix along the way got verified with a real request instead of assumed correct from the code alone. Also learned that "fixed and deployed" isn't the same as "fixed for users" when a client-side change is involved — a server-side fix can go live immediately while every existing app install is still running the old client, and in this case that gap actively made things worse for current users rather than just leaving them unfixed.

**Skills Gained:**
Diagnosing a multi-layered bug by verifying each candidate cause with a real test instead of stopping at the first plausible one · reasoning through a genuine security tradeoff (server-side secrecy vs. an unfixable IP block) and presenting the real cost of each option rather than picking one silently · designing a fix that moves only the minimum necessary piece (SMS delivery) to the client while keeping every security-relevant piece (rate limits, code hashing, verification) server-side · applying the same fix consistently across three differently-architected codebases instead of re-solving it from scratch each time · recovering from a force-pushed, history-rewritten remote by cherry-picking the one relevant commit instead of fighting a full rebase through unrelated conflicts · recognizing and flagging a deployment gap (no OTA, old Play Store binaries) that made the live situation worse, not better, until a real release ships.

**Challenge & Solution:**
The hardest part wasn't any single fix — it was that Sparrow's own account dashboard and its live API behavior directly contradicted each other (`0.0.0.0/0` allowed, but every request still rejected by IP), with HR's own support channel insisting the setting should work. Resolved by not treating either side as automatically right: reproduced the rejection live, multiple times, from multiple Supabase invocations, and used that concrete evidence — two different rejected IPs — to explain why a simple allowlist fix couldn't be the actual answer, rather than continuing to wait on an account-side setting that had already been shown not to be the real constraint.

**Message to Mentor:**
Started from a single "partnership form isn't working" report on HomeSewa and traced it through migration drift, then a silent Sparrow-failure bug, to the real cause: Sparrow blocking Supabase's outbound IP entirely, contradicting the account's own dashboard settings. Laid out the real tradeoff between fixing it client-side versus standing up a relay server, and after client-side delivery was chosen, redesigned `send-otp`/`send-sms` so the server keeps all the security-relevant work (rate limits, code hashing, verification) and only the final SMS send moves to the client. Verified the full loop with real SMS delivery, then applied the same pattern to RocketSingh (which has two different OTP trust models plus an admin-side PIN-delivery path) and NEPAL Motor, where I discovered the OTP feature had never actually been deployed at all. Got NEPAL Motor's fix live despite a force-pushed remote history by cherry-picking just the relevant commit instead of fighting a full rebase. One piece is still open — NEPAL Motor's Cloudflare Pages build is failing and I don't have access to see why — and I flagged a real risk on the other two apps: without an OTA update mechanism, existing Play Store installs won't get this fix until a new build actually ships, and until then they're worse off than before, not just unfixed.

---

### Day 81 — 18 August 2026 (Tuesday)

**Progress:**
Today started small — HR asked for RocketSingh's admin login to match HomeSewa's convention of a 4-digit PIN instead of 6 — and turned into the largest single-day audit-and-fix pass of the internship so far. The PIN-length change touched more surface than it looked: the login screen itself, the forgot-PIN and change-PIN flows, both staff account-creation screens, and two Supabase Edge Functions that independently enforce the 6-digit format server-side, all updated to 4 and consistently deployed.

Next, asked to check the Lead Fee (Khalti) payment flow and the Zoho invoice sync "from beginning to end." The Khalti side held up under close reading — session-gated Edge Functions that re-verify the real payment status and amount with Khalti before ever recording an unlock, real unique constraints backing the idempotency instead of just a check-then-insert, and server-side masking of customer contact details until paid. The Zoho side did not: tracing the actual trigger condition (a booking's `deal_amount` transitioning from null to a value) back to where it's supposed to be set showed it could never fire. `BookingDetails_2.tsx` only renders the Deal Amount/Deal Note fields when the status is already "Completed," but its own submit handler routes away to `WorkCompletionOTP.tsx` on that exact status before ever reading those two fields into the update — and `WorkCompletionOTP.tsx` itself has no reference to either field. Fixed it by threading the two values through as route params and writing them in a follow-up update right after the completion claim succeeds, restoring the actual trigger path the sync was built for.

That result prompted a full "is there anything else broken" request, scoped to the whole project. Rather than review everything alone, split the remaining surface — the rest of the Edge Functions, the admin screens, the customer/professional-facing screens, and the data-access/migrations layer — across four parallel research passes, then personally re-verified every finding rated High or above against the actual live code and, where practical, the live database, rather than trusting a sub-agent's claim at face value. That verification step caught real things: one claimed finding turned out to already be self-documented as an intentionally deferred limitation, not a hidden bug, and confirming a database-level claim required actually reading the relevant migration's SQL rather than its comment.

The audit surfaced upwards of twenty real, verified issues. Fixed the three most severe first: `send-notification` decided whether to require a staff session purely from a boolean the *caller* supplied, meaning anyone holding the app's own public key could broadcast a push to every installed device with no login at all; the `customers` table's RLS policy let any caller overwrite any other customer's row despite its own comment claiming it was scoped to "your own row"; and the `claim_booking_status` database function — callable directly with the anon key — had no caller-identity check inside it whatsoever. Rewrote all three, wrote two new migrations, and pushed everything to the live project. Live-testing the fix immediately caught a bug of my own making: `admin_sessions.token` is a `uuid` column, and the new check compared it against the incoming session token as plain text with no cast, so every single call to `claim_booking_status` — meaning every real job completion — was failing outright. Caught it with a direct database probe before it reached a real completion, shipped a follow-up migration casting the value safely, and re-verified with both a rejected-invalid-token test and an accepted-valid-token test run directly against production.

Went on to fix the remaining eleven High-severity findings the same day: a superadmin "Reject" action on a professional's pending profile-update request was applying the disputed data anyway, just under a Rejected label; booking navigation between screens resolved to an array index rather than the real booking id whenever the Firebase-era `id`/`push_id` fields it fell back to were absent, meaning staff could end up looking at or completing the wrong booking if sort order shifted between two independent fetches; a "Photos" section was showing five hardcoded stock images for every booking instead of the customer's real uploads, which the fetch already returned but nothing read; a customer-block action was writing `session.uid` — a field that doesn't exist on the session object — instead of `session.phone`, silently losing the block's audit trail every time; a professional's leads screen was converting any real API failure into an indistinguishable "no leads right now"; six approve/reject/status-toggle Edge Functions shared a check-then-act race letting a double-tap generate two different valid-looking PINs for one account; the OTP send-rate-limit could be reset by simply padding the same phone number with an extra digit; and a workforce profile-update could silently orphan itself with a null foreign key if the write it depended on quietly matched zero rows. Deployed all ten touched Edge Functions, including two left over from the morning's PIN-length change that had been edited but never actually redeployed.

Closed the day on the remaining Medium/Low findings: wired up banner editing (the button had been showing "coming soon" and discarding all edits despite the real update function already existing, unused, in the same file), gave BookingDetails_1's "Cancel" button an actual database write instead of just navigating away with the booking left untouched, added a double-submit guard to all eight of UserManagement's approve/reject/block/role-change actions, closed a pre-PIN-check phone-number enumeration gap in professional login by moving the account-status check to after PIN verification instead of before it, fixed a staff role-change that silently reported success even against a nonexistent account id, added a resend/back option to both PIN-reset flows that previously dead-ended if the code expired mid-flow, and rebuilt `ViewNotifications.tsx` — previously pure hardcoded scaffolding with a filter that did nothing — into a real screen backed by a new `list-notifications` Edge Function, since the underlying table's row-level security has no policy allowing the client to read it directly at all. Added a defensive row cap to two previously-unbounded full-table fetches and removed two files confirmed unreferenced anywhere in the codebase. Verified the whole day's work with a clean `tsc --noEmit`, a full redeploy of every changed Edge Function, and direct live tests against the production project for the highest-risk changes — a padded-phone rate-limit probe, a professional-login regression check, and a real fetch through the new notifications endpoint — rather than treating a "Deployed Functions" message as proof of correctness on its own.

**Learning:**
The single sharpest moment of the day was catching a bug in my own fix within minutes of shipping it — a `uuid`-vs-`text` comparison that would have quietly broken every job completion in production — purely because the habit from earlier in the internship (verify a deploy against the live system, don't just trust the success message) is now automatic rather than optional. The Zoho investigation was a smaller version of the same lesson: the sync function itself was well-built, and the actual break was one screen away, in a field that was collected but never reached the code that would use it — a reminder that "is this feature broken" often means tracing a value's full path rather than reviewing the piece that looks most relevant.

**Skills Gained:**
Parallelizing a full-project audit across independent research passes and then personally re-verifying each severity-ranked claim against live code and a live database before acting on it, rather than trusting a first-pass finding at face value · designing a minimal, zero-regression-risk server-side gate (`send-notification`) for a case where the fully correct fix would have required redesigning the function's entire contract, and being explicit about exactly what the narrower fix does and doesn't close · catching a self-introduced production bug through the same live-verification discipline used to catch other people's bugs, rather than assuming a just-shipped fix is correct because it deployed cleanly · replacing a check-then-act race with an atomic, conditioned database write (`.eq('status', expected)` plus an affected-row check) across six structurally identical functions consistently rather than fixing one and leaving the rest · recognizing when a security fix (moving an account-status check after PIN verification) actually improves legitimate user experience rather than trading security for usability · scoping a database RLS fix to match this codebase's own established pattern (a narrow `SECURITY DEFINER` RPC) instead of inventing a new one.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The `send-notification` broadcast gap had no single clean fix — the function accepts a nearly-arbitrary payload, and the narrow, legitimate no-session call sites turned out to share almost the exact same shape as the dangerous ones (a "notify all admins" alert triggered by a real customer inquiry looks structurally identical to a staff-only "broadcast to all admins" button). Resolved by scoping the fix to the two payload shapes — `included_segments` and a `not_exists` filter — that are never used by any legitimate no-session caller, closing the worst vectors with zero regression risk, and being explicit in both the code comment and the report that a fully airtight fix would need a larger redesign. (2) Shipping a fix and then immediately breaking the exact feature it was meant to protect (`claim_booking_status`) was only caught because the live-verification step wasn't skipped even though the migration had already succeeded. Resolved by treating "the migration applied" and "the function actually works" as two separate claims requiring two separate proofs, exactly as flagged as a lesson on Day 79.

**Message to Mentor:**
What started as a small PIN-length change turned into the biggest audit day of the internship: found and fixed a genuine unauthenticated mass-broadcast path in the push-notification system, an overly-broad database write policy, and a database function with no caller-identity check at all — all three deployed and verified live, including catching and fixing a bug I introduced in my own first fix (a type mismatch that briefly broke every job completion) before it could affect a real booking. Went on to fix eleven further High-severity issues (a "Reject" action that applied the rejected change anyway, a booking-navigation bug that could point staff at the wrong booking, fake stock photos standing in for real customer uploads, a silently-lost audit field, a masked-error leads screen, a six-function PIN-generation race, an OTP rate-limit bypass, and an orphanable profile-update write) plus eight Medium/Low items (dead-end banner editing, a no-op Cancel button, missing double-submit guards across User Management, a phone-number enumeration gap, a silent no-op role change, missing resend/back controls on two PIN-reset flows, and a fully non-functional notifications screen rebuilt with a real backend). Every Edge Function touched today is redeployed and, for the highest-risk changes, tested directly against the live project rather than assumed correct from a clean deploy message. Also gave a proper commit message for pushing today's work to GitHub, at your request, without pushing it myself since you wanted to run that step directly.

---

### Day 82 — 19 August 2026 (Wednesday)

**Progress:**
Started by closing out the two items Day 81's audit had deliberately left open rather than rushing. First, the `blocked_customers` list had never actually been enforced at booking time — its own migration comment admitted as much. Rather than route the fix through a client change, added a `BEFORE INSERT` trigger directly on `booking` so the block is enforced at the database layer regardless of how a row gets inserted, matching this project's recurring lesson that a client-side check alone isn't real enforcement. Verified it live rather than trusting the migration's success message: blocked a throwaway test number, confirmed the insert was rejected with a clear message, confirmed an unblocked number still inserted normally, then cleaned up both test rows.

Second, and more consequential: a past audit had flagged an unverified inconsistency in how phone numbers were sent to Sparrow for SMS — some paths prefixed the number with Nepal's `977` country code, others sent a bare 10-digit number, and nobody had confirmed which was actually correct. Rather than guess or leave it deferred again, pulled Sparrow's own official API documentation directly. Every single example shown — the curl reference, the PHP/Python/C# snippets — used a bare 10-digit number; none used a country-code prefix. That flipped the original assumption: the booking and helpbox OTP flows, which build a `977`-prefixed number, were the ones out of step with Sparrow's documented contract, not the PIN-delivery paths. Since every customer booking or help-request submission goes through that OTP step, this was a real, live delivery risk for actual users, not a style inconsistency. Traced every place the OTP "phone" value is used internally first to confirm nothing else depended on its format (verification only ever compares the entered code, never the phone), then fixed both call sites to send the bare 10-digit form Sparrow actually documents.

With those closed, ran a second full-project "any bugs left" pass, deliberately scoped to what the prior audits hadn't touched — the `components/` directory (25 reusable UI components, never independently reviewed before) and the remaining utility/data files — plus a third, skeptical pass whose only job was to find something wrong with the fixes made in the last two sessions rather than re-confirm they were fine. That last pass earned its place: it found that Day 81's `send-notification` broadcast-gap fix, while correct as far as it went, hadn't covered every forgeable payload shape — a bare single-role filter (the exact shape two legitimate, already-session-gated broadcast functions use) could still reach every customer or every admin device with no session at all. Closed that gap, reasoning carefully through every no-session caller's real payload shape first to be sure the fix couldn't misfire against legitimate traffic, then verified live with a safe negative test (a rejected call never reaches the push provider, so no real notification risk). The same pass also caught `WorkCompletionOTP.tsx` treating a session-expiry error and a genuine "someone else already completed this booking" conflict as the identical case — a professional whose session lapsed mid-completion was being told their booking was already handled by someone else, when in fact nothing had been saved at all, with no accurate path to retry.

The components audit turned up its own finding worth fixing on the spot: `Header3drawer.tsx`, used across seven customer-facing screens, has a right-side icon button styled and positioned identically to the one other header components use for a WhatsApp-contact link — except its `onPress` had never been wired up at all. Compared it against the working version in `Header2.tsx` and wired it the same way. The same pass also surfaced a genuinely significant bug outside its original brief: `CountryContext` initializes to `'india'` synchronously and only resolves the real country asynchronously afterward (an AsyncStorage read, or up to a 3-second IP lookup on a fresh install), while the Nepal-only gate used by the entire professional marketplace reacts to that placeholder default the instant it renders. Any Nepal professional opening the app straight into a lead-payment screen — exactly what a "new lead" push notification does — was being redirected out before their real country was ever known. Fixed it by tracking whether detection has actually resolved and holding the redirect until it has, rather than reacting to the placeholder value.

**Learning:**
The Sparrow phone-format issue was the clearest example yet of the difference between "looks inconsistent" and "is broken" — a static read of the code alone couldn't settle which of the two formats was correct, and guessing wrong in either direction (fixing the paths that were actually fine, leaving the actually-broken one alone) would have shipped confidently on the wrong assumption. Going to the vendor's own documentation instead of reasoning from internal precedent was the only way to actually know. The country-detection race was a similar shape at a smaller scale: the bug wasn't in any single line, it was in the gap between "state has a default value" and "state has resolved to a real value," which is invisible unless you specifically ask what happens to every consumer of that state during the window before it's confirmed.

**Skills Gained:**
Enforcing a business rule at the database layer via a trigger rather than a client check, so it holds regardless of how a row actually gets inserted · resolving an unverified format-inconsistency finding by consulting the third-party vendor's own documentation directly instead of picking a side from internal precedent, then tracing every internal consumer of the changed value before touching it, to confirm nothing else depended on its exact shape · running a third audit pass whose explicit purpose was adversarial toward my own recent work, distinct from the first two passes' purpose of finding new issues — and having it earn its keep by finding a real, still-open gap in a fix I'd already shipped · distinguishing an initial/default value from a resolved value in a shared context, and recognizing that any consumer reacting to state before it's confirmed resolved can produce a real, live bug even though the code "looks" correct · verifying a security-fix extension safely in production by constructing a test whose failure mode (a clean rejection) is provably harmless, rather than testing the success path in a way that would have required tolerating a real side effect (an actual push notification).

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The phone-format question couldn't be resolved by reading this codebase alone — both formats were already "live" in the sense that each had shipped and presumably been used, so there was no internal signal for which one was actually correct. Resolved by checking Sparrow's documentation directly rather than trusting whichever format seemed more common in the code, which turned out to contradict the initial assumption. (2) Verifying the extended `send-notification` fix safely meant proving a forged request now gets rejected, without ever letting a similarly-shaped legitimate request actually complete, since doing so would mean sending a real push to real devices just to test. Resolved by testing only the rejection path directly (a 401 response is proof the code never reaches the push provider) and relying on careful shape-by-shape reasoning, checked against every real call site in the codebase, for the acceptance path instead of a live test.

**Message to Mentor:**
Closed out both items flagged as open from yesterday's audit: the customer block list is now actually enforced at booking time via a database trigger (verified live with a real blocked-and-unblocked test), and — this one matters — the phone-number format used for customer OTP delivery (booking and help-request confirmation) was checked against Sparrow's real API documentation and corrected; the previous format was contrary to Sparrow's own documented contract and was a live risk to real OTP delivery, not just a style issue. Ran a further audit pass afterward, this time including a dedicated adversarial review of my own last two sessions' fixes rather than only looking for new problems, and it found two real gaps worth closing: the notification-broadcast security fix from yesterday hadn't covered every forgeable payload shape (now closed and verified live with a side-effect-free test), and a booking-completion screen was telling professionals their job was "already updated by someone else" when the real cause was an expired session — misleading and with no accurate retry path. Also found and fixed a dead button on a header component used across seven screens, and a more serious one outside any of today's specific asks: the whole professional marketplace's Nepal-only gate was reacting to a placeholder default value before real country detection had resolved, meaning a Nepal professional opening the app straight into a lead-payment screen from a push notification could get redirected away before ever seeing it. One open item I'd like your input on rather than deciding myself: the "Remember me" checkbox on Admin Login has no wiring behind it at all and no session-duration concept exists server-side for it to control — cheapest honest fix is removing it, but let me know if you'd rather it get real behavior instead.

### Day 83 — 22 August 2026 (Saturday)

**Progress:**
Today combined a full functional audit with three focused follow-up fixes. Asked to verify the whole app "from the beginning to end" — form submission, SMS, OTP, and login — using a real phone number, started by trying to actually run the app in a browser rather than reading the code and assuming it worked, since a static reading can't confirm an SMS actually arrives. The web dev server wouldn't even bundle: `BookingDetail.tsx` and `BookingOtp.tsx` both had a static top-level `import { OneSignal } from 'react-native-onesignal'`, which throws at module-load time outside a native runtime and was crashing Metro's entire web bundle before a single screen could render — every other file in the codebase already lazy-`require()`s OneSignal inside a try/catch for exactly this reason, so brought both files in line with that pattern. With the bundle fixed, installed Playwright to drive a real headless Chromium session against the running app instead of guessing from the code, and used it to actually click through the booking form, submit it, and capture the live network traffic. That surfaced two more real, unfixed problems specific to the web build: `@react-native-community/datetimepicker` has no web implementation at all, so the "Choose Date" field silently does nothing and blocks the booking form from ever being submittable on web; and the client-side Sparrow SMS call from Day 80's IP-block fix is rejected by the browser's own CORS policy (Sparrow's API sends no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` header), so Nepal OTP delivery is completely broken on web specifically, even though it works correctly on native. Rather than stop at "web is broken," proved the actual OTP/SMS pipeline still works by replicating the exact same `send-otp` → Sparrow call from a plain Node script instead of a browser (Node's fetch isn't subject to CORS, matching how the native app behaves) — it queued a real SMS to the test number successfully, with a real message ID and credit deduction confirmed in Sparrow's own response. Verified the database side the same direct way, inserting a real booking through the same Supabase path the app uses and confirming it landed. Also drove the real Admin Login and Professional Login screens end-to-end and confirmed both submit to their respective Edge Functions and get back correct, real responses (a 401 with a live rate-limit counter for a bad admin PIN, a clean invalid-credentials rejection for the professional login).

A follow-up screenshot of the Booking OTP screen asked for the header to match the homepage on every OTP screen. Checked all of them rather than just the one screenshotted, and found `helpboxOTP.tsx` and `otpVerifiedHB.tsx` were both importing `Header3drawer` but aliasing it locally as `Header2` — which made the mismatch invisible from the JSX alone. `Header3drawer` is missing the country-flag toggle entirely and uses a different WhatsApp icon asset than the real `Header2` used everywhere else. Repointed both imports to the real component and confirmed all OTP screens now render pixel-identical headers. A follow-up ask to check "other otp verification pages" covered the admin and professional sections too — `WorkCompletionOTP.tsx`, `AdminForgotPin.tsx`, and `ProfessionalPinReset.tsx` all turned out to already correctly match their own section's sibling screens (`Header5Admin`, `Header4Admin`, and `HeaderProfessional` respectively), so nothing further needed changing there; those sections deliberately don't carry the WhatsApp/flag combination the customer-facing pages do.

A separate report — the drawer looked scrollable and should match HomeSewa's non-scrolling version — turned out not to be a live bug. Read `CustomDrawer.tsx` directly rather than assuming the screenshot proved a regression, confirmed the `ScrollView` had already been removed in the drawer rewrite from Day 78, then rendered the actual component through Playwright in both the logged-out state and a faked superadmin session (the state with the most menu items) to check for real overflow rather than trusting the code alone — neither scrolled, and the one scrollable element found on screen belonged to the Home page sitting behind the drawer, not the drawer itself. Flagged that distinction back rather than making a speculative change, and confirmed with the reporter it was a stale build rather than a live regression.

Closed the day helping diagnose an `eas build` upload failure (`ECONNRESET` partway through sending the archive to Google Cloud Storage). Rather than guess, checked whether the project itself was unusually bloated first — `android/app/build` is 199MB locally but already excluded from both git and the EAS archive by the existing `.gitignore`, so the reported 58MB compressed upload wasn't actually abnormal. Did find two real leftover JVM crash-dump files from a past out-of-memory Gradle crash sitting inside `android/` uncovered by `.gitignore`, plus a large `repomix-output.xml`, adding avoidable dead weight to every future upload. Gave the practical fix path in order of likelihood — retry (GCS drops mid-transfer often enough on residential connections to be the first suspect), update the noticeably outdated local `eas-cli` (20.1.0 vs. the current 22.2.0), rule out VPN/antivirus TLS interception, and clean up the stray files — rather than treating it as a code problem needing a deep investigation it didn't need.

**Learning:**
The web platform findings were the clearest example yet of why "the code reads correctly" isn't the same as "it works" — the OneSignal import, the date picker, and the CORS block would all have passed a code review and even a clean `tsc --noEmit`, and only showed up because the app was actually run in a browser and driven through its real flows instead of reasoned about from the source. The drawer report was the inverse lesson: a screenshot that looks like a bug isn't proof of one, and reading the component plus rendering it directly settled a stale-build question that would otherwise have led to fixing code that was already correct.

**Skills Gained:**
Standing up a headless-browser test harness (Playwright against the Expo web dev server) from scratch to actually drive the app's real screens and capture live network traffic instead of relying on static code review · replicating a client-side network call outside its normal execution context (a plain Node script instead of a browser) specifically to isolate whether a failure was environmental (CORS) or in the underlying logic · distinguishing a genuinely broken flow from one merely untestable through a particular deep-link path, by bypassing a known-broken UI control (the date picker) via direct route params to still exercise the real downstream code · auditing every screen in a category (all OTP-adjacent pages, not just the one reported) instead of only fixing the specific instance flagged · verifying a "looks scrollable" report by rendering the actual component in more than one data state (logged-out and max-menu-item superadmin) rather than trusting a single screenshot · triaging a cloud-build network failure by first ruling out the project itself as the cause (checking real vs. gitignored archive size) before pointing at infrastructure or environment.

**Challenge & Solution:**
No headless browser or emulator was already set up in this environment, and the web bundle itself wouldn't even build at first, which made "just test it" a much bigger first step than expected. Resolved by fixing the actual blocking bug first (the OneSignal static import), then installing Playwright and Chromium directly rather than trying to test through the native Android path, which would have needed a full emulator or physical device neither available here. Once running, the booking form's date picker turned out to have no web implementation at all, which would have blocked testing the OTP/SMS flow through the normal UI entirely — resolved by deep-linking straight to `BookingDetail` with the same route params `Book.tsx` would have sent, which still exercises every line of the real OTP-send and SMS-delivery code, just skipping the one broken form control.

**Message to Mentor:**
Ran a full functional pass on the app today — form submission, SMS, OTP, and login — using a real phone number end to end. First had to fix a genuine crash-on-load bug blocking the web build entirely (a static OneSignal import in the two booking-OTP screens), then used a headless browser to actually drive the app instead of reading the code and assuming it worked. Confirmed the real backend pipeline is sound: the booking database insert, the `send-otp` function, and Sparrow SMS delivery all verified live, including a real test SMS delivered to the number you gave me. But found the web build specifically has three real problems worth knowing about even though they don't affect the native app: the date picker doesn't render at all on web, the browser's CORS policy blocks the Sparrow SMS call that Day 80's fix relies on, and React Native's `Alert.alert` is silently a no-op on web, so every validation and error message across the whole app currently shows nothing to a web user. None of that touches the Play Store build, but it means the web target as currently configured isn't usable end-to-end if it's ever actually deployed. Also fixed a real header inconsistency on the two Helpbox OTP screens (they were secretly rendering a different, broken header component under a misleading import alias), confirmed every other OTP-adjacent screen in the app already matches its own section correctly, and ruled out a reported "scrollable drawer" as a stale build rather than a live bug by rendering the component directly in its highest-menu-item state. Closed the day helping diagnose an `eas build` upload failure — confirmed it wasn't the project's fault (the large local `android/app/build` folder is already excluded from the upload), found a couple of harmless leftover crash-log files worth cleaning up, and gave a prioritized fix list (retry, update the outdated local `eas-cli`, rule out VPN/antivirus interference). Also handed over a commit message for today's fixes so you can push directly, without pushing it myself since you wanted to run that step.

### Day 84 — 23 August 2026 (Sunday)

**Progress:**
The day started with a business decision handed down from HR, not a bug report: drop the dynamic Khalti QR payment system entirely and replace it with a static SRIYOG Consulting QR code, since Sparrow's IP-block history meant Khalti's own merchant account activation was still stuck. Removed the two Khalti Edge Functions and the client-side payment screen that called them, and rebuilt the lead-unlock paywall around the static QR. The catch with a static QR is that it carries no per-transaction reference, so there is no way to verify server-side that a given professional actually paid for a given lead the way Khalti's lookup API used to. Rather than ship a pure honor-system unlock, built a proper review loop instead: the professional scans the QR, uploads a screenshot of the payment as proof, and the lead stays locked until an admin or superadmin reviews it on a new "Lead Unlock Requests" screen and approves or rejects it — only approval writes the row that actually unlocks the lead. Made the tradeoff explicit rather than silent: flagged plainly that this is trust-plus-a-human-check, not cryptographic verification, so it was a decision made with full knowledge rather than a gap discovered later.

Testing that flow immediately surfaced a second, unrelated bug: every file upload in the app — not just the new payment-proof screenshots — was failing with a storage row-level-security error. Traced it to every anon upload policy on the `uploads` bucket being scoped to files with the literal extension `'jpeg'`, while the app's own upload code only ever produces `.jpg`, `.png`, or `.pdf` from real cameras and document pickers — meaning no real file uploaded through Career, Partnership, workforce documents, profile pictures, or admin banners had ever actually matched its own bucket policy. Rewrote all nine affected policies in a migration to accept the real extension set. That investigation also caught a mistake in the new proof-upload feature before it shipped broken: it had been pointed at a private `id-documents` bucket that turned out not to exist anywhere in the live project at all — confirmed directly against `storage.buckets` rather than trusting the pattern a research pass had found — so repointed it at the same real, public `uploads` bucket every other upload in the app already uses.

A screenshot of the Career form surfaced a header inconsistency (missing the WhatsApp icon and country-flag toggle every other customer-facing screen has) and, separately, a "Preferred Working Area" dropdown showing Chennai neighborhood names to a Nepal-flagged user. Fixing the second one nearly went wrong: the obvious-looking fix was to swap the dropdown's data source to a different list already in the codebase labeled `positionAppliedFor` — but tracing where that field's selections actually get written showed they'd land in the same `workforce.area_of_expertise` column the professional-matching engine depends on, which needs the *services* vocabulary, not job titles. Caught it before shipping, reverted, and instead built the options from the country context's own per-city area data, the same source `Book.tsx` already used correctly. The same pass fixed the same Chennai-vs-Nepal bug in Partnership's City field and in an admin banner-targeting screen that had silently diverged from its own sibling create-screen.

Asked whether every form has OTP verification "like HomeSewa," checked rather than assumed, and found Booking and Helpbox genuinely do but Career and Partnership do not — despite an earlier day's log describing OTP screens for both that don't actually exist in the current code. Built real ones: new `CareerOTP.tsx` and `PartnershipOTP.tsx` screens, extended the shared Sparrow OTP service and its Edge Function with two new purposes, and rewired both forms to send a real code and hold the database write until it verifies, for Nepal numbers only — matching the same Sparrow-can't-reach-India constraint every other form already works around. Cloned this session's copy of the actual HomeSewa repository to check what "like HomeSewa" really meant rather than guessing, and found HomeSewa sends a distinct "application received, login details coming later" SMS after Career OTP verification specifically — added the same message with RocketSingh's own name and link, not HomeSewa's.

A question about whether a professional's approval actually works led to a bigger find: the screen that generates a professional's login PIN and activates their account (`UserManagement.tsx`) was fully built and functional but had no navigation path to it anywhere in the app — no drawer link, no button, nothing. A superadmin using the screen they *could* reach (`ProfessionalDetails.tsx`) could mark an application "Accepted" and reasonably believe the professional was approved, when in fact the account never got a PIN or an SMS. Added the missing drawer link. Testing that loop end-to-end then surfaced a professional-login failure, traced to `professionalAuth.ts` swallowing the edge function's real error message and showing a generic one instead — fixed to match the same error-unwrapping pattern already used elsewhere in the codebase. The credentials underneath turned out to be entirely valid on inspection, which reframed the actual ask: HR wanted the Admin Login screen itself to accept any active account, staff or professional, the way HomeSewa's does. Checked HomeSewa's real `admin-login` function directly and found it falls back to the professional table when a phone isn't staff — ported the same fallback, but issuing a `professional_sessions` token rather than folding professionals into the staff session table, so none of the professional-only Edge Functions built earlier in the day needed to change.

The last major thread was making the logged-in professional experience match HomeSewa's structurally, checked against the real HomeSewa source rather than assumption at every step. Added a staff-confirmation gate so a brand-new booking is invisible to professionals until an admin explicitly confirms it (new bookings now start `"Pending Confirmation"`, and confirming is what now triggers the professional-facing notification, not creation). Extended `claim_booking_status` so a professional — not just staff — can mark a job complete, but only for a lead they actually paid to unlock, checked server-side against `lead_unlocks` rather than trusted from the client. Rebuilt the professional's lead list with the same search bar, status filter chips, and calendar date view HomeSewa's `BookingHistory.tsx` has. That rebuild is what surfaced the session's biggest correctness bug: `list-leads` had been filtering to only bookings matching the professional's own registered service and area, while HomeSewa's real `fetchBookings()` applies no such filter at all — every logged-in professional there sees every open booking. Removed the filter to match. Also gave professional screens the hamburger menu and drawer access they never had (`HeaderProfessional` had no menu button at all), and built out the professional's drawer content to mirror HomeSewa's real menu structure using only screens that actually exist in this app. Closed the day wiring push notifications through the new payment-proof review loop — admins get notified when a professional submits proof, the professional gets notified when it's approved or rejected — and, checking rather than assuming it would work, found the dedicated Professional Login screen never registered the device with OneSignal at all, meaning a targeted approve/reject push would silently never arrive for anyone who logged in there instead of through the newly-unified Admin Login. Fixed to match.

**Learning:**
The Career dropdown near-miss was the clearest reminder yet that a fix which looks obviously correct from the UI alone can be wrong once you trace where the data actually flows — the "Position Applied For" label pointed one way, but the column it wrote to was load-bearing for something else entirely, and only checking the write path caught it before it shipped. The `list-leads` filtering bug was the same lesson from the opposite direction: a restriction that looked like a sensible feature (only show professionals leads that match their profile) turned out to be an undocumented divergence from the actual reference behavior, invisible until a real redesign of the surrounding screen made the empty result impossible to ignore. Both were resolved the same way every hard-to-verify claim on this internship has been: by reading the actual authoritative source — a database write path, a vendor's real code — instead of reasoning from what looked right.

**Skills Gained:**
Designing a manual-review fallback (payment-proof upload plus an admin approve/reject queue) for a paywall that lost its automated verification path, and being explicit about exactly what security property was traded away rather than letting it pass unstated · diagnosing a storage RLS policy mismatch by comparing the app's actual upload code against the deployed policy conditions directly, rather than assuming a generic "permission denied" meant a client-side bug · catching a plausible-looking fix before shipping it by tracing a form field's data through to where it's actually consumed downstream, not just where it displays · cloning and reading a sibling production codebase directly to settle what a vague "make it like X" request actually means, rather than guessing from the target app's own conventions · auditing whether a built feature is actually *reachable* through the app's real navigation, not just whether the underlying code and edge function exist and work · extending a single database RPC to safely serve two different session types (staff and professional) with different authorization rules, without changing the calling contract for either existing caller · recognizing when a security-hardening habit (server-side ownership checks, not client-trusted state) needs to extend to a brand-new access path the moment it's added, not as a follow-up.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Dropping Khalti removed the one thing that made the lead-unlock paywall trustworthy — a real payment verification — and there was no way to fully replace that with a static QR alone. Resolved by building the closest honest substitute (photo evidence plus a human reviewer) rather than either a silent honor-system unlock or refusing to ship, and stating the tradeoff plainly instead of leaving it to be discovered later. (2) Confirming whether HomeSewa's professional experience matched what was being asked for required knowing HomeSewa's actual behavior, not RocketSingh's assumptions about it, and no amount of reading RocketSingh's own code could answer that. Resolved by reading HomeSewa's real source directly at several points in the day — its `send-otp` messages, its `admin-login` fallback logic, its `fetchBookings` filtering (or lack of it) — each of which either confirmed or corrected an assumption before it turned into a wasted implementation.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today covered a full pivot away from Khalti to the static SRIYOG QR you asked for, replacing automated payment verification with a photo-proof-plus-admin-review flow and being upfront that this trades cryptographic certainty for a manual check. That work surfaced a real, systemic bug along the way — every file upload in the app, not just the new payment proofs, was silently failing storage's security policy because the policy only ever accepted a literal `.jpeg` extension while every real upload is `.jpg`/`.png`/`.pdf` — now fixed everywhere. Added real OTP verification to the Career and Partnership forms, which turned out not to actually exist despite being described in an earlier log, plus the same "application received" confirmation SMS HomeSewa sends. Found and fixed a gap where a superadmin could believe they'd approved a professional when the screen that actually finishes that (generates the PIN, sends the SMS) had no way to be reached in the app at all. Unified Admin Login so any active account — staff or professional — can log in from the one screen, matching HomeSewa, without disturbing any of the professional-only backend logic already built. Brought the logged-in professional experience to real parity with HomeSewa: a staff-confirmation step before new bookings become visible to professionals, the ability for a professional to mark their own completed jobs, a redesigned leads screen with search/filters/calendar, a drawer menu they never had before, and — the most consequential fix of the batch — removing an undocumented restriction that was hiding real, open bookings from professionals whose profile didn't happen to match a lead's exact service and area, which HomeSewa's real behavior never enforced in the first place. Closed the day wiring push notifications through the new payment-review loop in both directions, catching and fixing a registration gap that would have made those notifications silently never arrive for anyone who logged in through the older Professional Login screen. Also handed over a commit message for today's work so you can push it yourself, without pushing it myself since you wanted to run that step directly.

### Day 85 — 24 August 2026 (Monday)

**Progress:**
Yesterday's Khalti-to-static-QR pivot had been built on RocketSingh. Today HR pushed the same decision one level further — drop the dynamic QR for good and keep only the static SRIYOG Consulting QR — and since RocketSingh already had the full static-QR-plus-proof-review flow live, the actual task was porting that same system into HomeSewa itself, whose lead-unlock screen was still running the old Khalti checkout end to end.

Started by reading, not copying. Went through RocketSingh's real implementation first — its `LeadPayment.tsx`, the four supporting Edge Functions (`submit-lead-unlock-proof`, `approve-lead-unlock`, `reject-lead-unlock`, `list-lead-unlock-requests`), its `lead_unlock_requests` migration, and the separate professional-session module those functions check against. Then read HomeSewa's existing Khalti-based `LeadPayment.tsx`, its `khalti-confirm-lead-unlock` function, and its `lead_unlocks` table to find the real seam to port into rather than dropping RocketSingh's files in wholesale.

That comparison surfaced the actual risk in this task: the two apps only look identical on the surface. RocketSingh keeps professionals and staff in genuinely separate login flows with separate session tables, while HomeSewa authenticates both through one shared Admin Login screen and a single `admin_sessions` table, distinguishing them only by an `adminTable` value (`'workforce'` vs `'admins'`) and a `role` string (`'professional'`, `'admin'`, `'super_admin'`). Confirmed this by reading HomeSewa's actual `admin-login` Edge Function source and `CustomDrawer.tsx`'s role-branching logic directly rather than assuming the two apps' session models matched just because the feature and route names did — a straight file copy would have quietly built the whole flow against a session system HomeSewa doesn't have.

Rebuilt all four Edge Functions against HomeSewa's real shared session verifier instead of RocketSingh's: `submit-lead-unlock-proof` gates on `role === 'professional'`, while the two review functions and the list endpoint gate on `adminTable === 'admins'` — the exact same check HomeSewa's own `toggle-professional-status` function already uses, reused rather than reinvented. Wrote a new migration for `lead_unlock_requests` matching RocketSingh's schema but adjusted to HomeSewa's actual `booking_id` column, confirmed directly against `fetchBookingData.ts`'s own explicit column list rather than guessed from RocketSingh's `bookingid`.

Rewrote HomeSewa's `LeadPayment.tsx` to drop the Khalti initiate/poll/confirm flow entirely in favor of a static `QRCode` component rendering the SRIYOG payment URL, a screenshot upload through the existing `FileUploadBox`, and a submit call into the new function — reusing HomeSewa's own `uploadToStorage` helper instead of importing RocketSingh's separate `uploadPaymentProof` utility, since HomeSewa already had one that does the same job. Built a new `LeadUnlockRequests.tsx` admin/super-admin review screen from RocketSingh's version, ported onto HomeSewa's own theming system instead of RocketSingh's hardcoded colors, and gated with the same `AsyncStorage` `adminTable` check pattern HomeSewa's other admin-only screens use. Wired two new push-notification helpers — admin notified on submission, professional notified on the decision — into HomeSewa's own `notifications.ts`, matching its existing tag-filter and `external_id`-targeting conventions exactly rather than porting RocketSingh's differently-shaped helpers as-is.

Registered the new screen in the admin stack layout, added its drawer menu entry, and installed `react-native-qrcode-svg` (`react-native-svg` itself was already a shared dependency, so no extra native linking was needed). Deployed for real rather than stopping at "it compiles": pushed the new migration with `supabase db push` and deployed all four new Edge Functions with `supabase functions deploy` against the linked project, confirming both completed cleanly. Left the old Khalti Edge Functions and `khalti.ts` in place but unused rather than deleting already-deployed infrastructure nobody had asked to remove — a deliberate, reversible choice, flagged rather than assumed. Closed by running a full `tsc --noEmit` across the whole project, not just the new files, and confirmed zero errors anywhere.

**Learning:**
The clearest lesson of the day was that "same feature, same screen name" across two sibling codebases is not the same guarantee as "same underlying architecture." RocketSingh and HomeSewa were clearly forked from a shared lineage, but the account/session model had diverged in a way that was invisible from the feature-level code alone — it only showed up by reading the actual login Edge Function and session-verification source of both apps side by side. Porting a feature between sibling projects is closer to a targeted rebuild against the target's real primitives than a copy-paste, and confirming that difference before writing code, rather than after something failed at runtime, was what kept this port correct on the first deploy.

**Skills Gained:**
Reconciling two sibling apps' differently-shaped account and session systems before porting a feature between them, instead of assuming file-for-file parity from matching screen and route names · confirming a target database's real column names and types directly from its own query helper before writing a new migration against it · reusing a target app's own existing upload and notification conventions instead of importing a sibling app's parallel implementation of the same job · deploying a real Supabase migration and multiple Edge Functions to a linked production project within the same session, rather than leaving deployment as an unverified follow-up · validating a cross-file port with a full project-wide `tsc --noEmit` rather than only checking the new files in isolation.

**Challenge & Solution:**
The two apps' identical screen names and route paths made it easy to assume the professional/admin session wiring underneath was identical too, which would have been a silent, hard-to-diagnose failure mode — code that typechecks and deploys cleanly but rejects every real professional's session token at runtime. Resolved by reading HomeSewa's actual `admin-login` function and `CustomDrawer.tsx` role logic directly before writing any of the four Edge Functions, then building each one against HomeSewa's real `verifySession` helper and its actual `adminTable`/`role` values instead of RocketSingh's separate professional-session module.

**Message to Mentor:**
Finished today's ask: brought the same static-SRIYOG-QR-plus-photo-proof-plus-admin-review lead-unlock flow from RocketSingh over into HomeSewa itself, so both apps are now off the dynamic Khalti QR for good. Rather than copy RocketSingh's files directly, first confirmed the two apps' professional/staff account systems don't actually match one-to-one — HomeSewa authenticates both through one shared Admin Login and a single session table, RocketSingh keeps them fully separate — and rebuilt all four supporting Edge Functions against HomeSewa's real, existing session-verification helper instead. Added the new database table, the four Edge Functions, the rebuilt payment screen with the static QR and screenshot upload, a new admin/super-admin review screen, drawer and routing wiring, and push notifications for both the submission and the approve/reject decision — reusing HomeSewa's own upload and notification helpers throughout rather than introducing RocketSingh's parallel versions of the same thing. Pushed the database migration and deployed all four functions to the live project directly rather than leaving that for later, and left the old Khalti functions deployed but unused rather than deleting infrastructure nobody asked to remove. Full project typecheck is clean. Flagging one thing for you rather than deciding it myself: this hasn't been run through the app on a device yet, and since it's a money-handling screen, I'd like to actually click through the pay-and-review loop end to end before calling it done.
