### Day 86 — 25 August 2026 (Tuesday)

**Progress:**
With both apps now running the same static-QR-plus-review lead-unlock flow, today's task came from HR looking past it toward a real gateway: they'd been told IME Pay and Khalti wallets don't interoperate, and proposed Nabil Bank's Electronic Payment Gateway (EPG) as a bank-agnostic replacement — pending an "eCommerce license" — with one hard requirement, that the public be able to pay from their own wallet without downloading a QR. Before evaluating Nabil at all, checked what payment code actually exists in RocketSingh rather than accepting "replace IME Pay" at face value: a full-repository search for IME Pay turned up nothing — it has never been integrated anywhere in either app. The only gateway either app ever ran was Khalti, reverted on Day 84 and Day 85 in favor of the static SRIYOG QR-plus-admin-review flow now live in both, in `LeadPayment.tsx` and `leadUnlock.ts` on this side.

With the actual starting point established, researched Nabil EPG itself instead of taking the "works with any gateway" framing at face value. A focused research pass against Nabil's own product page and independent coverage (ICTFrame, a public directory of Nepali payment gateways) found EPG consistently described as a Visa/MasterCard/UnionPay card-acquiring gateway with 3D Secure — no source mentions it aggregating eSewa, Khalti, or IME Pay wallets, which is the opposite of what HR's "pay with their wallet, no QR" requirement needs. Set that against Khalti's own Payment Gateway and eSewa's ePay v2, both genuine multi-wallet/bank aggregators with public API docs and sandboxes, unlike Nabil EPG, which appears to hand over integration details only after a signed branch merchant agreement with no public docs site found. Also checked the "waiting on an eCommerce license" claim against how Nepal Rastra Bank actually licenses payments — PSP/PSO status under the Payment and Settlement Act 2075 sits with the gateway operator itself, not the merchant using it — so flagged that as a claim worth confirming directly with Nabil rather than assuming it's a real blocker.

Wrote the findings up as a proper due-diligence document rather than a chat reply, since this is going to HR as a recommendation: covering the current, IME-Pay-free state of both apps, what Nabil EPG is actually scoped to with sourced citations, a side-by-side comparison against Khalti and eSewa, and the license question — ending in a recommendation to start a parallel Khalti/eSewa sandbox evaluation now rather than wait on Nabil. Published it as a shareable document so it can go straight to HR.

**Learning:**
The IME Pay search was the same lesson this report keeps returning to from a new angle: HR's framing ("replace our IME Pay system") described a system that was never actually built in either app, and proceeding on that framing without checking the code first would have meant researching a migration path for something that doesn't exist. Reading Nabil's own product page directly, rather than trusting the one-line pitch it was relayed through, was the only way to catch that a "works with any wallet" claim and a "card-only gateway" reality were two different products being described with the same words.

**Skills Gained:**
Verifying a business claim against a vendor's own primary source before acting on it, rather than researching a solution to a requirement the claim doesn't actually satisfy · cross-referencing a stakeholder's framing of a request against the real codebase, across both live apps, before scoping any work · separating a regulatory question (NRB PSP/PSO licensing) from a vendor's account of it, and flagging the gap for direct confirmation instead of assuming either side is correct · structuring a technical comparison (feature ledger, sourced findings, a clear recommendation) for a non-technical decision-maker.

**Challenge & Solution:**
HR's request was framed as a straightforward swap ("replace IME Pay with Nabil Bank"), but neither half of that framing held up: IME Pay isn't in either app's codebase, and Nabil EPG's public documentation doesn't describe the wallet-aggregation capability the request was actually trying to get. Resolved by researching both halves independently against primary sources — the real codebase for the first, Nabil's own page and independent coverage for the second — rather than accepting the framing and researching a Nabil integration that would not have solved the underlying problem.

**Message to Mentor:**
HR asked me to explore replacing IME Pay with Nabil Bank's EPG as a payment gateway that "works with any wallet." Checked the codebase first and found IME Pay was never actually integrated in either RocketSingh or HomeSewa — the only gateway either app ever ran was Khalti, now off in both after yesterday's port. Researched Nabil EPG against its own product page and independent coverage and found it's consistently described as a card-only gateway (Visa/MasterCard/UnionPay) with no evidence of wallet aggregation, which doesn't satisfy the actual requirement — letting the public pay from whichever wallet they hold without a QR. Khalti's own Payment Gateway and eSewa's ePay v2 already do that, with public docs and sandboxes available today, no license wait involved. Also flagged that the "waiting on an eCommerce license" reason doesn't map cleanly onto how Nepal Rastra Bank actually licenses payment operators, and is worth confirming directly with Nabil. Wrote all of this up as a sourced comparison document for HR rather than a verbal summary, recommending a parallel Khalti/eSewa evaluation instead of waiting on Nabil.

---

### Day 87 — 26 August 2026 (Wednesday)

**Progress:**
Continued yesterday's Nabil EPG thread with HR. Sent the recommendation drafted from yesterday's research — confirm directly with Nabil whether EPG has any wallet-aggregation capability beyond cards before committing to the license wait, and pursue Khalti Payment Gateway or eSewa ePay v2 in parallel if it's card-only as documented. HR's reply asked for links to "ePay" that supports both Khalti and eSewa, phrasing that read as if a single gateway might aggregate both wallets. Checked that assumption before answering it: eSewa ePay v2 and Khalti Payment Gateway are separate products from competing companies, and neither accepts payment from the other's wallet — confirmed against each provider's own current developer documentation (docs.khalti.com, developer.esewa.com.np) rather than answering from memory, since gateway docs and sandbox endpoints are the kind of thing that goes stale. Sent both official doc links back with that distinction made explicit, rather than sending links that would have reinforced the wrong assumption.

That prompted the real question from HR: is there any company that accepts both wallets from one channel? Researched it rather than guessing, and the answer turned out to be yes, but not in the shape HR's question implied — no single payment gateway product does it, but Fonepay, the interoperable QR network built on Nepal Rastra Bank's own NepalQR standard, does: one Fonepay QR can be scanned by Khalti, eSewa, IME Pay, and most mobile banking apps, with the wallet choice left entirely to whoever's paying. That's effectively the official, bank-backed version of the static SRIYOG QR both apps have been running since Day 84/85. Also surfaced two third-party aggregator APIs, PayBridgeNP and APINepal, that wrap eSewa, Khalti, and Fonepay behind one checkout integration for a business that wants an API instead of a QR — noted as a separate, non-bank-infrastructure option in case HR wants a proper checkout later rather than another static QR.

**Learning:**
HR's "is there an ePay that supports both" question could have been answered two different ways depending on which noun was doing the work — "ePay" read as a specific eSewa product name versus "a payment channel" in general — and answering the narrow reading first (no, they're separate) before the broader one HR was actually reaching for (yes, via Fonepay) kept the reply from being technically correct but practically useless. The distinction that mattered wasn't Khalti-vs-eSewa at all, it was gateway-vs-network: a gateway is scoped to one wallet's own money, a network like Fonepay sits underneath multiple wallets and is what actually answers "one channel, many wallets."

**Skills Gained:**
Reading past a stakeholder's literal phrasing to the underlying question they're actually asking, and answering both the literal and the intended version rather than picking one · verifying vendor/product claims (which gateway supports which wallet) against each provider's own current documentation instead of relying on general knowledge that may be stale · distinguishing a payment gateway (single-wallet-scoped) from a payment network (multi-wallet interoperability layer) when researching options for a non-technical stakeholder · citing sourced, linkable documentation in HR-facing answers so the recommendation can be independently checked rather than taken on faith.

**Challenge & Solution:**
HR's question conflated two different kinds of question — "which gateway works with both wallets" (answer: none, they're separate) and "how do we let customers pay from whichever wallet they hold" (answer: Fonepay's interoperable QR, or an aggregator API). Answering only the literal question would have been accurate but would have left HR without the thing they actually needed. Resolved by researching and answering both: confirmed no single gateway aggregates Khalti and eSewa, then researched and named the actual mechanism — Fonepay's NRB-backed interoperable QR — that does what HR was really asking for.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today was HR follow-up on the Nabil EPG research from yesterday. Sent the recommendation to confirm wallet-aggregation directly with Nabil and evaluate Khalti/eSewa in parallel. When HR asked for links to an "ePay" supporting both wallets, checked that eSewa ePay v2 and Khalti Payment Gateway are genuinely separate, single-wallet-scoped products before replying, and sent official docs for both (docs.khalti.com, developer.esewa.com.np) with that distinction made explicit rather than implicitly confirming a wrong assumption. HR's actual follow-up question — is there any company that accepts both wallets through one channel — has a real answer: Fonepay, Nepal Rastra Bank's interoperable QR network, already lets a single QR accept Khalti, eSewa, IME Pay, and most banking apps, which is effectively the official version of the static QR flow already live in both apps. Also flagged PayBridgeNP and APINepal as third-party aggregator APIs in case a proper checkout integration is wanted later instead of another QR. No code changed today — this was HR-facing research and communication continuing directly off yesterday's Nabil due-diligence document.

### Day 88 — 01 September 2026 (Tuesday)

**Progress:**
HR's instruction today was to make GardenSewa's backend "the same as HomeSewa," starting with a custom form on Supabase. Rather than take that at face value, first cloned into HomeSewa's actual repository — its Supabase migrations, its Edge Functions, its `api/PostApi*.ts` insert pattern — to establish what "same backend" concretely means before writing anything. That reading surfaced the real design: no separate server at all, just direct `supabase.from(table).insert()` calls from the client, RLS-gated, with sensitive operations (admin creation, status changes) pushed into service-role Edge Functions instead. Flagged one ambiguity before proceeding — "same Supabase" could mean literally sharing HomeSewa's live project or just reusing its pattern on a new one — and confirmed a separate project was the right call, since sharing would mix two unrelated businesses' data under one set of credentials, quotas, and blast radius.

HR then named the actual target: the "Join as a Gardener" form. Checked it directly at gardensewa.com/join rather than assuming it already had a backend, and found it was a bare Google Form with zero connection to any database — so the task wasn't porting an existing form, it was building one from scratch using HomeSewa's Career-form pattern as the template. Captured all ~35 real fields and every dropdown's exact option list directly from screenshots of the live form (down to values like `SLC/SEE`, `Hourly Basis ( 3 Hours in the Morning )`) rather than guessing plausible-sounding categories.

With GardenSewa's own Supabase project credentials in hand, designed and pushed the `gardener` table migration, plus a public `uploads` bucket and a private `id-documents` bucket mirroring HomeSewa's split for sensitive citizenship/NID uploads. Deliberately tightened the RLS beyond HomeSewa's own documented posture — HomeSewa's `workforce`/`admin` tables are left fully open to the anon key because tightening them now would break an existing admin panel that reads them directly, a tradeoff their own migration comments describe as known debt. GardenSewa has no such panel yet, so there was no reason to inherit that gap: the anon key gets INSERT only, and even that is restricted column-by-column so the public form can never set its own `status`. Rewrote the `joinasaprofessional.js` screen field-for-field to match the real form and wired it to the new table through new `PostApiGardener`/`uploadToStorage` helpers.

HR then asked for the review side and backend for the remaining roles (BDM, Call Center, Customer). Built one `admin` table with a role column covering Super Admin/Admin/BDM/Call Center, session-token auth matching HomeSewa's own pattern (an opaque, revocable DB token via a custom `x-admin-session-token` header, not `Authorization`, which Supabase's gateway consumes before function code runs), and six Edge Functions — login, logout, list, approve, reject, and a signed-URL endpoint for viewing the private ID document. Built a real fix in from day one for a race condition HomeSewa had documented having to patch reactively later (an atomic Postgres function for the login-lockout counter instead of read-then-write). Added a new `(admin)` route group with a gardener-applications review screen (status tabs, approve/reject, reason capture, document viewing) and rewired the existing `adminLogin.js` screen, which turned out to be checking a hardcoded mock array and routing into the *professional* booking screen despite being labeled "ADMIN LOGIN" — not a design choice, just an old placeholder.

Rather than call any of this done once it compiled and deployed, ran a full live test pass: seeded four temporary accounts (one per role) and two temporary gardener applications directly against the production project, exercised every function and permission boundary through real HTTP calls, then deleted all of it afterward. That process caught two real bugs before they'd have surfaced in actual use. First, on the `gardener` table: a `REVOKE INSERT (status)` was silently doing nothing because a broader table-level `GRANT INSERT` was still active — Postgres allows either grant to satisfy a privilege check, so the column-specific revoke had no effect until the table-level grant was replaced with an explicit column list. Second, and more consequential: `admin-login` returned "Invalid phone or PIN" for a correctly seeded test account. Traced it by checking whether the failed-login counter had incremented (it hadn't, ruling out a hash mismatch) and then querying `information_schema.role_table_grants` directly — which showed `service_role` itself had zero SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE privileges on any table this session had created. This specific Supabase project does not set up default table privileges for any role, including `service_role`, unlike the typical Supabase default where that role is assumed to bypass everything. Every admin Edge Function was silently broken until an explicit grant was pushed. Re-ran the entire test suite afterward and confirmed every role, permission boundary, city-scoping rule, and session-invalidation path now behaves correctly, then cleaned up all test data.

**Learning:**
The `service_role` finding was the sharpest version yet of a lesson this internship keeps returning to from new angles: a platform default that's safe to assume on every other project you've touched is not safe to assume on this one until it's actually been tested against real requests. `service_role` bypassing all access restrictions is about as close to a universal Supabase assumption as exists, and it was still wrong here — invisible from reading the SQL, invisible from the deploy succeeding, only catchable by calling the live function and then tracing the actual privilege grants when the result didn't match expectation. The HomeSewa side of the day was the same principle applied to a stakeholder instruction rather than a platform default: "same backend as HomeSewa" and "the join form already has a backend" both sounded like settled facts until checked directly against HomeSewa's real source and the live gardensewa.com site, and both turned out to need correcting before any code was worth writing.

**Skills Gained:**
Reverse-engineering a live third-party form's complete field set and every dropdown's real option list directly from UI screenshots when no API or schema is available · designing Postgres RLS and column-level privilege locking correctly by testing the deployed policy with real insert/select calls rather than trusting the SQL's apparent correctness · discovering a non-standard Supabase project configuration (no default privileges for any role, service_role included) by systematically querying `information_schema.role_table_grants` instead of assuming platform-standard behavior · porting a sibling company's backend *pattern* (session tokens, storage bucket split, RLS philosophy) onto a fresh project without also inheriting that sibling's own documented technical debt · designing a four-role back-office authentication system (Super Admin/Admin/BDM/Call Center sharing one login and session mechanism, with per-role and per-city permission scoping) from a one-line stakeholder request into a concrete schema · building, exercising, and fully tearing down a disposable end-to-end test fixture against a live production database to verify a security-sensitive feature before calling it finished.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) HR's instruction to keep GardenSewa's backend "the same as" HomeSewa's was ambiguous between literally sharing HomeSewa's live Supabase project and reusing its architecture on a new one — the former would mix two unrelated businesses' data and risk under one set of credentials and quotas. Resolved by laying out the tradeoff explicitly before building anything, and proceeding with a separate project once confirmed. (2) The gardener join form was assumed by the request to already exist as a backend-connected form; it turned out to be an unconnected Google Form, which would have made a literal "port the form" task impossible as stated. Resolved by checking the live site directly, then reframing the task as building the form from its real fields rather than migrating one that didn't exist. (3) `admin-login` failed against a correctly seeded, correctly hashed test account with no indication of why. Resolved by ruling out the hash path first (failed-attempts counter hadn't moved) and then inspecting the actual Postgres grants directly rather than assuming the standard Supabase service-role behavior applied here.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's work was standing up GardenSewa's Supabase backend to match HomeSewa's architecture, starting from HR's request for a custom gardener join form. Read HomeSewa's real Supabase project first to establish what "same backend" means concretely, then discovered the actual join form at gardensewa.com/join was an unconnected Google Form, not something with an existing backend to port — so captured its real ~35 fields and every dropdown's exact options from screenshots and built the table, storage buckets, and app screen from that. Deliberately tightened the new table's RLS beyond HomeSewa's own posture, since HomeSewa's equivalent tables are left open to the anon key as documented, known debt from an existing admin panel that would break if tightened — GardenSewa has no such panel yet, so there was no reason to start with that gap. Then built the full backend for the remaining roles HR named (Super Admin, Admin, BDM, Call Center, and a placeholder for Customer): one role-based admin table, session-token auth matching HomeSewa's own pattern, and Edge Functions for login/logout/list/approve/reject/document-view, plus a new in-app screen for reviewing applications. Rather than stop once everything deployed, ran a full live test pass against the production project — seeding and then deleting temporary test accounts and applications — which caught two real bugs first: a column-privilege revoke that Postgres was silently ignoring, and, more seriously, that this Supabase project grants zero default table privileges to any role at all, including `service_role`, which had left every admin function reading nothing with no visible error. Fixed both, re-ran the full test suite to confirm every role, city-scoping rule, and permission boundary now works correctly, and left the project in a clean, fully verified state with all test data removed.

### Day 89 — 02 September 2026 (Wednesday)

**Progress:**
Started the day by auditing Day 88's "same backend as HomeSewa" work against what HomeSewa's backend actually contains end to end, rather than assuming the gardener join-and-review flow already covered the ask. Compared migration and Edge Function counts directly — HomeSewa's real project runs 38 migrations and roughly 24 Edge Functions; GardenSewa had 5 and 6 — and mapped the gap role by role: Gardener's application flow was solid, but there was no post-approval gardener login, no Customer auth or booking backend at all, and no way to actually create a BDM/Call Center/Admin/Super Admin account except by hand in the database. Wrote that up plainly rather than reporting the earlier work as complete, then handed back a phased roadmap (auth foundations first, since bookings and ratings all foreign-key into identity; the booking/lead system second; notifications and supporting infrastructure third).

HR then supplied an EasyService SMS API key and its API guide URL to close the OTP gap first. The guide itself was incomplete — it showed the request shape (`to`, `text`, `message_type`, a `sender_id` map per telecom operator) but only a placeholder domain and no real sender IDs — so treated it as a starting hypothesis to verify live rather than a finished spec. Built the OTP/SMS infrastructure as a direct architectural port of HomeSewa's own `send-otp`/`verify-otp`/`send-sms` (same `otp_codes`/`otp_send_log`/`sms_send_log` shape, SHA-256 code hashing, atomic attempt-counter RPC, resend cooldown and daily cap), but diverged deliberately on delivery: EasyService's docs gave no sign of the IP-allowlist block that forced HomeSewa's SMS send client-side, so built it server-side instead, with the API key stored only as a Supabase Function secret, never in a file. Wired the existing "Join as a Gardener" → OTP confirmation screen to this real backend — it had been a UI-only mock with no server call behind it at all.

Before guessing at the missing sender ID, checked whether HomeSewa or RocketSingh's live Supabase projects already had one to reuse — queried both directly via the CLI rather than trusting the local HomeSewa checkout alone, which turned up a real, separate finding: RocketSingh's deployed project is materially fuller than the local repo, with `admin-create`/`approve-admin`/`reject-admin`, a real `professional-login`, `list-staff`/`update-staff-role`, and OneSignal/Firebase push already wired — exactly the admin-account-management and gardener-login gaps flagged that morning, and a better reference to build Phase 1 against than the local checkout. Neither project used EasyService, though — both run Sparrow SMS — so the sender ID itself still had to come from HR. Tested it live as values arrived rather than assuming any of them were correct: `THE_Alert` and a `TheAlert` variant (guessed from HomeSewa's own Sparrow sender name, on the theory it might be a cross-provider carryover) were both rejected by EasyService's real API for the NT operator; HR then supplied `MD_Alert`, which the live API accepted for NT but not yet for Ncell until the same value was tried there too and accepted. Once both operators confirmed on the same value, simplified the two per-operator secrets down to one shared `EASYSERVICE_SENDER_ID` and re-verified the simplification didn't break delivery before redeploying.

Moved on to push notifications once OTP was confirmed working. Walked HR through retrieving the OneSignal App ID and REST API key from the OneSignal dashboard (including creating a fresh named key once it turned out the existing legacy key's value could no longer be revealed) and the Firebase `google-services.json` (including registering a new Android app in Firebase console, since none existed yet, using the exact package name read out of `app.json` rather than a guess). Installed `react-native-onesignal` and `onesignal-expo-plugin`, wired `app.json`'s Android `googleServicesFile` and the plugin block, added native initialization in the root layout guarded against the app's own web build target, and wired admin login/logout to `OneSignal.login(phone)`/`logout()` plus role tagging so back-office devices become individually targetable. Built a `notifications` history table and a `send-notification` Edge Function, but deliberately did not copy HomeSewa's own design for it: HomeSewa lets the client build the full OneSignal payload and only gates the broad-reach shapes behind a super-admin session, which is safe there because that session check and an admin broadcast screen already exist — neither exists in GardenSewa yet, so an equally open endpoint would let anyone holding the anon key push arbitrary content to arbitrary recipients. Built it purpose-driven instead, the same pattern already used for `send-otp`: fixed server-side message templates and recipient lookups per purpose, with the one real trigger that exists today — new gardener application received — pushing to active Super Admin/Admin/BDM accounts. Tested live end to end; the call executed cleanly and correctly reported zero recipients, since no admin accounts are seeded in GardenSewa yet, and separately confirmed the anon key still has zero read access to the `admin` table as designed.

When asked directly whether the new SMS/OTP/push systems now matched HomeSewa's and RocketSingh's, gave an honest comparison rather than a blanket yes: OTP's underlying logic is a direct copy of HomeSewa's security pattern but covers 1 of its 6 purposes; SMS uses a different provider and a different delivery architecture entirely, by necessity; push shares the same OneSignal transport and phone-targeting mechanism but covers 1 of HomeSewa's 16 triggers, deliberately built with a tighter request contract given the missing admin infrastructure around it. Closed the day on two smaller items: after HR pushed the branch to GitHub, its secret scanner flagged the Firebase API key inside `google-services.json` — explained that this specific key class is designed by Google to ship publicly in every Android build and isn't a credential leak the way the OneSignal/EasyService keys would be, and walked through the actual correct response (restrict the key by Android package and API scope in Google Cloud Console, then dismiss the alert) rather than scrubbing it from git history, which would only have broken the next build. Then updated this report with the day's work at HR's request.

**Learning:**
The sender-ID search was a clean example of a pattern this report keeps returning to: a plausible-looking guess grounded in real, related context (HomeSewa's actual Sparrow sender name) is still a guess, and the only way to know is to fire it at the live API and read what comes back. Two wrong guesses in a row cost nothing because each one returned a specific, actionable error rather than a silent failure — the design choice to surface EasyService's real response text instead of a generic "failed" message is what made iterating to the correct value fast instead of directionless. The RocketSingh discovery carried the same lesson from a different angle: assuming the local HomeSewa checkout was the authoritative picture of "HomeSewa's backend" would have meant designing Phase 1 against an incomplete reference, when the live sibling project one CLI call away already had working versions of the exact pieces still missing.

**Skills Gained:**
Auditing a previous day's "task complete" claim against the real reference implementation's actual scope (migration and function counts, role-by-role gap mapping) before accepting it as finished · treating an incomplete third-party API guide as a hypothesis to verify against the live endpoint rather than a finished spec, and iterating sender-ID/config guesses against real API error responses instead of guessing blindly · querying a sibling project's live deployed state directly via CLI rather than trusting a local checkout that had visibly drifted from it · storing every third-party credential (SMS API key, OneSignal REST key) as a server-side secret immediately on receipt and never in a file, then consolidating redundant per-operator secrets once live testing confirmed they were identical · choosing a stricter, purpose-driven server-side design over a more open sibling pattern once the security precondition that made the sibling's design safe (a session-gated broadcast screen) was confirmed absent · walking a non-technical stakeholder step by step through two unfamiliar third-party dashboards (OneSignal, Firebase Console) to retrieve exact credentials, including recovering from a dead end when a legacy API key's value could no longer be revealed · correctly distinguishing a public-by-design Firebase client key from an actual leaked secret when triaging a GitHub secret-scanning alert, and recommending API-key scoping over a pointless git-history rewrite.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The EasyService API guide didn't document the real request domain or any valid sender ID, and guessing wrong risked silently misconfiguring SMS delivery. Resolved by treating every value as untested until fired at the live endpoint, reading EasyService's own error text back each time, and converging on the correct shared sender ID across two operators through several fast, cheap live tests rather than one unverified assumption. (2) HR's "make GardenSewa's backend the same as HomeSewa" instruction from Day 88 risked being treated as settled once the gardener flow shipped, when most of HomeSewa's actual backend surface was still unbuilt. Resolved by directly counting and role-mapping HomeSewa's real migrations and functions against GardenSewa's before reporting status, surfacing the gap honestly instead of letting one shipped slice stand in for the whole ask. (3) A GitHub secret-scanning alert on a committed Firebase API key looked like a credential leak requiring rotation. Resolved by checking what class of key it actually was against Google's own documented security model for Firebase client keys before recommending any action, avoiding an unnecessary and disruptive git-history rewrite for a file that has to ship in every build regardless.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today started by checking Day 88's gardener-flow work against HomeSewa's full backend rather than treating it as the whole ask — it wasn't; most of HomeSewa's actual surface (customer auth, gardener/professional login, bookings, admin account management) is still unbuilt, and I reported that gap directly along with a phased plan. HR then supplied EasyService SMS credentials, so built real OTP/SMS infrastructure as a direct port of HomeSewa's own security pattern (hashing, rate-limiting, atomic attempt counters), wired to the app's existing but previously mock "Join as a Gardener" OTP screen. The provider's own API guide was incomplete, so found the working configuration — including the correct sender ID for both NT and Ncell — by testing live against the real API rather than guessing, with HR supplying the value that ultimately worked. While checking whether HomeSewa or RocketSingh already had reusable SMS config, found that RocketSingh's live project is a fuller, more current reference than the local HomeSewa checkout, with working versions of exactly the admin-management and gardener-login pieces still missing here. Then connected OneSignal and Firebase for push notifications end to end — walked through retrieving credentials from both dashboards, wired the native app, and built the first real trigger (new gardener application notifies active back-office reviewers), deliberately using a stricter server-side design than HomeSewa's own since GardenSewa doesn't yet have the admin-broadcast infrastructure that makes HomeSewa's more open version safe. When asked directly, gave an honest comparison against HomeSewa/RocketSingh rather than claiming full parity: the security patterns match, the feature coverage is intentionally narrower because the surrounding systems (bookings, professional login) aren't built yet. Also triaged a GitHub secret-scanning alert on the committed Firebase config key — confirmed it's a key class Google designs to be public, recommended scoping it in Google Cloud Console instead of rewriting git history, which would have broken the app build for no security benefit. Handed over a commit message for today's work rather than pushing it myself.

### Day 90 — 03 September 2026 (Thursday)

**Progress:**
Picked up Phase 1 of yesterday's gap-mapped roadmap: admin account management, gardener/professional login, and customer OTP login. Asked which to start with and built all three across the day, each grounded in HomeSewa's actual verified source rather than assumption.

Started with admin account management. Read HomeSewa's real `admin-create`, `toggle-admin-status`, and `update-admin-cities` directly rather than guessing at their shape from RocketSingh's function names alone, and found a real adaptation was needed: HomeSewa stores PINs in a plaintext `pin` column and leaves its `admin` table openly readable by the anon key (documented debt), while GardenSewa already closed both gaps on Day 88 with bcrypt `pin_hash` and zero anon access — so admin-create had to hash into `pin_hash`, not carry HomeSewa's plaintext storage forward, and listing accounts needed a new `list-admins` Edge Function GardenSewa's real source, since the client can't just query the table directly the way HomeSewa's UserManagement.tsx does. Built a "Manage Staff" screen for Super Admin (add account, role/city pickers, status toggle). Hit a real chicken-and-egg problem before any of it could be tested: every path requires an existing Super Admin session, but the `admin` table was completely empty. Resolved it with a one-time bootstrap function locked behind two independent gates — a random secret AND the table being empty — so it stayed safe even if left deployed, then deleted it entirely once used. Live-tested the full loop (login, list, create, toggle, city-edit) against the real seeded account, then deleted the throwaway test account afterward.

Moved to gardener/professional login. Read HomeSewa's real `create-professional-login`, `approve-professional`, and `admin-login`'s branching logic before writing anything, and that surfaced HomeSewa's actual architecture: a `workforce`/`professional` split (application data vs. login/account data, created early at application time to reserve the phone number) unified into one login screen by having `admin-login` fall through to the professional table when a phone isn't found in `admin`. Adapted rather than copied: created `gardener_account` as a separate table from `gardener` (its own `status` column already means application-review status, and overloading it with login status would conflate two different concepts under one column — a mistake this report has flagged before), and created it only at approval time rather than HomeSewa's early-reservation pattern, since Garden Sewa's `gardener` table has no duplicate-phone constraint to protect. Found GardenSewa's `admin_sessions` table couldn't represent a gardener session at all — `admin_id` was a NOT NULL foreign key with nothing for a gardener session to reference — and rather than copy HomeSewa's looser FK-less session design (just a denormalized phone string), added a second nullable foreign key plus a check constraint guaranteeing exactly one owner is ever set, keeping real referential integrity HomeSewa's own version doesn't have. `approve-gardener` now provisions the login account and SMS's the PIN in the same action as approval, mirroring `approve-professional` exactly, with idempotent reactivation if an account already exists rather than crashing on the unique-phone constraint. Added a real auth guard to the `(professional)` route group, which had been completely open to anyone regardless of login state since no real gardener login existed yet to gate it. Full live test cycle — create a test application, approve it through the real function, confirm the account was provisioned, log in with a known test PIN, confirm the session row's shape and the check constraint, confirm re-approval doesn't duplicate or crash, confirm zero regression on existing admin logins — then deleted every trace of test data.

Customer OTP login was the one where checking first changed the actual design. Before building anything, searched HomeSewa's real migrations and client code for its customer login system and found there isn't one: no PIN, no session table, no login endpoint at all. A customer in HomeSewa is just a phone number, OTP-verified once, remembered locally on the device, with the `customers` database row itself created as a side effect of a booking-insert trigger, not a dedicated signup flow. Building the heavier PIN+session pattern used for admin/gardener accounts would have been over-engineering relative to what HomeSewa actually does, so matched the lighter real model instead: refactored the OTP hash/expiry/attempt-lockout logic out of `verify-otp` into a shared helper first (so the new flow wouldn't duplicate it), then built a `customer-login` function that verifies the code and upserts the `customer` row in one call, preserving an existing name rather than wiping it when a returning customer leaves the name field blank on a repeat login. Built the app's first-ever real call site for `AuthContext.login()` — grepped the whole app first and confirmed it had never actually been invoked from anywhere, meaning customer "login" had been entirely dead, unreachable code up to this point — plus the Logout action that was missing for the same reason. Live-tested new-customer creation, replay protection on an already-consumed code, name preservation on a blank-name resubmit, and confirmed the `verify-otp` refactor caused zero regression in the existing gardener OTP flow, then cleaned up all test rows.

Closed the day by translating both today's and yesterday's technical work into a plain-language status update for HR, organized by role rather than by ticket, and by adding this entry to the report at HR's request.

**Learning:**
The customer-login task was the clearest instance yet of "read the real source before designing" saving real rework: the natural instinct, having just built PIN+session auth twice today for admin and gardener accounts, would have been to build a third copy of the same pattern for customers — and it would have been strictly wrong, over-engineered relative to what the actual reference system does. HomeSewa's own customer model is deliberately the lightest of the three (OTP-verified phone, no session, no PIN), and only checking its real migrations and client code before writing anything caught that before a heavier, unnecessary system got built. The admin_sessions foreign-key gap was the same lesson in the opposite direction — HomeSewa's own session design has a real gap (no FK at all, just a denormalized string), and "same as HomeSewa" was correctly read as "same behavior," not "same technical debt," matching Day 88's explicit precedent of tightening rather than inheriting a known gap.

**Skills Gained:**
Reading a reference system's actual source for three separate, differently-shaped auth patterns (admin PIN+session, professional PIN+session-with-early-reservation, customer OTP-only-no-session) in one day and correctly matching each new one to what its real counterpart does, rather than applying one uniform pattern by default · resolving a genuine bootstrap chicken-and-egg problem (every account-creation path requires a session that can't yet exist) with a function gated by two independent, non-bypassable conditions, then fully removing it once used rather than leaving unnecessary standing capability · extending a session table to support two mutually-exclusive owner types via a database check constraint, improving on the reference system's own looser design rather than copying its gap forward · refactoring shared verification logic out to a common helper specifically to keep a new caller from duplicating it, then using that same refactor as a regression check against the existing caller · grepping an entire codebase for real call sites before trusting that an existing auth function was reachable, rather than assuming defined-and-exported meant used · running a full create-approve-login-verify-cleanup live test cycle against a production database for two different account types in one session and leaving zero residue in either case.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) Admin account creation requires a Super Admin session, but the `admin` table was empty — no valid way to create the first account through the normal path. Resolved with a one-time function gated by both a random secret and an empty-table check, so it was safe even before use and impossible to reuse after, then deleted entirely once it had served its purpose. (2) GardenSewa's `admin_sessions` table structurally couldn't hold a gardener session — `admin_id` was a required foreign key with nothing for a gardener to reference. Resolved by adding a second nullable foreign key with a check constraint enforcing exactly one owner, rather than either breaking the existing admin session shape or copying HomeSewa's own FK-less, looser design. (3) The instinct after building two PIN+session auth systems in one day was to build a third the same way for customers, which would have been over-engineered relative to HomeSewa's real, much lighter customer model. Resolved by checking HomeSewa's actual migrations and client code first, which surfaced that customers have no PIN or session there at all, and matching that lighter design instead of the pattern already used twice that day.

**Message to Mentor:**
Completed all three Phase 1 items today: admin account management, gardener/professional login, and customer OTP login — each built against HomeSewa's real source rather than assumption, with real, deliberate adaptations where GardenSewa's schema already diverged (bcrypt PIN hashing, closed anon access, a tighter session foreign-key design) rather than blind copying. Solved a genuine bootstrap problem for the first Super Admin account with a self-disabling one-time function, since every normal account-creation path needs a session that couldn't yet exist — removed it completely once used. Gardeners can now actually log in once approved, with their PIN delivered by SMS in the same action as approval, and the previously wide-open professional area of the app is now properly gated behind that login. For customer login, checking HomeSewa's real implementation first caught that it needed a much lighter design than admin/gardener auth — no PIN, no session, just a verified phone — which is what got built, including the app's actual first working login/logout capability (the existing scaffolding had never been wired to anything real). Every piece was live-tested end to end against the production database with test data fully cleaned up afterward, and zero regressions confirmed on existing admin/gardener login paths. Also sent you a plain-language progress update covering both today's and yesterday's work by role rather than by ticket, per your question about where things stand.

### Day 91 — 04 September 2026 (Friday)

**Progress:**
Moved into Phase 2 — the booking/lead system — starting the same way every prior piece has: reading HomeSewa's real, current implementation before designing anything. That reading surfaced that HomeSewa had already dropped Khalti entirely (documented in this report back on Day 84/85) in favor of a static-QR-plus-photo-proof-plus-admin-review model for its lead-unlock paywall, which meant the actual task wasn't a payment gateway integration at all — it was porting a much simpler, already-working pattern. Confirmed two business-model questions directly rather than assuming: whether GardenSewa should copy HomeSewa's pay-to-unlock-contact model exactly (confirmed yes), and whether GardenSewa's payment QR should point to the same `sriyog.com/payment` URL HomeSewa uses, since both apps share the same parent company (confirmed yes, same URL).

Built the `booking` table and, for the first time, wired `book.js` — a fully built form UI that, like the gardener join form before Day 88, had zero backend behind it; its "Confirm Booking" button just showed a fake success alert. While testing the new table's real anon-key insert, hit a genuine platform quirk: `has_table_privilege('anon', 'gardener', 'INSERT')` returns false, `pg_class.relacl` shows no INSERT grant for anon on either `gardener` or `booking`, yet the live REST API successfully inserts into `gardener` and fails with a real `42501 permission denied` on `booking`. Chased this through `information_schema.role_table_grants`, `pg_class.relacl`, `has_table_privilege()`, and table ownership before concluding the grant materialization on this project is inconsistent depending on how/when a table was created, not something explainable from the SQL alone — fixed pragmatically with an explicit `GRANT INSERT` rather than relying on RLS-policy-only, and documented the finding in the migration so the same trap doesn't get hit blind next time.

Built the masked open-jobs listing (`list-open-bookings`) — a gardener sees a customer's name masked and no phone until they unlock it, enforced server-side rather than HomeSewa's real, actually-open equivalent (its `booking` table has zero RLS at all, so anyone holding the anon key can already read every customer's raw phone number regardless of what the UI shows — a real gap HomeSewa carries as documented debt, closed here from the start the same way Day 88 closed the equivalent gardener-table gap). Built the full lead-unlock pipeline — `submit-lead-unlock-proof`, `list/approve/reject-lead-unlock`, and `accept-booking` with an atomic CAS-guarded claim — then ran a complete live cycle against the production database: created a test booking, confirmed it came back masked, submitted proof, reviewed and approved it as super admin, confirmed the same booking now returned unmasked, accepted it, confirmed a second accept attempt correctly failed, then separately confirmed a gardener who never unlocked a job is blocked from accepting it, and that a rejected request stays blocked. All test data deleted afterward.

Wired the client side into the existing (but previously mock or fake) professional booking screens rather than building new ones from scratch: the booking list and detail screens now pull real data instead of `Data.ts` mock rows, and `pay.js` — previously a fake Khalti mockup that marked a booking "paid" with zero verification the moment the button was tapped — is now the real static-QR-plus-screenshot-upload flow. Built a new admin review screen for lead-unlock requests, mirroring the gardener-applications screen's approve/reject pattern.

Closed the day on job completion, ratings, and chat. Reading HomeSewa's real `booking_ratings`/`booking_messages` migrations surfaced that both tables have zero RLS at all — fully open to the anon key for every booking, documented as accepted debt because neither customers nor professionals authenticate via Supabase Auth there. Rather than copy that gap forward wholesale, split the difference honestly: GardenSewa's gardener side does have a real, verifiable session, so every gardener-side write on ratings/chat/completion goes through a session-checked Edge Function instead of an open table; the customer side genuinely has no session to check against in this app's model either (confirmed back on Day 90), so that half still trusts a client-claimed phone number, same real limitation HomeSewa has, not fixed today because it can't be without customers having a session at all. Built `complete-booking` as one atomic call combining OTP verification and the status write, closing a small real race HomeSewa's own version has (its client calls `verify-otp` and a separate booking update independently, so nothing actually guarantees the write only happens because the OTP checked out). Replaced `[id].js`'s job-completion flow, which had been a fully fake local OTP simulator generating and comparing a code against itself with no server involved, with the real thing — an OTP sent to the customer, required completion photos, and a call into the new function. Live-tested completion (including the correctly-rejected case of confirming before an OTP was ever sent), two-way ratings in both directions plus a rejected wrong-phone-claim attempt, and chat send/read from both sides plus a correctly-rejected unauthorized read — cleaning up all test data afterward.

**Learning:**
The grant-materialization inconsistency was the sharpest instance yet of a lesson this report keeps circling back to: a security-relevant fact can be flatly wrong by every introspection tool available (`has_table_privilege`, `role_table_grants`, `pg_class.relacl` all agreed anon had no INSERT on `gardener`) while the live system does something else entirely, and the only way to know which one is real is to fire the actual request and watch what happens — not to trust the query that's supposed to answer the question. The HomeSewa-parity work on ratings/messages was the same lesson applied to a design decision instead of a bug: "same as HomeSewa" was correctly read, again, as copying real *behavior* where GardenSewa has the means to do better (a verifiable gardener session) and accepting the same real *limitation* where it doesn't (no customer session exists in either app), rather than mechanically porting HomeSewa's exact table permissions either way.

**Skills Gained:**
Diagnosing a live discrepancy between every available Postgres privilege-introspection method and actual REST API behavior by testing the real request path directly rather than trusting `information_schema`/`pg_catalog` in isolation · designing a masked-until-unlocked data model where the sensitive fields are withheld server-side entirely rather than sent to the client and hidden by the UI, closing a gap the reference implementation actually has · running a full multi-stage live test cycle (create → mask → unlock request → review → approve → unmask → accept → duplicate-accept rejection → no-unlock rejection → reject-path rejection) against a production database in one session and fully cleaning up afterward · consciously choosing which half of a reference system's security posture to inherit and which to improve, based on which side actually has the technical means to be improved · folding two dependent operations (OTP verification, a conditional status write) that a reference implementation does as two independent client calls into one atomic server-side operation to close the gap between them · retrofitting real backend calls into UI screens that had either no backend at all or a fully fake one, without needing to rebuild the UI itself.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) A newly created table's anon INSERT failed with a real permission error despite an RLS policy identical in shape to an existing table's working policy, and every privilege-introspection query available said neither table should work. Resolved by testing the real request directly rather than trusting the introspection tools, finding the actual difference (a missing base GRANT) empirically, and fixing it with an explicit grant documented for future migrations rather than continuing to trust RLS-policy-only. (2) HomeSewa's real ratings/chat tables are fully open with no RLS, which would have been the literal reading of "keep the backend the same as HomeSewa" but was a real, documented security gap there, not a deliberate design worth reproducing on a table GardenSewa didn't already have committed to that pattern. Resolved by splitting the inherited posture along the one line that actually matters — which side has a real session to check — rather than either blindly copying the gap or over-building session infrastructure (a persistent customer login) that hasn't been asked for and that HomeSewa itself doesn't have either.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today built out Phase 2, the booking and lead-unlock system, grounded throughout in HomeSewa's actual current implementation (which turned out to have already dropped Khalti for a simpler static-QR-and-photo-proof model, confirmed with you along with reusing the same SRIYOG payment URL). Wired the customer booking form to a real backend for the first time — it had a complete UI with nothing behind it — and along the way found and fixed a real platform quirk where this project's grant system disagreed with itself about whether a new table's anon insert privilege existed, documented so it doesn't get missed again. Built the full lead-unlock pipeline (masked job listings, payment-proof submission, admin review, atomic accept with double-accept protection) and live-tested the entire cycle end to end against the production database before cleaning up every trace of test data. Wired the previously mock or fake professional booking screens to all of it, including replacing a fake "Pay Now" button that marked jobs paid with zero verification with the real QR-and-proof flow, and added an admin review screen for payment requests. Closed with job completion, ratings, and chat — deliberately keeping GardenSewa's gardener side properly session-verified rather than copying HomeSewa's fully-open equivalent tables, while being upfront that the customer side carries the same real limitation HomeSewa has (no customer session exists to verify against in either app). One gap flagged rather than guessed at: there's no customer-facing UI yet for viewing bookings, chatting, or rating a gardener, since no such screen exists in the app at all — that needs a product decision from you before it's worth building. Handed over a commit message for today's work rather than pushing it myself.

### Day 92 — 06 September 2026 (Sunday)

**Progress:**
HR brought a new requirement: a request-submission path for customers who call in rather than use the app — a keypad phone, or just not comfortable with a smartphone — submitted by a BDM on their behalf, with one explicit twist: no OTP goes to the customer at all, confirmation happens via an OTP to the BDM's own phone instead. Built `submit-booking-for-customer`, scoped to the role HR named, inserting the booking with two new audit columns (`booking_source`, `submitted_by_admin_id`) so a BDM-submitted request is never indistinguishable from a self-service one, then issuing the confirmation code to the BDM's own number pulled from their account, never the caller-supplied customer phone. While building it, caught and fixed a real duplication problem before it shipped: the first draft re-implemented OTP generation/hashing/rate-limiting inline instead of reusing what `send-otp` already does, which would have silently skipped the daily-cap and resend-cooldown protection every other OTP purpose gets. Refactored that shared logic out of `send-otp`'s handler into an `issueOtp` helper in `_shared/otp.ts` (same move already made for the verification half via `checkOtp`), so `send-otp` became a thin wrapper and the new function reused the exact same path — then re-ran a live regression check on an existing purpose to confirm the refactor changed nothing about current behavior. Live-tested the full BDM flow end to end (submit → confirm OTP lands on the BDM's own phone, not the customer's → booking correctly tagged) before cleaning up. Asked directly which roles should actually have this — HR's literal ask was BDM-only, but Call Center is the role this app's own model already built for fielding inbound customer calls, which is exactly HR's scenario — got confirmation to widen it, then live-tested Call Center access working and every other role (tried Super Admin specifically) correctly rejected.

Moved on to wiring push notifications into the booking lifecycle, since nothing built over the last two days — lead-unlock approve/reject, a booking being accepted, a job finishing — actually notified anyone. Rebuilt `send-notification` from its one hardcoded purpose into the same purpose-handler shape already used for OTP: each new trigger resolves its own recipient and message entirely server-side from a database id the caller supplies, rather than trusting a client-supplied phone number directly — closing off a real way this could otherwise have been abused to push arbitrary content to an arbitrary number now that it has genuine per-user targets instead of only a fixed admin-lookup. Wired all four triggers (lead-unlock approved/rejected notifying the gardener, booking-accepted and job-completed notifying the customer) into their respective screens. That surfaced a gap the customer side never had: nothing ever registered a customer's device with OneSignal at all, since customers have no login by default. Fixed it by registering at the two real points a customer's phone gets confirmed — the anonymous booking OTP step and the explicit customer login — mirroring exactly where HomeSewa's own `BookingOtp.tsx` does the same registration rather than requiring a separate login flow. Live-tested all four new triggers plus an invalid-id case (correctly returns "nothing to send" rather than erroring) and a regression check on the original gardener-application trigger, confirmed the audit log recorded each one correctly, then cleaned up every test row.

Closed the day on something overdue rather than another feature: with two full days of new screens and functions verified only through direct API calls and never through the actual app, recommended stopping to actually run and click through everything before building more, and got agreement to do exactly that. No emulator or device was already running, so started with the fastest available path — `expo start --web` — as a first pass rather than waiting on a full native dev-client build. That alone was worth doing immediately: the web bundle crashed outright with a `TurboModuleRegistry.getEnforcing` error traced to `AdminAuthContext.js`, from `react-native-onesignal`'s own top-level code reaching for a native module that doesn't exist on web. Every actual call site already had a `Platform.OS !== 'web'` guard around it — the bug was that a static `import` statement runs unconditionally regardless of any runtime check wrapped around its call sites later in the file, so guarding usage was never enough on its own. Fixed it by centralizing the import behind a `Platform`-guarded `require()` in one new `lib/oneSignal.js`, so every one of the four affected files imports from that safe module instead of the raw package directly, and confirmed the web bundle now loads cleanly with no crash. Was setting up Playwright to actually drive the running app and screenshot real screens — book.js, the professional booking flow, the admin review screens, the new BDM form — when its browser-binary download timed out against an external CDN, which is where the day's testing pass stands: the web build itself is now proven not to crash, but the deeper interactive pass through the real screens is still incomplete, and a genuine native build is still needed regardless to verify OneSignal actually functions on-device rather than merely not crashing when it's absent.

**Learning:**
The `react-native-onesignal` web crash was a precise, costly-if-unfound version of a distinction this report hasn't had to draw before: guarding *how a value is used* is not the same as guarding *whether it's loaded at all*. Every call site was already correctly wrapped, and the bug shipped anyway, because a static `import` at the top of a module is not conditional in any way a runtime `if` statement inside that same module can affect — it always executes, on every platform, before any of that guarded code ever runs. This only became visible by actually starting the app; nothing about reading the source, or the fact that every call site looked correctly defensive, would have surfaced it, which is itself the whole argument for treating "run it and look" as a real step rather than something a careful enough read can substitute for.

**Skills Gained:**
Distinguishing between guarding a value's usage and guarding whether its import executes at all, and fixing a platform-incompatible native module's crash at the module-loading level rather than only at each call site · designing an audit trail (`booking_source`, `submitted_by_admin_id`) for an alternate creation path specifically so it stays distinguishable from the default path it parallels, rather than only building the happy path · catching a duplication regression in my own first draft — re-implementing rate-limited OTP issuance inline instead of reusing the existing helper — before it shipped, and correcting course by extracting the shared logic properly instead of leaving two divergent implementations · designing a notification-recipient model that resolves the actual target from a server-side id lookup specifically to prevent a client from ever supplying a push target directly · recognizing when a feature request's literal scope (a named role) differs from its actual intent (whoever handles the described scenario) and confirming the wider scope explicitly rather than either guessing or under-building to the letter of the ask · choosing the fastest available verification path (`expo start --web`) over waiting on a slower, more complete one (a native dev-client build) when the goal is catching bugs quickly, while being honest about what that faster path can't prove.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) A first draft of the new BDM-booking OTP confirmation re-implemented generation, hashing, and rate-limiting inline rather than reusing `send-otp`'s existing logic, which would have quietly shipped without the daily-cap/cooldown protection every other OTP purpose already has. Resolved by extracting that logic into a shared `issueOtp` helper before deploying anything, then verifying an existing purpose still worked identically afterward. (2) The web bundle crashed with no clear connection between the error (a native-module lookup failure) and its actual cause (an unconditional top-level import), and every call site already looked correctly guarded, which would normally suggest the bug was elsewhere. Resolved by recognizing that a static import's own side effects run regardless of any guard placed around later usage in the same file, and moving the import itself behind a runtime-conditional `require()` in one shared module instead of four separate ones. (3) Wanted a full interactive, screenshot-verified pass through the real app, but no device or emulator was already available and Playwright's own browser download failed against an external network dependency mid-setup. Resolved pragmatically rather than blocking on it — used the faster web path to catch a real, high-value bug immediately, and left the deeper visual pass explicitly marked incomplete rather than either waiting indefinitely or reporting a check that hadn't actually finished.

**Message to Mentor:**
Built the BDM/Call-Center request-submission feature you asked for today — a customer who calls in rather than using the app can have their request submitted on their behalf, with confirmation going to the staff member's own phone rather than the customer's, exactly as specified. Along the way, caught my own first draft duplicating OTP logic that already existed and fixed it by sharing the real implementation instead. When you asked which roles should have access, flagged that Call Center — the role this app already treats as the inbound-call-handling one — fits the actual scenario better than BDM alone, and widened it once you agreed, live-testing both the new access and that every other role still gets rejected. Then wired push notifications into the whole booking lifecycle from the last two days, which until today notified no one when a payment was approved/rejected, a job got accepted, or work was completed — including registering customer devices for push for the first time, at the same point HomeSewa's own app does it (the booking OTP step), since customers otherwise never touch a login screen at all. Closed the day doing something I should flag as a genuine process win regardless of the specific bug: stopped building new features to actually run the app for the first time since the last two days of screens went in, and immediately found a real crash that no amount of re-reading the source would have caught — an OneSignal import that crashed the entire web build despite every actual usage already being correctly guarded. Fixed it cleanly. The deeper visual walkthrough of the real screens is not finished — Playwright's browser download failed against an external CDN mid-setup — so that remains open for next time, and a genuine on-device build is still owed regardless to confirm OneSignal actually works rather than merely fails safely in its absence.

### Day 93 — 07 September 2026 (Monday)

**Progress:**
Started from a bug report: after logging in, the drawer still showed a login button. Traced it to the drawer only ever checking the customer login context and never the separate admin/gardener one — so an admin, BDM, or gardener session left the drawer thinking nobody was logged in at all. Fixed it by having the drawer read both identities, and once logged in as staff, show a "logged in as [name] · [role]" line with Update Profile and Logout instead of either login button.

That surfaced the next real problem immediately: Update Profile itself didn't work. It turned out to be a leftover mock screen — hardcoded fake name and phone, a fake save button that only popped an alert and never touched the database — sitting in the wrong part of the app and not linked from anywhere a user could actually reach. Rebuilt it for real: two new functions (`get-my-profile`, `update-my-profile`) that resolve the logged-in account from its session and split editable fields by role, the same way HomeSewa's own version splits a plain admin (name and photo only) from a full professional profile (email, expertise, experience, city). Added a missing `photo_url` column for admin accounts, moved the screen out from under the gardener-only route group so every role could reach it, and wired it into the new drawer menu. Live-tested a real read and write against the real admin account before moving on.

The next ask was bigger: build pages after login "like HomeSewa and RocketSingh" — customer booking details, gardener approvals, and so on. Rather than guess at what that meant, ran two research passes through the actual HomeSewa and RocketSingh source and found something worth knowing before building anything: neither app actually has a stats-tile dashboard. Every role in both apps lands on a filtered list screen and reaches everything else through a role-gated drawer — which is structurally what this app already had for admin and gardener. So instead of inventing a dashboard neither reference app has, built the real matching piece: expanded the drawer into a proper role-gated Staff Menu linking to every back-office and gardener screen, the same way HomeSewa's and RocketSingh's drawers reveal different destinations per role. On the customer side there was a genuine, confirmed gap — no booking-tracking screen existed at all, and the drawer's own "Booking History" link was quietly broken, pointing at the gardener-only screen, which would have bounced a customer straight to Admin Login. Built a real My Bookings list and a detail screen (gardener contact reveal once assigned, in-app chat, a post-completion star rating) backed by a new `list-my-bookings` function, and fixed the broken link.

With the drawer now carrying every back-office link, the old header-icon row on Gardener Applications was pure duplication, so removed it.

Ran the same two-agent research process again, this time a full feature inventory rather than just dashboards, specifically to answer "is anything missing" honestly instead of guessing from memory. That turned up two things that were actively broken, not just absent: the "Become a Partner" form's OTP call was missing a required parameter so it failed every time, with no database table behind it even if it had succeeded; and the Notifications screen was rendering a hardcoded fake list, despite push notifications actually working under the hood.

Fixed both. Become a Partner now uploads its photos, inserts a real row, sends a real OTP, and notifies admins — the same working shape as the Join-as-a-Gardener form — with a new admin review screen to go with it. For notifications, found the app already had an audit-log table built for exactly this, down to a code comment predicting "a future My Notifications screen reads this" — the write path just never populated it correctly and nothing had ever read it back. Fixed the write to log a real audience and recipient per push, added a `list-my-notifications` function scoped correctly per identity (session for staff and gardeners, phone-trust for customers, matching the booking list), and replaced the fake screen with the real feed.

Live-tested the full loop for both features rather than trusting the code alone — inserted a real test booking, triggered a real push through the actual send path, and confirmed it showed up in the right phone's inbox and nowhere else. Mid-testing, the real admin account's PIN stopped matching, from unrelated concurrent work happening on the same live project rather than anything done here — flagged it plainly instead of guessing around it, got the corrected PIN, and re-ran every session-gated check against it before closing out the day's leftover test rows.

**Learning:**
The dashboard research was the most valuable hour of the day precisely because it disproved something I would otherwise have just built: the assumption that "make it like HomeSewa and RocketSingh" meant a stats-tile dashboard. Actually reading both real codebases first, rather than pattern-matching from what admin panels usually look like, turned up that neither one has that at all — and building it anyway would have been effort spent matching an imagined reference instead of the real one. The second lesson was quieter but showed up twice today in almost the same shape: both the fake Update Profile screen and the fake Notifications screen looked, at a glance, like working features — right layout, right copy, a button that did something. Only tracing what actually happened on submit (an alert with no network call, a local array with no query) revealed they were dead ends. "It renders correctly" and "it works" turned out to be a distinction worth checking explicitly, not assuming from the UI alone.

**Skills Gained:**
Diagnosing a UI bug (stale login button) back to a state model gap (two separate auth contexts, one ignored) rather than patching the symptom in place · auditing existing screens by tracing what their submit handlers actually do over the network, not just what they render, to tell a real feature apart from a convincing-looking mock · using targeted research against real reference codebases to settle a design question before building, instead of assuming the answer · designing a per-viewer read model (notification inbox) across three different identity types in the same app — session-verified staff, session-verified gardener, and a phone-trusted customer with no session at all — and keeping each one scoped to only what it should see · recognizing and completing a feature a previous comment in the codebase had already predicted, instead of re-solving a problem someone had already designed for · live-testing a full write-then-read loop end to end (trigger a real push, confirm it lands only for the right recipient) rather than trusting each function in isolation · treating an unexpected credential failure during testing as a signal to ask, not a bug to work around silently.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The reported bug ("login button after login") had a root cause one level deeper than the report itself — the drawer wasn't failing to hide a button, it was reading the wrong auth state entirely. Resolved by tracing which context actually held the truth for each login type before touching any UI. (2) Both Update Profile and Notifications looked like working screens at first glance, and treating the underlying ask ("build pages like HomeSewa and RocketSingh") as new work would have skipped over the fact that existing screens were already broken. Resolved by testing every submit/save action against what it actually persisted, not just how it looked, before deciding what needed to be built versus fixed. (3) "Build it like HomeSewa and RocketSingh" was genuinely ambiguous without more information — building the wrong interpretation (a dashboard) would have been wasted, convincing-looking work. Resolved by researching both real apps first and being upfront that the assumption behind the request didn't hold, rather than silently building the more common pattern instead of the real one. (4) A live admin credential stopped working mid-testing for a reason outside today's own changes. Resolved by disclosing it immediately and precisely rather than guessing at a fix or quietly working around it.

**Message to Mentor:**
Fixed the login-button bug you'd want fixed regardless, and while tracing it found the real Update Profile screen underneath it was fake — never actually saved anything. Rebuilt both today, and live-tested the fix against the real account. For the bigger ask — pages after login like HomeSewa and RocketSingh — I looked at both real apps before building anything, and found neither one actually has a stats dashboard; they use a role-gated drawer menu instead, which this app already partly had. So I built the real version of that (a proper Staff Menu covering every back-office and gardener screen) and, more importantly, built the customer-facing booking tracking screen that was genuinely missing entirely, with chat and rating included. Then did a full side-by-side feature audit against both apps to answer honestly whether anything else was missing, and that caught two things that looked fine but were actually broken: the Become a Partner form and the Notifications screen. Fixed both today and live-tested the complete flow for each — a real submission through Become a Partner, and a real push notification showing up correctly in one phone's inbox and nowhere else. One thing to flag: the real admin account's PIN changed mid-day from work happening elsewhere on the same project, not from anything here — you already reset it and I've re-verified everything against the new one.
