### Day 94 — 08 September 2026 (Tuesday)

**Progress:**
Started the day trying to run an EAS build and hit a wall before any build even queued: `app.json` pointed at an EAS project (`owner: "pratigyaluitel"`) that the logged-in account had no access to at all. Rather than guess at a fix, confirmed the actual account state first — `eas whoami` showed the session was authenticated as `mr.ded`, with real access to two accounts (`mr.ded` and `sriyog`), and `eas project:info` against the existing `projectId` came back with a hard `Entity not authorized` error, proving the config itself was stale rather than a login problem. Checked the Expo dashboard directly rather than assuming which account the project should live under, and found a real, already-existing project — `sriyog/gardensewa` — correctly named, linked to the right GitHub repo, and reachable with the current session. Relinked `app.json`'s `owner` and `extra.eas.projectId` to it and verified the fix by re-running `eas project:info`, which resolved cleanly to `@sriyog/gardensewa`. Also caught and flagged a second, unrelated blocker on the same build attempt — the locally installed `eas-cli` didn't satisfy the minimum CLI version pinned in `eas.json` — and worked around it with `npx eas-cli@latest` while recommending a global CLI upgrade for regular use. Committed the project-link fix on its own.

Moved on to testing the app on a real iPhone via Expo Go and hit a second, unrelated blocker: the project's Expo SDK 54 was incompatible with the installed Expo Go app, which only supports the latest SDK (57) on iOS, with no way to install an older Expo Go version on a real device. Weighed the two real fixes — upgrading the project to SDK 57 (free, but a 3-major-version dependency jump) versus building a custom dev client (avoids the SDK lock-in permanently, but requires a paid Apple Developer account to distribute to a device) — and since no Apple Developer account exists yet, dev-client distribution wasn't actually available today, so went with the SDK upgrade. Ran `expo install expo@^57 --fix`, which pulled up all `expo-*` packages, React, and React Native to their SDK 57–compatible versions, and confirmed the result was healthy with `expo-doctor` (21/21 checks passed) before starting the dev server, which came up clean with no SDK-mismatch error this time. Hit a separate, unrelated blocker there too — Expo Go requires its own account sign-in on the device, distinct from the CLI's login — and left that as a next step rather than a code problem.

Before doing anything further with the SDK bump, weighed it again with a step back: the upgrade was still fully uncommitted, and a 3-version jump touching `react-native-reanimated`, `react-native-worklets`, and the OneSignal plugin carried more native-dependency risk than the moment actually called for, versus just continuing device testing on Android in the meantime. Reverted it cleanly — discarded the uncommitted `app.json`/`package.json`/`package-lock.json` changes with `git checkout` and reinstalled `node_modules` to match the restored SDK 54 lockfile — confirming the working tree was back to exactly its last committed, known-good state.

**Learning:**
Both blockers today shared the same root lesson: a config file's claims (which EAS project this points to, which SDK Expo Go expects) are not the same as the live, authoritative state, and the only reliable way to tell the difference is to ask the real system directly — `eas whoami`/`eas project:info` for account/project truth, the Expo dashboard for what actually exists under an account — rather than editing config on assumption. The SDK-upgrade reversal was a smaller but related lesson: because the upgrade had been kept uncommitted while it was still being evaluated, discovering it wasn't the right call for today cost nothing to undo. Validating a risky, wide-reaching change live before committing it — and being willing to walk it back cleanly when the risk doesn't clear the bar — is what made the reversal a non-event instead of a rollback.

**Skills Gained:**
Diagnosing an EAS "not authorized" error back to a stale `owner`/`projectId` in config rather than a login problem, by cross-referencing `eas whoami`'s actual account list against the project the config claimed to point to · confirming a project's real identity on the Expo dashboard before relinking local config to it, instead of guessing at an account name · performing a major Expo SDK upgrade (`expo install --fix`) and validating it with `expo-doctor` before trusting it enough to test on-device · recognizing that Apple's real-device restriction (only the latest Expo Go SDK installable on iOS) forces a choice between an SDK upgrade and a paid dev-client path, and picking the free one when the paid prerequisite isn't available yet · keeping a wide-reaching dependency change uncommitted specifically so it stays cheaply reversible while still being evaluated, then actually reverting it cleanly with `git checkout` plus a matching `node_modules` reinstall once it was judged riskier than the task warranted.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) `app.json` referenced an EAS project under an account with no real access, which failed silently as a permissions error rather than an obvious config error. Resolved by confirming actual account access via `eas whoami`, locating the real matching project on the Expo dashboard, and relinking `app.json` to it, verified with a clean `eas project:info` resolution. (2) The locally installed `eas-cli` didn't meet the minimum version `eas.json` requires for builds. Resolved by using `npx eas-cli@latest` for the immediate command and flagging a global upgrade for ongoing use. (3) Expo Go on a real iPhone only supports the latest SDK, and the project was three versions behind, with no Apple Developer account yet available for the alternative dev-client path. Resolved by upgrading the project to SDK 57 and validating it with `expo-doctor` before testing. (4) After the SDK upgrade proved technically clean, judged on reflection that the 3-version native-dependency jump was riskier than the day's actual goal (unblocking device testing) warranted. Resolved by reverting the still-uncommitted changes with `git checkout` and reinstalling `node_modules` to restore the known-good SDK 54 state, at zero cost since nothing had been committed yet.

**Message to Mentor:**
Spent today unblocking build and device-testing infrastructure rather than adding features. Found that `app.json` had been silently pointing at an EAS project under an account nobody currently has access to, which would have blocked every future build attempt with a permissions error rather than an obvious config error — traced it to the real cause, found the actual existing `sriyog/gardensewa` project on the dashboard, relinked and verified it, and committed that fix on its own. Separately, tried to test the app on a real iPhone via Expo Go and hit an SDK mismatch — iOS only allows the latest Expo Go SDK, and the project was three versions behind. Since there's no Apple Developer account yet to build a proper dev client instead, upgraded the project to the latest SDK as the free alternative, confirmed it installed and booted cleanly (`expo-doctor` all green, dev server started with no errors). On reflection, judged that upgrade riskier than today's actual goal warranted — a 3-version jump touching push-notification and animation native modules — so reverted it back to the known-good SDK 54 state before committing anything, keeping today's actual shipped change limited to the EAS project link fix. Flagging one open item for you: getting a real device build tested on iOS will need either that SDK upgrade done deliberately (with full regression testing) or an Apple Developer account for a dev-client build — your call on which path and when.

### Day 95 — 09 September 2026 (Wednesday)

**Progress:**
HR opened the day with a UX question rather than a fixed instruction: why did the drawer show two separate login entry points, a customer "Login / Sign Up" button and a separate Admin Login button. The two possible fixes had genuinely different consequences — dropping customer accounts entirely versus hiding staff login from the public — so confirmed which one HR actually meant before touching anything rather than guessing from the wording. HR chose to drop the customer entry point; deliberately left the customer-only screens it fed (My Bookings, Favorites, customer notifications, Update Profile, Reset PIN) in place rather than deleting them, since they're recently built, working features already documented earlier in this report, and removing them hadn't actually been asked for.

Fixed a genuinely nagging home-screen popup ad firing on every single visit by adding a 24-hour AsyncStorage cooldown, reusing the same pattern already used elsewhere in the app for a different spam guard rather than inventing a new mechanism. HR also asked that photo uploads across the app stop forcing a crop, keeping that only for the profile picture — audited every image-picker call site in the app and found one screen sharing a single picker function between a profile photo and an identity-document photo, so made the crop conditional on which one was being picked rather than a blanket removal, since a cropped ID photo could cut off required information. Separately removed a booking-form validation bug that still required at least one photo despite HR wanting photos fully optional, and fixed two real calendar bugs — an ending-date calendar visually marking "today" as selectable even after a chosen start date had already pushed it out of the valid range — in both the customer booking form and the gardener's reschedule screen.

The larger piece of the day was a push-notification broadcast feature "like HomeSewa." Found the actual HomeSewa codebase available locally and read its real `AdminNotifications.tsx`/`api/notifications.ts` implementation before building anything, rather than guessing at its shape from the name alone. Built a first version scoped to three audiences, then rebuilt it twice more across several rounds of direct feedback — including screenshots of HomeSewa's actual running drawer and notification screen — to reach real parity: a "Send To" tab picker across five audiences (Admin, Customer with an optional service filter, Professional/gardener with a required service-and-city filter resolved against the real `gardener`/`gardener_account` tables, Public, and a broadcast to everyone), an "All Notifications" history tab, and, per an explicit correction partway through, composing restricted to Super Admin only rather than Admin as well, matching HomeSewa's own gate exactly. Each new audience needed its own database value and OneSignal targeting shape, added across two follow-up migrations rather than one, since the scope kept widening honestly with each round of feedback instead of being fully specified up front.

The same screenshots drove a full drawer rebuild: moved Admin Login from a large block near the top down to the bottom, matched HomeSewa's icon and font sizing exactly, and made the whole drawer non-scrollable by mirroring its `space-evenly` layout instead of a `ScrollView` — which for a logged-in staff member also meant trimming the drawer down to only their own role's tools rather than still showing every public link, the same way HomeSewa's own staff view does. Caught and corrected my own inconsistency twice in this pass after the user pointed at screenshots showing one group of menu items sized differently from another within the same drawer state. The reference screenshots also showed a "Popup Banner" admin feature and the logged-in user's phone number under their name — built both: persisted the admin's phone through login for the header, and built a real admin-editable popup-banner system (a new table, three Edge Functions, an admin management screen with image upload, and rewired the earlier home-screen promo popup to read from it live instead of hardcoded content) — deliberately narrower than HomeSewa's own version, which also does city/role targeting and scheduling this app has no matching customer-segmentation data to support.

Every backend change today went through the same deploy discipline: pushed each migration and redeployed each Edge Function only on explicit confirmation, and ran a full local build/lint/typecheck pass — `eslint`, a `tsc` check against every edited Edge Function, and a full Metro/`expo export` bundle — before any of it went live, catching mistakes locally rather than in production.

Closed the day on verification rather than more building. Asked to seed dummy data to confirm the new gardener-application notification actually reaches Super Admin/Admin and shows up in Gardener Applications, inserted a real dummy application through the exact same public insert path the real join-form uses and fired the exact same notification call the form fires afterward, then had the user confirm the result themselves in the running app. That surfaced a real bug: the new "All Notifications" history tab returned "Requested function was not found." Rather than patch around the symptom, ran a proper audit — diffing every Edge Function name the client code actually calls against what was live on the Supabase project — and found the real cause was systemic: after the last notification rewrite, three functions plus one migration had never actually been redeployed, so the live project was still quietly running an older version of the whole feature. While inspecting the deployed code to confirm this, `supabase functions download` overwrote the local rewritten file with that stale deployed version — caught it immediately via `git diff` before it could ship again, and reconstructed the correct file completely from the working session rather than losing the work. Fixed all four gaps and re-ran the same audit script to confirm nothing else was left out of sync.

Asked directly whether every form in the app actually worked, not just whether it looked right. Ran a dedicated audit across every submission flow in the app — booking, partnership, help box, admin login, reset PIN, update profile, manage staff, popup banner, every gardener/partnership/lead-unlock review action, the professional booking lifecycle, ratings, chat, and the new five-tab notification composer — checking each one's client payload against its real table schema or Edge Function body for missing required fields or name mismatches, not just whether the function existed. Verified the one finding personally before reporting it: the gardener's "Edit Schedule & Budget" screen was entirely fake end to end — a locally generated fake OTP compared against itself, with the edited budget and dates handed back to the previous screen only as navigation parameters and never written to the database at all, so the change silently reverted the next time the booking was reopened.

Built the real fix: a new `update-booking-schedule` Edge Function mirroring the same ownership-check-plus-real-OTP-plus-atomic-update pattern the existing job-completion function already uses, added a matching OTP purpose, and rewired the screen to send a real code to the customer's phone and only confirm once the server verifies it. While wiring it up honestly rather than patching just enough to pass a demo, found two smaller, related bugs in the same screen: the customer's phone number was never actually being passed into it at all, so a real OTP could never have been sent even after the rewrite, and the "Scope of Work" field always opened blank instead of showing the job's actual current value. Fixed both, then deployed the new function and redeployed `send-otp` itself, since it's the function that actually reads the shared OTP-message templates the new purpose was added to.

**Learning:**
Today's clearest recurring lesson was that "make it like [a reference app]" is only as good as how literally it gets checked against that reference's real, current source rather than a screenshot half-remembered or guessed at from a feature name — the notification feature needed three separate passes specifically because the earlier ones were built from a reasonable-sounding guess rather than HomeSewa's actual `AdminNotifications.tsx`, and stopped needing revision only once that real file was read end to end. The deployment-audit finding was a sharper, more consequential version of a lesson this report has circled before: local code being correct is not the same claim as production running that code, and the only way to know the two actually match is to check the live project directly — a rewrite made two turns after the last confirmed deploy is invisible until something depending on it is actually exercised, not something a "the code looks right" read would ever catch.

**Skills Gained:**
Clarifying which of two materially different outcomes a UX request actually meant before acting, rather than guessing from wording alone, since the two paths (remove a feature vs. hide it from one audience) were not interchangeable · locating and reading an actual reference codebase's real source files instead of building from a feature description, and being willing to revise a shipped feature multiple times as closer parity got requested rather than defending the first guess · designing a role-and-service-scoped push-notification targeting system against real join tables (`gardener`/`gardener_account`) rather than a flat tag, and widening a database's allowed-value vocabulary incrementally and honestly as real new audiences were confirmed rather than pre-guessing the full set · building a full admin-editable content system (banner table, write/read Edge Functions, image upload, live client wiring) scoped deliberately narrower than the reference implementation it was modeled on, based on what the app's actual data could support rather than porting unused complexity · writing and running a script-based audit that diffs every Edge Function name a client actually calls against what is live on the remote project, to catch deploy-sync gaps systematically rather than one at a time as they're hit · recognizing a tool's own side effect (a download command silently overwriting a local file) immediately via `git diff` and fully reconstructing the lost work from session context rather than treating it as an acceptable loss · auditing a whole application's submission flows for real backend correctness — payload-to-schema matching, not just "does the function exist" — and personally re-verifying an automated audit's single finding by reading the actual code before reporting it as fact · tracing a fake, UI-only feature (a client-generated OTP compared against itself, an edit that only survived as navigation parameters) back to zero real persistence, then rebuilding it against the exact same session-plus-OTP-plus-atomic-update pattern an already-correct sibling feature uses.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) A UX complaint ("two login buttons") had two structurally different valid fixes with real, different consequences, and the wording alone didn't disambiguate them. Resolved by asking directly rather than picking the more common-sounding interpretation, then respecting the answer even though it wasn't the one that first seemed most likely. (2) A "make it like HomeSewa" notification feature needed three rebuilds because each earlier pass was closer to a plausible guess than to HomeSewa's actual screen. Resolved by treating each round of screenshot feedback as a signal to re-read the real source again rather than patch the guess further, and reaching real parity only once that real file was checked line by line. (3) A `supabase functions download` command run purely to inspect deployed code silently overwrote the local, already-correct rewritten file with a stale version. Resolved by checking `git diff` immediately after every remote-touching command as a matter of habit, catching the clobber before it could be redeployed, and reconstructing the file completely from what had already been built earlier in the session. (4) The live project silently drifted from local code after a notification rewrite that was never redeployed, surfacing only as a specific missing-function error days later rather than at the point it actually went stale. Resolved by writing a small script to diff every client-called Edge Function name against the live project's deployed set, finding all three affected functions plus the missing migration in one pass instead of chasing each symptom individually, and re-running the same script after fixing them to confirm zero gaps remained. (5) A form (gardener schedule editing) looked fully functional — a real-looking OTP flow, a working "Update" button, a confirmation screen — while doing nothing at all server-side. Resolved by tracing the submit handler's actual network calls rather than trusting its UI, confirming the fake OTP and the missing database write by reading the code directly, then rebuilding it against the same real, already-trusted pattern a sibling feature (job completion) already uses correctly.

**Message to Mentor:**
Handled a UX cleanup HR raised (two login buttons in the drawer), several polish fixes (a popup ad firing every visit, forced photo cropping where it wasn't wanted, a booking-form validation bug, and a real calendar display bug), and the day's larger piece: a full admin broadcast-notification system and a matching popup-banner content system, both built directly against HomeSewa's real source and refined across several rounds until they matched what you actually showed me — including narrowing composing access to Super Admin only, per your correction, and scoping the banner feature to what this app's real data can support rather than copying every HomeSewa capability wholesale. Closed the day on verification: seeded a real dummy gardener application to prove the notification pipeline end to end, which surfaced that part of an earlier rewrite had quietly never made it to the live project — found and fixed every instance of that with a systematic audit rather than a one-off patch, including catching and reversing an accidental local-file overwrite from my own inspection command before it could ship. Then ran a full audit of every form in the app on request, found one genuinely broken feature (the gardener's schedule-edit screen was saving nothing at all), verified that finding myself before reporting it, and rebuilt it for real against the same trusted pattern the app's working job-completion flow already uses. Everything that touched the live database or Edge Functions was deployed only after your explicit go-ahead at each step, and the local build/lint/bundle all pass clean as of the last change tonight.

### Day 96 — 10 September 2026 (Thursday)

**Progress:**
HR opened today with feedback relayed secondhand rather than a direct spec: the admin section — built by a different developer — was expected to look and work "100% the same" as HomeSewa's, right down to the login screen, and the instruction as given was simply to "copy the whole system of HomeSewa and replace it in GardenSewa." Rather than start moving files on that phrasing alone, spent the first stretch of the day establishing what "HomeSewa's admin" actually referred to, since the obvious candidate turned out to be wrong: the HomeSewa Website project on this machine is only the public Next.js marketing site and has no admin panel in it at all. HomeSewa's real admin — the one HR meant — turned out to live in a separate React Native/Expo codebase entirely, structured almost identically to this project (its own `app/`, `api/`, `context/`, `supabase/`), with a proper drawer-based admin shell: a custom drawer menu, a shared `Header4Admin` top bar, a light/dark theme-token system, and around a dozen admin screens covering everything from gardener-equivalent verification to staff management to a popup-banner composer.

Compared that real reference against GardenSewa's current admin screen by screen before deciding anything, and found the two were much closer in substance than the "copy the whole system" framing suggested: GardenSewa's eight admin screens already cover nearly every one of HomeSewa's — lead-unlock requests and partnership applications even share the exact same names — wired to their own already-correct Supabase edge functions and tables. What GardenSewa's admin section was actually missing was never the underlying features; it was a design system. Every screen hardcoded its own colors, badges, and buttons independently with no shared components, there was no dark mode, and — the most functionally real gap — there was no navigation shell connecting the admin screens at all: no drawer, no menu, nothing but direct deep links and a plain back arrow, so an admin who logged in had no in-app way to discover or reach the other six screens.

Given how much this touches (a colleague's existing, working screens, and the app's whole navigation shape) confirmed scope with two direct questions before writing any code: whether GardenSewa's screens that HomeSewa doesn't have (Popup Banner, Send Notification, Submit Booking for Customer, Manage Staff) should be dropped for an exact clone or kept and rebuilt in HomeSewa's style, and whether "copy the whole system" meant the backend too or only the frontend. Got clear answers on both — keep every existing GardenSewa screen, rebuilt visually; frontend and design only, leave GardenSewa's own edge functions and tables untouched — and used those to scope the actual plan: build a real theme system and a small shared admin component library, add the missing drawer, then re-skin all eight existing admin screens on top of it without touching a single data call or role check.

Before writing any implementation, ran two research passes in parallel against both real codebases rather than working from memory of "what an admin panel usually looks like" — one over HomeSewa's actual admin screens, theme tokens, and drawer component, one over GardenSewa's current screens, its own hardcoded color usage, and exactly which Supabase tables and edge functions each screen already depends on — then read every one of those files directly myself before touching anything, so the rebuild could carry over each screen's real logic byte-for-byte rather than reconstructing it from a description. Built the new pieces in order: a `theme/colors.js` token set structured exactly like HomeSewa's (background/surface/text/brand/danger/success/warning, plus a light and dark variant of each), anchored to GardenSewa's own existing brand teal rather than swapping in HomeSewa's hex values, since the two were already close enough that keeping GardenSewa's own color preserved its identity without sacrificing the shared system; a `ThemeContext` provider ported from HomeSewa's, seeding from the device's own light/dark setting on first launch and persisting an explicit user choice after that; and a `components/admin/` folder holding a `Header4Admin` top bar, a brand-new `AdminSideBar` drawer (the piece that didn't exist at all before), and small shared `AdminCard`/`StatusBadge`/`AdminButton` primitives to stop every screen re-declaring the same badge and button styles from scratch. The new drawer turned out to also be the natural place to finally wire up a working Logout button — the context already had a `adminLogoutLocal()` method from earlier work, but nothing in the app actually called it from anywhere a user could tap.

With the shared pieces in place, rewrote all eight admin screens plus the admin login screen to sit on top of them — swapping each screen's ad hoc header, hardcoded hex colors, and inline badge/button markup for the shared components and theme tokens, while leaving every `useState`, edge-function call, and role gate exactly as it was. Ran the project's linter across the whole change before testing anything live and confirmed zero new errors or warnings on any file touched today. Then, rather than trusting that a lint-clean, logic-preserving rewrite was actually correct, started the app for real (`expo start --web`) and drove it with a small Playwright script to click through the login screen, the new drawer, and a couple of the redesigned screens, screenshotting each step. That live pass caught two real bugs neither the lint pass nor a code read would have: the root navigator was rendering its own leftover "GardenSewa" header directly on top of every admin screen's brand-new one, because the app's root stack had never explicitly turned off its header for the admin and general route groups the way each of those groups' own inner layouts already had — fixed with one `headerShown: false` option per group at the root. Separately, the new drawer's dark backdrop and menu panel were painting underneath each screen's later content instead of on top of it, a stacking-order side effect of the drawer being mounted as a child of the header component that renders first on every screen — diagnosed by faking a logged-in admin session directly in the browser's local storage and holding the screen in a stable loading state (by intercepting its backend calls) long enough to actually screenshot the problem, then fixed with an explicit `zIndex`. Re-ran the same screenshots after each fix to confirm a single clean header, a correctly role-gated drawer (a super-admin session showed a "Super Admin" section with Send Notification and Manage Staff that a lower role wouldn't), a working dark-mode toggle that actually swapped every color live, and the notification composer rendering correctly.

Deliberately left out of today's work — flagged rather than quietly built or quietly skipped — the handful of HomeSewa admin screens that have no GardenSewa equivalent at all: a general all-bookings browser, a professional/customer directory beyond the existing staff list, and self-service change-PIN/update-profile screens. Building those would be new functionality on top of a different data model, not a reskin of something that already exists, so raised it explicitly as a scope decision rather than folding it into "copy the whole system" silently, and the user confirmed picking that up tomorrow. Nothing from today has been committed yet, at the user's direction, pending a final look at the redesign before it goes in.

**Learning:**
The most consequential decision today was made before any code was touched at all: taking "copy the whole system of HomeSewa" literally would have meant deleting a colleague's working screens and rebuilding functionality that already existed correctly, when the actual gap — read from comparing the two real codebases side by side rather than from the instruction's wording — was a missing design system and a missing navigation shell, not missing features. A secondhand instruction like that is a description of the *outcome* someone wants, not a literal execution plan, and the two can diverge sharply once you actually look at what exists on both sides. The second lesson echoed something this report has already learned once, about a different feature, from a different direction: a rewrite that reads correctly and lints clean is still only a claim about correctness until it's actually run — both of today's real bugs (the doubled header, the drawer painting underneath its own screen) were invisible in the code and only became obvious once the app was driven live and screenshotted, and the second one specifically needed a deliberate technique (faking a session, holding a screen in a stable loading state) just to get a stable enough frame to see the problem at all, rather than a real bug that would have shown up naturally in a five-second glance.

**Skills Gained:**
Distinguishing a stakeholder's stated instruction from its actual underlying intent by comparing both real systems directly before acting, rather than executing the literal wording of a secondhand request · locating a genuine reference implementation when the obvious first guess (a same-named but unrelated project) turns out to be the wrong codebase entirely · scoping a large, ambiguous "make it match" request into a concrete, boundaried plan by asking two direct clarifying questions upfront instead of guessing at the answers and discovering the mismatch mid-build · designing a theme-token system structurally identical to a reference implementation while deliberately keeping the host app's own brand color, to gain shared design-system benefits without erasing product identity · building a navigation shell (a role-gated drawer menu) that never existed in the app before, including surfacing an already-written but never-wired-up logout method · re-skinning eight existing screens on top of a new shared component library while proving equivalence of every data call and role check, rather than reconstructing business logic from scratch and risking silent behavior drift · using a live, driven browser session (with a faked auth session and intercepted network calls) to hold a real app screen in a specific, otherwise-transient state long enough to actually observe and screenshot a rendering bug · diagnosing a CSS/React Native stacking-order bug back to component mount order rather than to the more obvious suspects (colors, layout math) · recognizing when a request implies net-new functionality against an unfamiliar data model rather than a visual reskin, and surfacing that boundary explicitly instead of silently either overbuilding or underbuilding against it.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) The instruction to "copy the whole system of HomeSewa" pointed, on its face, at the wrong codebase — a public marketing site with no admin panel — and taken completely literally would have meant deleting working, already-correct GardenSewa screens. Resolved by locating the real reference implementation first, then comparing it against GardenSewa's actual admin screen by screen before deciding what "matching it" should even mean, rather than executing the instruction's literal wording. (2) Whether to treat GardenSewa's HomeSewa-less screens as extra baggage to delete or as existing product value to preserve, and whether "the whole system" meant the backend as well as the UI, were both genuinely ambiguous and consequential either way. Resolved by asking directly before writing any code rather than picking the more literal-sounding interpretation and finding out later it wasn't what was meant. (3) A lint-clean, logic-preserving rewrite of eight screens still shipped two real rendering bugs — a doubled header and a drawer painting underneath its own screen — that no amount of reading the code would have surfaced. Resolved by actually running the app and driving it with a scripted browser session rather than trusting the review, and for the harder of the two bugs, by faking a session and hanging the network calls specifically to get a screen to hold still long enough to see the problem. (4) Part of HomeSewa's admin has no GardenSewa equivalent at all — different screens against a different data model, not a matter of styling. Resolved by explicitly separating that out as new functionality for a later, separately-scoped day rather than either quietly building it unasked or quietly dropping it from the conversation.

**Message to Mentor:**
HR's ask today was framed as "make the admin section 100% the same as HomeSewa," but the first real finding was that the obvious HomeSewa project on this machine isn't the one with an admin panel at all — found the actual reference app, and once I compared it screen-by-screen against what's already here, it turned out GardenSewa's admin already does nearly everything HomeSewa's does, functionally; what it was missing was a real design system and a way to navigate between its own screens (there was no admin menu at all before today — just deep links). Confirmed scope with you before touching anything: keep every existing GardenSewa admin screen rather than deleting the ones HomeSewa doesn't have, and rebuild the UI only, leaving the working backend untouched. Built a shared theme (light and dark, on GardenSewa's own brand color) and a small component library, added a real drawer menu that didn't exist before — including finally wiring up a logout button — and re-skinned all eight admin screens and the login screen on top of it without changing any of their underlying logic. Didn't stop at a clean lint pass: actually ran the app and drove it with a scripted browser session, which caught two real bugs a code read alone wouldn't have (a duplicate header, and the new drawer rendering underneath its own screen instead of above it) — both fixed and reverified with fresh screenshots afterward. One deliberate scope line I drew rather than crossed silently: a few HomeSewa admin screens (a general bookings browser, a fuller user directory, self-service profile/PIN screens) have no GardenSewa equivalent at all and would be new functionality, not a reskin — flagged that explicitly rather than guessing, and picking it up tomorrow per your go-ahead. Nothing from today is committed yet, holding for your review of the redesign first.

---

### Day 97 — 11 September 2026 (Friday)

**Progress:**
Picked up the three items deferred from yesterday — a general bookings browser, a professional/customer directory, and self-service profile/PIN screens — and before building anything, checked what already existed rather than assuming the gap was as wide as it looked. Self-service profile and PIN turned out to already be fully built and working (from an earlier day's fix), just never linked from the new admin drawer — a one-line gap, not new work. The bookings browser needed zero new backend at all: the existing `list-open-bookings` function already returns every booking, fully unmasked, to any back-office session — it just had no screen calling it yet. The professional/customer directory was the one genuinely new piece, and it came with a real decision to make first: whether to add customer blocking given the `customer` table had no status column at all. Confirmed with the user to add it, then found that bookings insert directly from the client with no session or OTP check in the actual write path — OTP there is only a soft confirmation after the row already exists — so blocking could only be enforced with a database trigger, not a client-side or API-layer check. Built that migration, four new Edge Functions (list/toggle for both gardeners and customers), and the two new screens, then shipped a Change-PIN link fix and deployed everything only after explicit confirmation.

The day's real turn came when the user started sending real screenshots from HomeSewa's live app rather than descriptions. The first ones showed the header rendering with phone status-bar icons overlapping the logo and menu — a real-device safe-area bug: the custom header and two custom hero screens used fixed pixel padding instead of the device's actual safe-area inset, invisible in my own web-based testing since a browser has no status bar to collide with. Fixed all three with `useSafeAreaInsets`. Then a report that "there is no booking history" turned out not to be a code bug at all — traced it to Metro's dev server needing a full restart (not just a reload) to pick up newly added route files, since expo-router builds its route table at server start.

Then came the real gap: side-by-side screenshots of HomeSewa's actual Booking History, Help Box, Popup Banner, and User Management screens showed the redesign wasn't matching as closely as the earlier pass had assumed. Rather than guess again, went through each screenshot literally and rebuilt to match: Booking History became a flat-row list with a status dropdown, search toggle, a Super Admin badge, and a real functional calendar view instead of the card layout from before. Help Box became an actual table with duration/status filters and pagination, and its detail view — which I'd built as a bottom-sheet modal — turned out to need to be a full page of its own, matching HomeSewa's real `HelpBoxDetail`. Popup Banner grew every field the reference actually has: an internal-only banner name, a preset button-text picker with a custom "Other" option, a "Browse App Pages" link picker covering every static and dynamic route, a close-button countdown that now actually drives the customer-facing popup (previously hardcoded to 10 seconds for every banner), and real scheduling and audience-targeting enforcement — checked against what data GardenSewa actually has, so city targeting is captured but honestly not enforced (no per-customer city data exists), while user-type targeting is, since the app already knows its own viewer type locally. User Management was rebuilt around real ID pills computed by stable signup order (not list position, which HomeSewa's own inconsistent numbering proved isn't how it works), detail sheets with the actual disable/block action inside them instead of an inline switch, inline Cities/Disable buttons on the Admins tab, and a phone-search field that prefills a match's name as a safe convenience rather than attempting to silently merge two account records together. Along the way, tracing why the customer-facing home screen's own hamburger menu showed a completely different, stale admin menu led to the real root cause: a second, independent, hand-rolled copy of the admin menu had existed inside the guest-facing drawer component the whole time, never touched by any of this redesign work — consolidated to one source of truth instead of patching the duplicate. Closed out the visual matching with one more direct correction: the drawer was scrolling, and HomeSewa's real one doesn't — fixed by reusing the exact non-scrolling layout the app's own customer-facing drawer already used elsewhere.

Ended the day on two rounds of "is this actually working" rather than taking the visual match as done. The first was a full-app health check, which turned up something with nothing to do with the code at all: 113 stray Node and Metro processes had piled up from a day's worth of repeated dev-server restarts, eventually exhausting memory and crashing the dev server outright. Confirmed every one of them actually belonged to this project before cleaning them up, rather than blanket-killing every Node process on the machine. With a clean environment, re-verified every migration and Edge Function was actually deployed and in sync with local code, and drove every admin screen live with no console errors. The second round was a real form audit: went through every admin form's submit handler and checked its payload field-by-field against what its Edge Function actually expects, then went further than a code read for the two most complex ones — actually filling in and submitting the real Add Admin form and the nested Reject Application flow in a driven browser, capturing the real outgoing network request rather than trusting that matching field names in two files meant the wiring was correct.

**Learning:**
The clearest lesson today was that a screenshot of the real reference is worth more than any amount of research notes about it — the second redesign pass was necessary specifically because the first one, built from a thorough but text-based investigation, got the structure and functionality right but missed real details (a table instead of cards, a full detail page instead of a modal, fields that don't exist in a description but are obviously there in a picture) that only became visible once actual screenshots arrived. The second lesson was about trusting the sequence of confirmations, not just the final one: it would have been easy to treat "everything renders without a crash" as proof of correctness, but the deeper form audit — driving a real submission and reading the real request body — caught the category of bug that a passing lint and a clean render both miss entirely, since a wrong or missing field name in a payload is invisible until the exact request is inspected. The environment issue was its own quieter lesson: a long day of "start the server, check something, kill it, repeat" silently accumulated real resource cost that had nothing to do with any single change, and only showed up as a seemingly unrelated crash much later — worth periodically checking the health of the tools themselves, not just the code running in them.

**Skills Gained:**
Checking what already exists before building a requested feature, and finding that two of three "missing" pieces were either already built or needed no new backend at all · making a real, disclosed schema decision (adding customer blocking) after tracing the actual write path and discovering the obvious enforcement point (a session check) doesn't exist for that flow, and reaching for a database trigger instead as the only point that's actually always hit · diagnosing a real-device-only bug (status bar icons overlapping custom headers) that a web-based testing environment structurally cannot reproduce, and fixing it with safe-area insets rather than guessing at padding values · distinguishing a real code bug from a dev-tooling gap (a new route needing a full Metro restart, not just a reload) before touching any source · treating a set of real reference screenshots as a literal, field-by-field specification rather than a general impression, and being willing to substantially rebuild screens a second time once better evidence arrived rather than defending the first pass · designing backend enforcement (scheduling, audience targeting) scoped honestly to the data that actually exists, rather than building UI for capabilities the backend can't really check · finding and consolidating a second, independent, silently-diverged copy of the same UI (a duplicate admin menu inside the guest drawer) instead of only fixing the copy that was reported broken · diagnosing a resource-exhaustion crash back to accumulated stray processes from the day's own workflow, verifying exactly which processes were safe to clean up before doing anything broad or irreversible · auditing a whole set of forms by comparing client payload shape against server-side destructuring field-by-field, then proving the riskiest ones live by driving a real submission and inspecting the actual network request rather than trusting that the code read was enough.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) A real-device screenshot showed the phone's own status bar overlapping every admin header — invisible in all of the day's own web-based testing, which has no status bar to collide with. Resolved by recognizing the platform gap in the testing method itself and fixing the actual custom headers with real safe-area insets rather than trusting a clean web screenshot as sufficient proof. (2) A "missing feature" report (no Booking History showing) turned out to be a dev-tooling gap, not a code bug — new route files need a full Metro restart, not a reload, to register. Resolved by reasoning through what changed and when, rather than immediately assuming the newly-written code was wrong. (3) Real screenshots revealed the redesign's first pass, though thoroughly researched, didn't match closely enough in several concrete ways — a card layout instead of a table, a modal instead of a real page, fields simply absent. Resolved by treating each screenshot as a literal spec and rebuilding rather than defending or lightly patching the first attempt. (4) A duplicate, independently-drifted admin menu was found living inside the customer-facing guest drawer, invisible to any test that only opened the drawer from inside an already-authenticated admin screen. Resolved by tracing exactly which component actually renders when the hamburger is tapped from each different context, then consolidating to one source of truth instead of patching the symptom in the wrong file. (5) The dev server crashed with an out-of-memory error that had nothing to do with any code change made that day. Resolved by inspecting exactly which processes were consuming resources, confirming every one belonged to this project's own repeated restarts before cleaning any of them up, rather than assuming and force-killing broadly. (6) A lint-clean, logically-matched set of form payloads could still hide a real bug invisible to a code read alone. Resolved by going one step further than static comparison for the highest-risk forms — actually driving a real submission through the UI and inspecting the real captured network request.

**Message to Mentor:**
Finished yesterday's deferred work first — found two of the three missing pieces either already existed or needed no new backend at all, and built the one real gap (a professional/customer directory with customer blocking) after tracing that bookings have no session check in their actual write path, so blocking needed a database-level trigger rather than an API check. The bigger part of today came from you sending real screenshots of HomeSewa's live app rather than descriptions — that surfaced a real device bug (the status bar overlapping every admin header, invisible in my own testing) and, more significantly, showed the first redesign pass hadn't matched as closely as the earlier research suggested: rebuilt Booking History, Help Box, Popup Banner, and User Management against the literal screenshots, including features I'd have otherwise never known were missing (a banner name field, a button-text preset picker, real scheduling, a dedicated help-ticket page instead of a popup). While chasing one of your bug reports, found and fixed a second, independent, duplicate admin menu that had been silently living inside the app's customer-facing drawer the whole time. Closed the day by not just declaring things done: ran a full health check that caught an unrelated environment problem (113 stray processes from the day's own restarts), verified every backend piece was actually deployed and in sync, then audited every single admin form's data against its backend contract and proved the two riskiest ones by actually submitting them live and reading the real network request. Everything is deployed, lint-clean, and verified end to end as of tonight.

### Day 98 — 13 September 2026 (Sunday)

**Progress:**
Today's work was on GardenSewa rather than NEPAL Motor. Started by closing out a bug from the previous audit pass: the lead-unlock requests screen wasn't refreshing its list after a failed approve/reject, so when the server's own race guard correctly rejected a stale second action with "this request was already reviewed," the admin was left staring at a row with live buttons still inviting the same failed tap — fixed by refetching on the failure branch as well as the success one, not just the happy path.

The rest of the day was HR-driven infrastructure work: HR asked to change the app's package identifier from `com.pratigyaluitel.gardensewa` to `com.gardensewa`. Before touching anything, mapped out the full blast radius rather than just editing one field — confirmed the app hasn't shipped to either store yet (so no existing users or store listings to strand), then identified every place the old identifier actually lived: `app.json`'s `ios.bundleIdentifier` and `android.package`, the Firebase-issued `google-services.json`, and the EAS-managed signing credentials. Updated `app.json`, then walked HR step by step through the Firebase Console to register `com.gardensewa` as a new Android app inside the same Firebase project and download a fresh `google-services.json` (which correctly came back containing both the new and old package entries, since Firebase bundles every registered app in a project into one file — not a bug, just how the file works). Ran `npx expo prebuild --clean` to regenerate the native `android/` directory against the new config and verified by hand that `build.gradle`'s `applicationId`/`namespace` and the regenerated `google-services.json` both actually landed on `com.gardensewa`, rather than assuming the prebuild step did the right thing.

That surfaced two more real gaps neither of us knew about until we actually tried to build. First, HR started an EAS credentials flow and answered "no" to generating a new Android keystore, which only makes sense if a real prior release keystore exists — it doesn't, since this is a first-ever release under a brand-new package identity. Checked the project for any existing keystore rather than guessing, found only the auto-generated local debug keystore (not suitable for a real release), and redirected HR to answer "yes" and let EAS generate and manage a real one. Second, checking OneSignal's dashboard to make sure Android push still worked after the rename revealed it had never actually been fully configured in the first place — the Google Android (FCM) setup showed 0 of 3 steps complete, unrelated to today's rename. Walked HR through generating a real Firebase Admin SDK service-account private key (Project Settings → Service Accounts → Generate new private key — a different, more sensitive credential than the client-side `google-services.json`, flagged explicitly as something that must never be committed), uploading it into OneSignal, and completing the setup wizard, confirming along the way that the OneSignal App ID already hardcoded in the app's `.env` matched the dashboard exactly.

Closed the day with a full live regression pass against the real production Supabase backend, using HR's own real phone number for every OTP-dependent flow rather than a placeholder — actual SMS OTP send and verification, a real Partnership application submission, a real Gardener application submission, and a real OTP-gated Help Box request, each traced through to a genuine success response rather than trusted from a code read. Logged in with real admin credentials to get an actual session token and confirmed every session-token-gated read endpoint (popup banners, notifications, lead-unlock requests) works correctly for that role — while deliberately drawing a hard line around anything with an irreversible real-world effect: no real push broadcast sent, no real pending request approved or rejected, no PIN changed on the live account. Gave HR a precise cleanup checklist for the two disposable test rows created during this pass so they don't linger in the live admin queues. Finished with a second full regression audit of every fix made across the day (and the session before it) to confirm none of them had quietly broken something else — only one small issue turned up (the same lead-unlock refresh bug fixed at the top of today), everything else held.

**Learning:**
The most important habit today was refusing to treat "change the package name" as a one-field edit. A package/bundle identifier is load-bearing across at least three independent systems that don't automatically stay in sync with each other or with `app.json` — the native build config, the push-notification provider's Firebase linkage, and the platform's own signing credentials — and two of today's three real problems (the keystore confusion, the never-finished OneSignal setup) were latent issues that had nothing to do with the rename itself; they were just finally forced into view by actually trying to complete the full chain instead of stopping at the config file. The second lesson was about live testing discipline specifically: testing against a real production backend with a real phone number is genuinely more convincing than any code read, but it also means the boundary between "verify it works" and "cause a real, irreversible side effect on a real user or account" has to be drawn and held deliberately, not discovered by accident.

**Skills Gained:**
Mapping the full dependency surface of a mobile app's package/bundle identifier across build config, a third-party push provider's backend linkage, and platform signing credentials, rather than treating it as a single string to find-and-replace · recognizing when a tooling prompt (an EAS keystore question) is being answered based on a false assumption (that a prior release keystore exists) and correcting course before it causes a broken or unverifiable build · diagnosing that a "does push still work after this change" question actually uncovered an unrelated, previously-incomplete third-party integration, rather than assuming the rename was the only variable in play · distinguishing a public, bundle-safe credential (`google-services.json`) from a sensitive, server-only one (a Firebase service-account private key) and handling each with the appropriate level of care · designing and executing a live end-to-end test plan against a real production backend with real credentials, scoped deliberately to stop short of any action with a genuine, irreversible business or user-facing consequence · running a full regression pass over an entire day's changes as a discipline in itself, not just testing the newest change in isolation.

**Challenge & Solution:**
(1) HR's package-rename request looked like a single-file change but actually touched native build config, the Firebase-issued push config, and EAS's own signing credentials. Resolved by explicitly mapping out every dependent system before making any edit, then walking through each one in order rather than fixing `app.json` and assuming the rest would follow. (2) An EAS credentials prompt was about to be answered in a way that assumed a release keystore existed for an app that has never shipped. Resolved by checking the actual project for any real keystore first, finding none, and redirecting to the correct answer before a broken or unverifiable signing setup could be created. (3) A routine "double check push still works" step uncovered that Android push had never been fully configured at all, unrelated to the day's actual task. Resolved by treating the dashboard's own "0 of 3 steps" state as the ground truth rather than assuming a green checkmark existed somewhere, and completing the real setup rather than working around it. (4) Verifying OTP, SMS, form submissions, and admin session auth thoroughly meant testing against the real live backend, which carries real risk of unintended side effects (real pushes, real approvals, a changed PIN on a real account). Resolved by scoping the test plan in advance to real-but-reversible actions only (OTP round-trips, clearly-labeled disposable test rows) and explicitly refusing to exercise anything with a genuine irreversible consequence, then handing back a precise cleanup list for what was created.

**Message to Mentor:**
Today's work was on GardenSewa, not NEPAL Motor. Closed out a small bug from the last audit (a stale admin list after a race-guarded action failed), then spent most of the day on an HR-requested package identifier rename (`com.pratigyaluitel.gardensewa` → `com.gardensewa`). Treated it as more than a config edit — mapped out everywhere that identifier actually lives (build config, Firebase's push config, EAS signing credentials) and walked HR through each one, which surfaced two real problems neither of us expected: HR was about to answer an EAS prompt in a way that assumed a release keystore already existed (it doesn't, since this app hasn't shipped yet — corrected before it caused problems), and a routine check of the push-notification dashboard revealed Android push had never actually been fully configured in the first place, unrelated to today's rename — walked through generating a real Firebase service-account key and finishing that setup properly. Closed the day with a full live test of the real production backend using HR's own phone number — real SMS/OTP, a real partnership application, a real gardener application, and a real Help Box request all confirmed working, plus admin-session-gated endpoints — while deliberately not touching anything with a real, irreversible effect (no live push broadcast, no approving a real request, no changing a real PIN), and handed back a clear list of the disposable test data to remove. Re-audited the whole day's changes afterward and confirmed everything holds except one small fix already made at the top of the session.

## Final Note

Dear Mentor,

This internship on the NEPAL Motor React Native project has been one of the most formative experiences of my early career as a developer. Over the course of these 23 days, I progressed from setting up a development environment and reading an unfamiliar codebase, to independently delivering production-ready features — OTP phone verification, the Admin Login screen, the new NEPAL Motor logo across all screens, drawer and footer UI refinements, a complete EAS Build and APK release pipeline, a Phase 1 push notification segmentation system, managing the Play Store release pipeline including upload key reset procedures, systematic post-release bug fixing, and expanding the Exchange to EV form with product-specific EV car images.

Each task challenged me in a different way: TypeScript migration taught me the value of type safety and documentation; OTP implementation taught me API integration and `useEffect` cleanup patterns; the Admin Login screen taught me authentication UX design; the EAS Build setup taught me how to diagnose and resolve cloud build failures by reading compressed binary logs; and the logo replacement taught me that even visually simple tasks can involve non-obvious technical challenges.

I am deeply grateful for the guidance, trust, and the opportunity to contribute to a live production application that serves real users in Nepal. I look forward to continuing to grow as a developer in the weeks ahead.

**Thank you.**

*Samek Shahi — React Native Developer Intern*
